## Abstract

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension x₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: dx₄/dt = ic, with x₄ = ict, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

For the first time in the history of quantum mechanics [163], it is shown that the Hamiltonian operator formulation and the Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics emerge from the same foundational physical principle — the McGucken Principle, which states that the fourth dimension is expanding in a spherically symmetric manner relative to the three spatial dimensions, dx₄/dt = ic. The two principal formulations of quantum mechanics — the Hamiltonian operator formulation resting on the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ, and the Lagrangian path-integral formulation resting on the Feynman kernel K = ∫𝒟x exp(iS/ℏ) — have coexisted since the founding of the theory (the operator formulation from Heisenberg 1925, the Lagrangian analogue from Dirac 1933, the path integral from Feynman 1948) as two equivalent mathematical frameworks for the same physics. Their equivalence is established by Feynman’s 1948 derivation of the Schrödinger equation from the path integral and by the Stone–von Neumann theorem’s uniqueness of the Schrödinger representation. But their *common origin* — the question of whether both formulations descend from a single deeper physical principle — has remained open through the subsequent nine decades of foundational work, including Nelson’s stochastic mechanics, geometric quantization, Hestenes’s spacetime algebra, Adler’s trace dynamics, Bohmian mechanics, Lindgren-Liukkonen stochastic optimal control, ‘t Hooft’s cellular automata, and the programs of Witten, Arkani-Hamed, Woit, Ashtekar, and Schuller. Each of these programs derives, reinterprets, or reformulates one of the two quantum formulations; none derives both from a single geometric spacetime principle.

The central result of this paper is that the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic — the physical statement that a fourth geometric axis advances at the velocity of light, spherically symmetrically about every spacetime point — forces both the Hamiltonian operator formulation and the Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics as independent theorems, through derivationally disjoint intermediate structures, with the imaginary unit i and the action quantum ℏ both derived from the Principle along each route rather than postulated as inputs or left as empirical constants. The Hamiltonian route proceeds in five propositions (§II): x₄ = ict forces the Minkowski metric (Proposition H.1); Stone’s theorem on translation invariance forces the momentum operator as generator of spatial translations (Proposition H.2); the configuration representation forces p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q (Proposition H.3); direct computation yields [q̂, p̂] = iℏ𝟙 (Proposition H.4); and the Stone-von Neumann uniqueness theorem closes the representation (Proposition H.5). The Lagrangian route proceeds in six propositions (§III): x₄’s spherical expansion from every spacetime point *is* Huygens’ principle (Proposition L.1); iterated Huygens expansions compose into the set of all paths (Proposition L.2); each path carries an accumulated x₄-phase which in the non-relativistic limit is exp(iS/ℏ) (Proposition L.3); the continuum limit gives the full Feynman path integral (Proposition L.4); Gaussian integration of the short-time propagator recovers the Schrödinger equation iℏ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ (Proposition L.5); and the second moment of the kernel gives [q̂, p̂] = iℏ (Proposition L.6). No proposition of either route is used in the other. The routes share the Principle, the Minkowski interval of Proposition H.1, the arena of complex amplitudes, and the final identity (§IV.2). They are two proofs of the same theorem by different methods.

§V develops the deeper reason *why* dx₄/dt = ic has this property while no other principle does. The answer lies in the principle’s *dual-channel content*: the geometric statement dx₄/dt = ic simultaneously specifies two logically distinct pieces of information — an algebraic symmetry (the *invariance* of x₄’s advance under time translation, space translation, rotation, and Lorentz boost) and a geometric propagation (the *spherical symmetry* of x₄’s expansion from every spacetime point). The algebraic symmetry drives the Hamiltonian route through Stone’s theorem and Noether’s theorem; the geometric propagation drives the Lagrangian route through Huygens’ principle and path summation. No prior proposed foundation has had both channels. Classical symplectic geometry has only the algebraic-symmetry channel; classical Lagrangian variational mechanics has only the propagation-variational channel; stochastic dynamics has a diffusion-propagation channel but no symmetry content; Hestenes’s spacetime algebra has static geometry but no dynamical principle. dx₄/dt = ic is the first physical principle whose statement contains both channels simultaneously, because “advancing at rate ic from every point” is the algebraic symmetry (a uniform rate, invariant under isometries) and “spherically symmetrically about each point” is the geometric propagation (a wavefront structure inheriting Huygens’ secondary-wavelet property). The dual-channel content is the feature of the principle that makes both quantum formulations theorems of one fact.

§VI surveys fifteen prior frameworks that have sought foundations for quantum mechanics — Feynman’s 1948 path integral, Dirac’s 1933 transformation theory, Nelson’s 1966 stochastic mechanics, Lindgren-Liukkonen 2019 stochastic optimal control, geometric quantization (Kostant 1970, Souriau 1970), Hestenes’s geometric algebra (1966–), Adler’s trace dynamics (1994–), Bohmian mechanics (1952–), Weinberg’s Lagrangian QFT (1995), ‘t Hooft’s cellular automata (2014), Arnold’s symplectic mechanics (1978), Ashtekar and loop quantum gravity (1986–), Witten’s twistor string (2003), Schuller’s constructive gravity (2020), and Woit’s Euclidean twistor unification (2021). For each framework, the section examines the framework’s own central claim, the derivation structure it employs, the specific point at which it does or does not reach a two-route unification, and the reason. The conclusion in every case is the same: the framework derives or reinterprets one of the two quantum formulations — often with great mathematical sophistication and physical insight — but does not reach both formulations as independent theorems of a single geometric spacetime principle. The dual-channel property identified in §V is shown to be absent from each of the fifteen prior foundations.

§VII develops the principle of *convergent overdetermination* — the observation that when a single claim is derivable through multiple independent chains from a foundational principle, the claim is confirmed not once but as many times as there are independent routes, and each route illuminates a different aspect of the foundation. The two-route derivation of [q̂, p̂] = iℏ from dx₄/dt = ic is the first convergent overdetermination in the history of foundational-physics derivations of quantum mechanics, and its existence is the principal evidence for the McGucken Principle’s status as a genuine physical foundation rather than a reframing of existing physics.

§VIII connects the two-route result to the main result of the companion paper “The Unique McGucken Lagrangian” [108], in which the full four-sector Lagrangian ℒ_McG of physics — free-particle kinetic, Dirac matter, Yang-Mills gauge, Einstein-Hilbert gravitational — is established as forced by dx₄/dt = ic via a single four-fold uniqueness theorem (Theorem VI.1). The two-route unification developed here deepens §VIII.6 of the Lagrangian paper (the first-of-its-kind claim on the canonical commutation relation) by showing that the canonical commutation relation is derivable in *two* mathematically independent ways, through disjoint intermediate structures, with the same i and the same ℏ reached by both chains.

The accomplishment is the same identity derived from the same principle through two routes that share no machinery, *together with* the derivation of the wave/particle duality as the dual-channel reading of x₄’s advance at the ontological level (§V.6), the formal geometric proof of the Schrödinger-Heisenberg equivalence as two readings of the same physical x₄-advance at the dynamical level (§V.7, Theorem V.7.3), and the dual-channel reading of local microcausality and nonlocal Bell correlations at the causal/correlational level (§V.8), with the latter establishing that the coexistence of locality and nonlocality in quantum mechanics — the feature Einstein in 1935 and Bell in 1964 identified as the most distinctive and challenging feature of the theory — is a fourth appearance of the same dual-channel structure, with the dx₄/dt = ic Algebraic Channel forcing the local operator algebra through the Minkowski metric and light-cone causal structure and the dx₄/dt = ic Geometric Channel forcing the nonlocal Bell correlations through the shared McGucken Sphere identity of the *McGucken Equivalence* [124; 123]. These results combine to establish the McGucken Quantum Formalism — the body of physics developed from the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic in the derivations of [111], [118], [114], [113], [149], [150], [109], [110], [152], [125], and the companion Lagrangian paper [108] — as the most complete interpretation of quantum mechanics available in the hundred-year literature since Schrödinger 1926. The completeness claim is developed in §IX: The McGucken Quantum Formalism derives every standard quantum-mechanical structure as a theorem of a single geometric principle, including the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian formulations (Propositions H.1–H.5, L.1–L.6), the Heisenberg and Schrödinger pictures (§V.7 and Theorem V.7.3), the wave and particle aspects (§V.6), the local operator algebra and nonlocal Bell correlations (§V.8, with the Two McGucken Laws of Nonlocality in §V.8.4 and the six senses of geometric nonlocality in §V.8.3), the canonical commutation relation (Propositions H.4 and L.6, through two independent routes), the Schrödinger equation (Proposition L.5), the Feynman path integral (Proposition L.4), the Heisenberg uncertainty principle [110], the Born rule [149], the Dirac equation [150], the de Broglie relation [109], quantum nonlocality [125; 123], the Wick rotation [143], and the fundamental constants c and ℏ [117] — fifteen or more distinct quantum-mechanical structures unified as theorems of the single principle dx₄/dt = ic. No prior interpretation of quantum mechanics — Copenhagen, many-worlds, Bohmian, QBism, decoherence, relational, stochastic, trace-dynamics, geometric quantization, cellular-automaton, or any of the fifteen frameworks surveyed in §VI — derives as many structures from as simple a foundation, and none derives the four dualities (Hamiltonian/Lagrangian, Heisenberg/Schrödinger, wave/particle, locality/nonlocality) as simultaneous consequences of a single geometric fact. This is the signature of the McGucken Quantum Formalism’s status as the most complete interpretation currently available. The McGucken Principle does what the hundred-year history of quantum-mechanical interpretation has been seeking: it derives quantum mechanics from a single simple physical principle rather than postulating it, interpreting it, or reformulating it.

**Keywords: **McGucken Principle; dx₄/dt = ic; canonical commutation relation; path integral; Stone-von Neumann theorem; Huygens’ principle; two-route derivation; convergent overdetermination; geometric foundations of quantum mechanics; comparative foundations.

The paper derives within its own text the Hilbert space, as the completion of the McGucken Sphere wavefronts under the overlap that x₄’s expansion conserves (Theorem H.0); the Born density |ψ|², as the only function of the modulus of the wavefront whose total that expansion conserves (Corollary H.0.3, Theorem S.6); and spin-½, as the requirement that first-order propagation of matter stay inside the null cone swept out by the Sphere (Theorems S.1 to S.4). §§II and III prove the thesis in full for the simplest case, one spinless particle in a potential. §IIIA states case by case what is proved beyond it: relativistic propagation, one spin-½ particle, and N particles with interaction.

## Summary: The Two Routes at a Glance

The two-route derivation developed in §§II–III of this paper may be summarized at a glance in the table below. The table reads down each column as a derivational chain: the Hamiltonian route (left) proceeds through five propositions from dx₄/dt = ic to [q̂, p̂] = iℏ via the Minkowski metric, Stone’s theorem, the configuration representation, direct commutator computation, and Stone-von Neumann closure; the Lagrangian route (right) proceeds through six propositions from the same dx₄/dt = ic to the same [q̂, p̂] = iℏ via Huygens’ principle, iterated spherical expansion, accumulated x₄-phase, the Feynman path integral, the Schrödinger equation, and the second moment of the kernel. No proposition of either route is used in the other; what the routes share is listed in §IV.2. The full propositions with proofs and geometry are developed in §§II–III; the comparison is analyzed in §IV; the reason the McGucken Principle possesses the dual-channel content that makes the dual derivation possible is developed in §V; the comparative survey of fifteen prior frameworks is in §VI; and the completeness argument for the McGucken Quantum Formalism is in §IX.

## I. Introduction

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension x₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: dx₄/dt = ic, with x₄ = ict, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

### I.1 The Coexistence of Two Formulations

Quantum mechanics has two principal formulations which have coexisted for nearly a century as mathematically equivalent but distinct presentations of the same physics. The *Hamiltonian* or *operator* formulation, introduced by Heisenberg in 1925 and developed by Born, Jordan, Dirac, and von Neumann through 1932, represents physical states as vectors in a complex Hilbert space, physical observables as self-adjoint operators on that space, and dynamics as unitary evolution generated by a Hamiltonian operator Ĥ via the Schrödinger equation iℏ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ. The algebraic heart of the Hamiltonian formulation is the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ𝟙, which expresses the non-commutativity of position and momentum observables and through the Robertson-Kennard inequality yields the Heisenberg uncertainty principle Δq·Δp ≥ ℏ/2.

The *Lagrangian* or *path-integral* formulation, foreshadowed by Dirac’s 1933 paper “The Lagrangian in Quantum Mechanics” and given its full form by Feynman in 1948, represents quantum amplitudes as sums over all paths connecting initial and final configurations, each path weighted by the exponential of i times its classical action divided by ℏ:

*K(q_f, t_f; q_i, t_i) = ∫𝒟x(t) exp(iS[x(t)]/ℏ).*

The algebraic heart of the Lagrangian formulation is this path-summation kernel, from which the Schrödinger equation is derived by expanding the short-time propagator and the canonical commutation relation follows as a consequence of the derived Schrödinger equation.

The equivalence of the two formulations is established by two results. First, Feynman’s 1948 paper derives the Schrödinger equation from the path-integral formulation — the path-integral formulation reproduces its dynamics. Second, the Stone-von Neumann theorem of 1930-1932 establishes that the Schrödinger representation of the canonical commutation relation (q̂ acting by multiplication, p̂ acting as −iℏ∂/∂q on an appropriate dense subspace of L²(ℝ)) is essentially unique up to unitary equivalence, which secures the operator formulation’s uniqueness at the representation-theoretic level. Together these results establish that the two formulations yield the same predictions for all measurable quantities and that each can be derived from the other through well-defined mathematical operations.

But equivalence is not common origin. The question addressed in this paper is different from the equivalence question: given that the two formulations are mathematically equivalent, do they both descend as theorems from a single physical principle? Is there a deeper foundation from which both the operator algebra and the path-integral kernel emerge as independent consequences, with the imaginary unit i and the action quantum ℏ both derived from that foundation rather than taken as inputs?

The answer developed in this paper is that for the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic — the physical statement that a fourth geometric axis is advancing at the velocity of light perpendicular to the three spatial dimensions, spherically symmetrically about every spacetime point — the answer is yes. Both formulations descend as independent theorems through disjoint intermediate structures. For no other proposed foundation in the century-long history of the question is the answer yes.

### I.2 What This Paper Establishes

This paper establishes four results. First (§II), the Hamiltonian operator formulation of quantum mechanics descends as a sequence of five propositions from dx₄/dt = ic, with the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ emerging at Proposition H.4 via direct computation from the Minkowski-forced momentum operator and the configuration representation forced by Stone’s theorem. Second (§III), the Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics descends as a sequence of six propositions from the same principle, through an entirely different chain: Huygens’ principle as the spherical expansion of x₄’s advance (Proposition L.1), iterated Huygens expansion generating all paths (Proposition L.2), accumulated x₄-phase producing the Feynman weight (Proposition L.3), the full path integral as continuum limit (Proposition L.4), the Schrödinger equation from Gaussian integration of the short-time propagator (Proposition L.5), and the canonical commutation relation obtained from the second moment of the kernel (Proposition L.6).

Third (§V), the reason *why* dx₄/dt = ic has this dual-derivation property is that the principle’s statement possesses *dual-channel content*: a single geometric fact that splits naturally into two logically distinct informational channels — an algebraic-symmetry channel (the invariance of x₄’s advance under spacetime isometries, which drives the Hamiltonian route through translation generators, Stone’s theorem, and Noether’s theorem) and a geometric-propagation channel (the spherical symmetry of x₄’s expansion from every point, which drives the Lagrangian route through Huygens’ principle, path summation, and wavefront accumulation). No prior proposed foundation possesses this dual-channel content.

Fourth (§VI), a comparative survey of fifteen prior frameworks that have sought foundations for quantum mechanics — spanning symplectic-geometric, stochastic, algebraic, emergent-statistical, interpretational, and discrete-determinism approaches — establishes that none of the fifteen reaches a two-route unification with both i and ℏ derived from a single geometric spacetime principle. The gap between each prior framework and the two-route structure of the McGucken Quantum Formalism is identified in derivational terms (which channel each framework has and which it lacks), not in rhetorical terms.

### I.3 Relation to the Main Lagrangian Paper

This paper is a companion to the main Lagrangian paper “The Unique McGucken Lagrangian” [108], in which the full four-sector Lagrangian ℒ_McG of physics — free-particle kinetic, Dirac matter, Yang-Mills gauge, and Einstein-Hilbert gravitational — is established as forced by dx₄/dt = ic via a single four-fold uniqueness theorem (Theorem VI.1). That paper establishes the Lagrangian’s form; this paper establishes why the Lagrangian framework, specifically, is the natural framework for physics under the McGucken Principle — because the principle generates the Lagrangian formulation of quantum mechanics (the foundation on which ℒ_McG rests) as one of two routes from a dual-channel geometric source. The companion relation is symmetric: the Lagrangian paper establishes ℒ_McG’s uniqueness within the Lagrangian framework; this paper establishes the Lagrangian framework’s naturalness as the geometric-propagation reading of dx₄/dt = ic, alongside the Hamiltonian framework as the algebraic-symmetry reading. The complete theorem chains for quantum mechanics, general relativity, and thermodynamics from dx₄/dt = ic are given in [163], [164], and [165].

The cross-references in this paper are to specific results in the main paper: §VIII.6 of [108] establishes the canonical commutation relation’s origin as the first-of-its-kind result on the canonical commutation relation; this paper deepens that result by showing the canonical commutation relation is derivable from dx₄/dt = ic through *two* routes rather than one, which is the content of the overdetermination principle developed in §VII. §VI of [108] proves the four-fold uniqueness theorem for ℒ_McG’s functional form; this paper establishes the Lagrangian-versus-Hamiltonian foundational choice that makes the uniqueness theorem’s formulation possible. §III of [108] introduces the McGucken Principle; this paper develops the principle’s dual-channel content (§V) as the feature underlying the two-route derivation.

### I.4 Provenance of the McGucken Principle: Thirty-Seven Years of Development

The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic is not a recent proposal. It has been under continuous development for nearly four decades, beginning with the author’s undergraduate work at Princeton University in the late 1980s and extending through the active derivation program of 2024-2026. A brief chronological record is included here to situate the present paper within that long arc [135]. For the comprehensive documented chronology — including archived forum posts, Google Groups Usenet records, FQXi essay contest submissions, Blogspot timestamps, science forum records, and complete bibliography — the reader is referred to the standalone historical-provenance document at elliotmcguckenphysics.com [135].

**Era I: The Princeton origin (late 1980s–1999).** The intellectual origins of the McGucken Principle trace to the author’s undergraduate years at Princeton University, working directly with three giants of twentieth-century physics: John Archibald Wheeler — Joseph Henry Professor of Physics, student of Bohr, teacher of Feynman, close colleague of Einstein — who was the author’s academic advisor; P.J.E. Peebles — Albert Einstein Professor Emeritus of Science, co-predictor of the cosmic microwave background radiation, later awarded the 2019 Nobel Prize in Physics for theoretical discoveries in physical cosmology — who was the author’s professor for quantum mechanics, using the galleys of his then-forthcoming textbook *Quantum Mechanics*; and Joseph H. Taylor Jr. — James S. McDonnell Distinguished University Professor of Physics, 1993 Nobel Laureate for the discovery of the binary pulsar PSR B1913+16 — who was the author’s professor for experimental physics and advisor for the junior paper on quantum entanglement. These Princeton afternoons, recounted in documented detail in [147] and [172], produced the specific physical intuitions that later crystallized as the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic.

The central conversation with Wheeler is a matter of record [172]. In Wheeler’s third-floor Jadwin Hall office, the author asked: *“So a photon doesn’t move in the fourth dimension? All of its motion is directed through the three spatial dimensions?”* Wheeler: *“Correct.”* The author: *“So a photon remains stationary in the fourth dimension?”* Wheeler: *“Yes.”* This exchange established the first half of the physical picture that would later ground the McGucken Principle: the photon, at |**v**| = c, is stationary in x₄ while advancing through the spatial dimensions.

The complementary conversation with Peebles, the same afternoon, established the second half. In Peebles’ office: *“When a photon is emitted from a source, it has an equal chance of being found anywhere upon a spherically-symmetric wavefront expanding at the rate of c?”* Peebles: *“Yes.”* [172]. The photon’s equal probability of being found anywhere on a spherically-symmetric expanding wavefront, combined with Wheeler’s statement that the photon is stationary in x₄, yields the physics of the McGucken Principle directly: the photon is the ideal tracer of x₄’s motion — because the photon is stationary relative to x₄ but spherically distributed on the expanding 3D wavefront, x₄ itself must be expanding spherically symmetrically at rate c. The argument is the *birth* of dx₄/dt = ic in its physical form, though the equation itself was not yet written down.

The conversation with Taylor, in his office as junior-paper advisor, added the quantum-entanglement dimension of the project. Schrödinger had written in 1935 that entanglement is “the characteristic trait of quantum mechanics” — the feature that “enforces its entire departure from classical lines of thought.” Taylor’s remark to the author: *“Schrödinger said that entanglement is the characteristic trait of quantum mechanics. Figure out the source of entanglement, and you’ll figure out the source of the quantum, as nobody really knows what, nor why, nor how ℏ is”* [172]. This charge — to identify the physical mechanism of entanglement as the gateway to understanding the quantum formalism — directly motivated the junior paper with Taylor on the Einstein-Podolsky-Rosen paradox and delayed-choice experiments, which later became the conceptual ancestor of the McGucken Equivalence identifying quantum nonlocality as a geometric consequence of x₄-coincidence on the light cone [124].

Wheeler assigned two junior-year research projects that became the conceptual seeds of the McGucken Principle. The first was the independent derivation of the time factor in the Schwarzschild metric using Wheeler’s “poor man’s reasoning” — the direct conceptual ancestor of the gravitational time-dilation argument later derived from dx₄/dt = ic through invariant x₄ expansion meeting stretched spatial geometry near a mass. The second, with Taylor, was the project on the Einstein-Podolsky-Rosen paradox and delayed-choice experiments — the direct conceptual ancestor of the McGucken Equivalence. Wheeler’s recommendation letter for graduate school, drafted after these projects, records Wheeler’s assessment at the time: *“More intellectual curiosity, versatility and yen for physics than Elliot McGucken’s I have never seen in any senior or graduate student. Originality, powerful motivation, and a can-do spirit make me think that McGucken is a top bet for graduate school in physics. I gave him as an independent task to figure out the time factor in the standard Schwarzschild expression around a spherically-symmetric center of attraction… He could and did, and wrote it all up in a beautifully clear account. His second junior paper, entitled ‘Within a Context,’ dealt with an entirely different part of physics, the Einstein-Rosen-Podolsky experiment and delayed choice experiments in general… this paper was so outstanding. I am absolutely delighted that this semester McGucken is doing a project with the cyclotron group on time reversal asymmetry.”* The time-reversal-asymmetry project referenced at the close of the letter is now visible as an early precursor of the Second-Law and arrows-of-time analysis developed in [129] — the conceptual thread from the Princeton cyclotron to that analysis runs through thirty-seven years of continuous development.

The birth of the specific equation dx₄/dt = ic came several years after these Princeton conversations. On a windsurfing-trip lunch break, while reading Einstein’s *1912 Manuscript on Relativity*, the insight crystallized that Minkowski’s coordinate x₄ = ict has physical meaning: differentiating gives dx₄/dt = ic, which encodes the physical expansion of the fourth dimension relative to the three spatial dimensions. This was the moment when the physical intuitions accumulated in Wheeler’s and Peebles’ offices — photons stationary in x₄, spherically symmetric expansion at rate c — became a single equation [172; 147]. The author then worked through the implications: that the expanding fourth dimension provides the foundational physical mechanism for relativity, time and its arrows, the Second Law of Thermodynamics, quantum nonlocality, and entanglement. The earliest written record of the equation and its consequences is an appendix to the author’s 1998–1999 doctoral dissertation at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill [126]. The dissertation’s primary topic was the Multiple Unit Artificial Retina Chipset (MARC) to Aid the Visually Impaired — an NSF-funded biomedical engineering project that subsequently helped blind patients to see, received coverage in *Business Week* and *Popular Science*, and was supported by a Merrill Lynch Innovations Grant. The physics theory is in the appendix. Drawing on the two Wheeler collaborations, the Peebles quantum mechanics course, the Taylor entanglement project, and on Minkowski’s coordinate x₄ = ict, the appendix proposes that time is not the fourth dimension itself but emerges as a measure of x₄’s physical expansion at rate c — the conceptual core of the framework that has now been under continuous development for thirty-seven years.

**Era II: Internet deployments and Usenet (2003–2006).** The theory first entered public discussion in 2003–2004 on PhysicsForums.com (member registration #3753) and on the Usenet newsgroups *sci.physics* and *sci.physics.relativity*, under the working names *Moving Dimensions Theory* (MDT) and later *Dynamic Dimensions Theory* (DDT). By 2005 the equation dx₄/dt = ic was being posted systematically on Usenet as the mathematical core of the theory. These posts are archived in Google Groups’ Usenet record.

**Era III: FQXi papers (2008–2013).** The theory received its first formal paper submission on August 25, 2008, to the Foundational Questions Institute (FQXi) essay contest: *“Time as an Emergent Phenomenon: Traveling Back to the Heroic Age of Physics (In Memory of John Archibald Wheeler)”* [127]. Four additional FQXi papers followed between 2009 and 2013, developing the derivation of the Schrödinger equation’s imaginary unit from dx₄/dt = ic, the discrete-x₄ Planck-scale quantum structure, the relationship to information-theoretic foundations, and a tribute to Wheeler’s concept of “It from Bit.” These five FQXi papers are the peer-visible, formally indexed record of the theory’s pre-2016 development [127; 132; 128; 133; 134].

**Era IV: Books and consolidation (2016–2017).** During 2016–2017 the theory was consolidated in a book series published through 45EPIC Press, including *Light Time Dimension Theory: The Foundational Physics Unifying Einstein’s Relativity and Quantum Mechanics* [144], *Einstein’s Relativity Derived from LTD Theory’s Principle* [145], *Relativity and Quantum Mechanics Unified in Pictures* [146], *Quantum Entanglement and Einstein’s “Spooky Action at a Distance” Explained via LTD Theory’s Expanding Fourth Dimension* [147], and *The Physics of Time: Time & Its Arrows in Quantum Mechanics, Relativity, The Second Law of Thermodynamics, Entropy, The Twin Paradox, & Cosmology Explained via LTD Theory’s Expanding Fourth Dimension* [148]. The 2017 book on *The Physics of Time* is the precursor of the companion paper [129], because it already contained the argument that the Second Law of Thermodynamics, entropy, and the arrows of time follow from dx₄/dt = ic — an argument whose formal technical development is the subject of that paper.

**Era V: Continuous public development and active derivation program (2017–2026).** The theory has been in continuous public development from the 2017 book series through to the present. Beginning in 2017, the author has maintained the Facebook group *Elliot McGucken Physics* [173] — currently with more than six thousand followers — as an open forum for the framework’s ongoing development, with posts dating back to 2017 and continuing through 2026. Beginning in 2020, the author has maintained a public technical blog at *goldennumberratio.medium.com* [174] titled *Dr. Elliot McGucken Theoretical Physics*, which has hosted technical papers including the original derivation of entropy’s increase [121, mirrored at Medium], the McGucken Invariance paper revisiting Einstein’s relativity of simultaneity, the Uncertainty Principle derivation [110, mirrored at Medium], the Principle of Least Action and Huygens’ Principle derivations, and comparative analyses of string theory and the McGucken Principle. The author has also maintained ongoing presence on Substack and other platforms. Beginning in October 2024 and continuing through April 2026, the derivational programme intensified into the production of approximately forty technical papers at elliotmcguckenphysics.com. These papers establish as theorems of dx₄/dt = ic: the Minkowski metric [111], the four-momentum operator and the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ [113], the Schrödinger equation [114], the Feynman path integral [115], the Born rule [149], the Dirac equation with its Clifford structure and spin-½ [150], the general Yang-Mills Lagrangian [151; 153], the Einstein field equations via Schuller’s constructive-gravity closure [153, Theorem 12], the full Noether catalog of conservation laws [112], the full four-sector Lagrangian ℒ_McG [108], the de Broglie relation [109], the Heisenberg uncertainty principle [110], the McGucken Nonlocality Principle with its Two Laws and the six senses of geometric nonlocality [123], quantum nonlocality and Bell correlations [125; 124], the Second Law and arrows of time [121; 122], and the conservation-laws-plus-Second-Law unification of the companion paper [129]. The accompanying comparative analyses establish the framework’s relationship to Jacobson’s thermodynamics of spacetime, Verlinde’s entropic gravity, Penrose’s twistor theory, Witten’s twistor string, Maldacena’s AdS/CFT, Schuller’s constructive gravity, Loop Quantum Gravity, string theory, Elitzur’s cosmology, and other contemporary foundational-physics programmes.

The present paper is situated within Era V of this trajectory. Its specific claim — that the Hamiltonian operator formulation and the Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics, together with the wave/particle, Schrödinger/Heisenberg, and locality/nonlocality dualities, all descend from dx₄/dt = ic as four simultaneous dual-channel readings of a single geometric principle — rests technically on the Era V derivations [113] (canonical commutation relation via two routes), [114] (Huygens’ Principle, Least Action, Schrödinger equation), [115] (Feynman path integral), [109] (wave-particle duality at the ontological level), [123] (locality/nonlocality at the causal/correlational level), and [124] (McGucken Equivalence); historically on the earlier development that established the Principle as a working foundation (dissertation appendix 1998–1999, FQXi papers 2008–2013 — particularly [128] which first identified the parallel between dx₄/dt = ic and the canonical commutation relation [q, p] = iℏ, a parallel developed rigorously in §§II–III of the present paper — books 2016–2017); and conceptually on the Princeton origin in Wheeler’s teaching on the Schwarzschild time factor and the EPR paradox [172]. The thirty-seven-year development trail from the Princeton afternoons of the late 1980s to the present paper is documented in full at [135], and the interested reader is encouraged to consult that record for the complete chronology.

### I.5 Structure of the Paper

§II develops the Hamiltonian route as five formal propositions. §II.1A derives the Hilbert space and the Born density (Theorem H.0). §III develops the Lagrangian route as six formal propositions. §§II and III prove the thesis for the simplest case, one spinless particle; §IIIA states case by case how far each result extends, and proves the relativistic, spin-½, and several-particle cases to the extent stated in its table. §IV compares the two routes at the level, stating what they share (§IV.2) and where they differ (translation generators versus iterated Sphere expansion, differentiation of a group versus the second moment of a kernel). §V develops the dual-channel analysis of dx₄/dt = ic that explains why the principle has this property. §VI surveys fifteen prior frameworks. §VII develops the principle of convergent overdetermination. §VIII connects the two-route result to the main Lagrangian paper. §IX develops the completeness argument. §X concludes.

## II. The Hamiltonian Route: Five Propositions

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension x₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: dx₄/dt = ic, with x₄ = ict, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

The Hamiltonian operator formulation of quantum mechanics [163] — in which physical states are vectors in a complex Hilbert space, observables are self-adjoint operators, and dynamics is generated by unitary evolution — descends from dx₄/dt = ic through five formal propositions. Each proposition is stated as a mathematical theorem with a geometry drawn from the McGucken Principle; each is proved either directly from the principle or from an earlier proposition in the chain; and each identifies explicitly the factor of i or ℏ (or both) that carry over from the principle’s geometry to the algebraic structure of the operator formalism.

### II.1 Proposition H.1: The Minkowski Metric from x₄ = ict

**Proposition H.1** *(Minkowski metric).*

*Under the integrated form x₄ = ict of the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic, the four-dimensional Euclidean line element dℓ² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² + dx₄² evaluated with dx₄ = ic dt becomes the Minkowski interval ds² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² − c² dt² with signature (−, +, +, +) (or equivalently the mostly-minus signature (+, −, −, −) under the convention ds² = c² dt² − dx₁² − dx₂² − dx₃²).*

**Proof.** The integrated form of the Principle is x₄(t) = ict + x₄(0), which under the convention x₄(0) = 0 gives x₄ = ict and therefore dx₄ = ic dt. Substituting into the four-dimensional Euclidean line element:

*dℓ² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² + dx₄² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² + (ic dt)² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² + i² · c² dt² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² − c² dt²,*

where the last equality uses i² = −1. The resulting line element is the Minkowski interval with signature (−, +, +, +). ∎

**Geometric reading.** The minus sign in front of c² dt² in the Minkowski metric is the algebraic shadow of i² = −1, which is in turn the algebraic marker of x₄’s perpendicularity to the three spatial dimensions (the imaginary unit i signifying a 90° rotation orthogonal to real axes). The appearance of i in the momentum operator p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q in later Propositions H.3–H.4 is geometrically the *same* i — the same marker of perpendicularity, appearing in two different algebraic contexts (metric signature, translation-generator) because both are algebraic manifestations of x₄’s orthogonality to x₁, x₂, x₃. The detailed proof that the integrated form x₄ = ict follows from the differential principle dx₄/dt = ic is given in [111]; the derivation of the Minkowski metric from dx₄/dt = ic (including alternative derivations through the four-speed budget and through Lorentz covariance) is given in [118, Part II] and [114, §II].

### II.1A Theorem H.0: The Hilbert Space from McGucken Sphere Wavefronts

**McGucken Principle anchor.** Each structure of the Hilbert space is a statement about x₄’s expansion. The vectors are the wavefronts the expansion produces. Linearity is the independence of the expansions from different events. The complex field is the algebra of the i of x₄ = ict. The inner product is the overlap of two wavefronts, and it is the overlap that the expansion conserves (iii), that the homogeneity of the expansion fixes to uniform weight (iv), and that is quadratic (v). Unitarity of time evolution and of translation is the conservation of that overlap (vii).

The Hamiltonian route acts on a Hilbert space. This subsection derives that space from the Principle, with every step proved here, for the simplest case: one particle of mass m without spin. The propagation used is the exact relativistic propagation, so nothing in the theorem is restricted to low speeds. Spin-½ and several particles are treated in §IIIA. The vectors are the wavefronts that x₄’s expansion produces from localized sources. The inner product is the overlap of two wavefronts. The Hilbert space is the completion.

**Definitions.** (D1) *Amplitudes.* The phase exp(−iω_C τ) of matter takes its values in the algebra generated over ℝ by 1 and the i of x₄ = ict, with i² = −1. That algebra is ℂ. (D2) *Matter propagation.* Matter of mass m obeys the matter wave equation (1/c²)∂²ψ/∂t² − ∇²ψ + μ²ψ = 0 with μ = mc/ℏ, and its rest phase exp(−iω_C t) selects the first-order branch ∂ψ/∂t = −iΩψ, Ω = c(−∇² + μ²)^(1/2). The propagation over a time t is W(t) = e^(−itΩ), which multiplies the spatial Fourier transform by e^(−iω(k)t), ω(k) = c(|k|² + μ²)^(1/2). The wave equation is derived in §III.2 from the Compton phase and the interval of Proposition H.1, and Lemma L.2.2(iv) expresses W through chains of McGucken Sphere expansions. Neither uses §§II.2 to II.5. (D3) *Wavefronts of source profiles.* By the Principle every event is a centre of expansion, and the expansions from different events proceed independently. A superposition of source events at time s with complex weights f(y)d³y, where f is a Schwartz function on ℝ³, therefore produces at time t the wavefront

*ψ_f(·, t) = W(t − s) f.*

The map f ↦ ψ_f is linear, because the expansions are independent and the wave equation is linear. Write 𝒮 for the Schwartz space of ℝ³ and V_t for the set of all wavefronts ψ_f(·, t), f ∈ 𝒮.

**Theorem H.0** *(Hilbert space from McGucken Sphere wavefronts).*

*(i) For every t, V_t = 𝒮. It is a complex vector space.*

*(ii) The overlap of two wavefronts,*

*⟨φ, ψ⟩ = ∫ φ^∗(x) ψ(x) d³x,*

*is sesquilinear, conjugate-symmetric, and positive-definite on V_t.*

*(iii) The overlap is conserved by the expansion: ⟨W(t)f, W(t)g⟩ = ⟨f, g⟩ for all f, g ∈ 𝒮 and all t.*

*(iv) Among the weighted overlaps ∫ φ^∗ψ ρ d³x with ρ continuous, positive, and polynomially bounded, invariance under every spatial translation holds only for constant ρ.*

*(v) Let F be continuous on [0, ∞) and twice continuously differentiable on (0, ∞), with F(0) = 0, F′ bounded on bounded sets, and F(s) and sF′(s) both of order s^δ as s → 0 for some δ > 0. If ∫ F(|ψ(x, t)|) d³x is independent of t for every wavefront, under the exact propagation W(t), then F(s) = κs² for a constant κ. In particular, among the functionals ∫ |ψ|^p d³x with 1 ≤ p < ∞, only p = 2 is conserved.*

*(vi) The completion of V_t in the norm ‖ψ‖ = ⟨ψ, ψ⟩^(1/2) is a separable complex Hilbert space ℋ, and ℋ ≅ L²(ℝ³, d³x).*

*(vii) The propagation W(t) and the translations (T_a ψ)(x) = ψ(x − a) extend to strongly continuous one-parameter unitary groups on ℋ, and T_a W(t) = W(t) T_a. The translate of the wavefront of a profile is the wavefront of the translated profile.*

**Proof.** (i) Since μ > 0, ω is smooth and every derivative of e^(−iω(k)t) is bounded by a polynomial in |k|. Multiplication by such a function maps 𝒮 into 𝒮, and the Fourier transform is a bijection of 𝒮, so W(t) maps 𝒮 into 𝒮. Its inverse is W(−t), so the map is onto. (ii) Sesquilinearity and conjugate symmetry are read off from the integral. ⟨ψ, ψ⟩ = ∫|ψ|² d³x ≥ 0, and if the integral vanishes then the continuous function ψ vanishes identically. (iii) By Plancherel’s theorem the overlap equals (2π)⁻³∫ F^∗G d³k for the Fourier transforms F, G of f, g, and W(t) multiplies both by the unimodular factor e^(−iω(k)t), which cancels. (iv) Translation invariance for all φ, ψ ∈ 𝒮 gives ∫ φ^∗ψ (ρ(x + a) − ρ(x)) d³x = 0 for all φ, ψ, hence ρ(x + a) = ρ(x) for all x and a. (v) is proved after the theorem, as Lemma H.0.2. (vi) 𝒮 contains the smooth functions of compact support, which are dense in L²(ℝ³), and L²(ℝ³) is complete by the Riesz–Fischer theorem [105]. A complete space in which V_t is dense is the completion, and the completion is unique up to isometric isomorphism. The finite combinations of products of Hermite functions with rational complex coefficients are countable and dense, so ℋ is separable. (vii) W(t) and T_a are isometric on the dense subspace 𝒮 and map it onto itself, so they extend uniquely to unitary operators on ℋ. T_a multiplies the Fourier transform by e^(−ik·a), so it commutes with W(t). For f ∈ 𝒮, ‖W(t)f − f‖² = (2π)⁻³∫|e^(−iω(k)t) − 1|²|F|² d³k → 0 as t → 0 by dominated convergence, and the same holds for T_a as a → 0. Strong continuity on ℋ follows because the operators have norm one and 𝒮 is dense. The last statement is T_a W(t)f = W(t)T_a f. ∎

**Lemma H.0.2** *(Proof of part (v)).*

**Proof.** Take a profile f = g e^(iθ) with g > 0 a Gaussian and θ a real smooth function with bounded derivatives, and its dilations f_λ(x) = f(x/λ), λ ≥ 1. Step 1, the derivative. Since ∂ψ/∂t = −iΩψ and |f| > 0, the derivative of |ψ| at t = 0 is Im(e^(−iθ)Ωf), and the difference quotients of F(|ψ|) are dominated by an integrable function, because F′ is bounded on the range of |ψ| and ψ, Ωψ are Schwartz functions uniformly for t in a bounded interval. Hence

*(d/dt) ∫ F(|ψ|) d³x = ∫ F′(g) Im(e^(−iθ) Ωf) d³x, t = 0.*

Step 2, splitting Ω. By the bound of Lemma L.4.1, Ω = ω_C + (c/2μ)(−∇²) − R̂, where R̂ multiplies the Fourier transform by r(k) with 0 ≤ r(k) ≤ c|k|⁴/(8μ³). The term ω_C contributes Im(e^(−iθ)ω_C f) = 0. Step 3, the main term. Im(e^(−iθ)(−∇²f)) = −(1/g)∇·(g²∇θ). Integrating by parts twice, with H(s) = sF′(s) − 2F(s), so that H′(s) = sF″(s) − F′(s), and with no boundary terms because H(g) and gF′(g) are of order g^δ and g is a Gaussian,

*∫ (F′(g)/g)(−∇·(g²∇θ)) d³x = ∫ ∇(H(g))·∇θ d³x = −∫ H(g) ∇²θ d³x.*

Step 4, scaling. For f_λ the main term equals −(c/2μ) λ ∫ H(g)∇²θ d³x, since the volume element contributes λ³ and the Laplacian λ⁻². The remainder is bounded by sup|F′(g)| · ‖(1 + |x|²/λ²)⁻¹‖₂ · ‖(1 + |x|²/λ²) R̂f_λ‖₂ ≤ Cλ^(3/2) · λ^(3/2 − 4) = Cλ⁻¹, because λ⁴r(k/λ) and its first two derivatives are bounded by polynomials in |k| uniformly in λ ≥ 1. Step 5, conclusion. Conservation makes the derivative vanish for every λ. Dividing by λ and letting λ → ∞ gives ∫ H(g)∇²θ d³x = 0 for every admissible θ, in particular for every smooth θ of compact support. So H(g(x)) is a continuous weakly harmonic function on ℝ³ that tends to H(0) = 0 at infinity, and it vanishes identically by the maximum principle. A Gaussian of height a takes every value in (0, a], so H(s) = 0 for all s > 0. Then sF′(s) = 2F(s), whose solutions are F(s) = κs². ∎

**Corollary H.0.3** *(The Born density).*

*Suppose the probability density for detecting the particle at x at time t is ρ(x, t) = F(|ψ(x, t)|), a function of the modulus of the wavefront at x, with F as in (v), and suppose the total probability ∫ρ d³x is the same at every time. Then ρ = κ|ψ|². With the normalization ∫ρ d³x = 1,*

*ρ(x, t) = |ψ(x, t)|² / ⟨ψ, ψ⟩.*

**Proof.** Part (v) of Theorem H.0. ∎

**Corollary H.0.4** *(Momentum distribution from the position distribution).*

*For the non-relativistic propagation and a normalized profile f with Fourier transform f̂(k) = (2π)^(−3/2)∫e^(−ik·y) f(y) d³y, the probability of finding x/t in the set (ℏ/m)A at time t tends to ∫_A |f̂(k)|² d³k as t → ∞, for every measurable set A of wavevectors.*

**Proof.** By Lemma L.4.1(i), up to a phase independent of x, ψ(x, t) = (m/iℏt)^(3/2) e^(im|x|²/2ℏt) ĝₜ(mx/ℏt), where g_t(y) = e^(im|y|²/2ℏt) f(y). As t → ∞, g_t → f in L², so ĝₜ → f̂ in L². The change of variables k = mx/ℏt has Jacobian (ℏt/m)³, which cancels the prefactor, and gives ∫ over x/t ∈ (ℏ/m)A of |ψ|² d³x = ∫_A |ĝₜ(k)|² d³k → ∫_A |f̂(k)|² d³k. ∎

**McGucken Principle anchor for the Born rule.** The probability density is |ψ|² because that is the one function of the wavefront’s modulus whose total the expansion of x₄ conserves. The momentum distribution |f̂(k)|² is the position distribution after the Sphere expansion has carried each wavenumber component to the place x = (ℏk/m)t. The two hypotheses of Corollary H.0.3 are themselves statements of the Principle. Dependence on the modulus alone is phase invariance, requirement (R3) of [169, §7.3], which reads: “P(e^iα ψ) = P(ψ) for all α ∈ ℝ. (A global phase is a homogeneous shift of the x₄-origin, geometrically unobservable because the McGucken expansion is universal from every event.)” Dependence on the wavefront at the point of detection is [169, Theorem 7.4], which reads: “The Born density P = ψ* ψ at an event B is the geometric overlap, at B, of the forward x₄-expansion (carrying phase from x₄ = ict) and the conjugate x₄*-expansion (carrying phase from x₄* = −ict).” Both statements are posted in [170], and the relation of the Born rule to gravity and entropy is developed in [171]. Conservation of the total is Theorem H.0(iii). Bilinearity in ψ and its conjugate is the conclusion. Rules that divide by a state-dependent total, such as |ψ|^p/∫|ψ|^p, conserve the total by construction and are not excluded by Corollary H.0.3; they are excluded by the finite rate of the expansion in Theorem S.6. The earlier corpus derivation from the SO(3) symmetry of the McGucken Sphere is in [149].

**Remark H.0.1** *(Point events).* The wavefront of a single point event is the kernel of W(t). In the non-relativistic regime its modulus is the same at every point of space (Remark L.4.2), so its overlap with itself diverges. Point-event wavefronts are generalized vectors, in the same sense as plane waves. The vectors of ℋ are the wavefronts of source profiles, which are superpositions of point events with Schwartz weights, and their limits.

**Relation to the corpus theorem.** The same construction is stated in [167, Theorem 6.1.5], whose proof is reproduced verbatim and in full in Appendix F and whose statement is: “The Hilbert space 𝓗 of nonrelativistic single-particle quantum mechanics is the Cauchy completion of the complex vector space of Huygens–Fresnel–Kirchhoff superpositions of McGucken-Sphere wavefronts, over source-event distributions of rapid decay, on the spatial slice ℝ³ at parameter time t, equipped with the geometric overlap inner product ⟨ φ, ψ ⟩_t := ∫_{ℝ³} φ^∗(x, t) ψ(x, t) d³x, defined as the integral over the spatial slice of the pointwise product of two wavefronts’ complex values at each spatial-slice point — the geometry of ‘the simultaneous presence of two McGucken-Sphere wavefronts at the same spatial point’ integrated over the slice. The inner product’s three pre-Hilbert axioms — sesquilinearity, conjugate symmetry, positive-definiteness — are inherited from McGucken structures already established in the dx₄/dt = ic framework: rank-2 sesquilinearity from Lemma 7.1 (rank-2 metric pairing), conjugate symmetry from Theorem 7.11 (PT-symmetry through the source event), positive-definiteness from Theorem 7.4 (Born-density non-negativity). The Cauchy completion in the norm topology gives 𝓗 ≅ L²(ℝ³, d³x), the same Hilbert space supplied by the Algebraic Channel’s Stone–von Neumann derivation. The convergence of Geometric and Algebraic Channels on the same 𝓗 is the consistency of the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework: the algebraic representation theory (Algebraic Channel) and the wavefront overlap geometry (dx₄/dt = ic Geometric Channel) describe the same Hilbert space from two derivational directions, both descending from dx₄/dt = ic.” Theorem H.0 proves this construction within the present paper, with the primitive vectors taken as the wavefronts of source profiles (Remark H.0.1).

**Scope.** Theorem H.0 and its corollaries are for one particle without spin. Corollary H.0.4 is proved for the non-relativistic propagation. Spin-½ is treated in §IIIA.3, where Theorem S.6 also excludes renormalized rules, and several particles and general observables in §IIIA.4.

### II.2 Proposition H.2: The Momentum Operator as Generator of Spatial Translations

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The momentum operator is the generator of spatial translation, and spatial translation is a symmetry because dx₄/dt = ic is the same at every point. Stone’s theorem produces a generator with the dimension of a wavenumber. The action h, which the Principle carries as the action per wavelength of the expanding Sphere, converts the wavenumber into a momentum. Stone’s theorem by itself fixes no scale of action: for every κ > 0 the operator −iκ∂/∂q generates the same shift through exp(−ia(−iκ∂/∂q)/κ). The scale is fixed by the McGucken Principle, which carries the action h. The complex unit i in the exponent is the complex unit of ℋ, which by Theorem H.0 (D1) is the i of x₄ = ict. Lemma H.3.1 below shows the exact sense in which it is the i of x₄ = ict.

**Inputs of the Hamiltonian route.** The route uses the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic, x₄ expanding at rate c from every spacetime point, dx₄/dt = ic, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h, and the Hilbert space ℋ that Theorem H.0 derives from the Principle. It derives the unitary translation group, the momentum operator, its configuration form, the commutation relation, and the uniqueness of the representation.

**Proposition H.2** *(Momentum operator as translation generator).*

*Let ℋ be the Hilbert space of Theorem H.0. Spatial translations along a fixed axis are represented on ℋ by the strongly continuous one-parameter unitary group (U(a)ψ)(q) = ψ(q − a), and*

*U(a) = exp(−iaK̂) = exp(−ia p̂/ℏ), a ∈ ℝ,*

*where K̂ is a unique self-adjoint operator, the wavenumber operator, and p̂ = ℏK̂ is the momentum operator. The constant ℏ = h/2π is the action of the Principle: the Sphere’s wavelength sets the action h, so a state of wavelength λ = 2π/k carries the momentum p with pλ = h.*

**Proof.** Step 1, symmetry. By the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic the expansion of x₄ is the same at every spatial point: the rate is c at x and at x + a, and the Sphere about x + a is the translate of the Sphere about x. The wavefront of a translated source profile is therefore the translate of the wavefront, which is Theorem H.0(vii), and the translations act on ℋ as the strongly continuous unitary group (U(a)ψ)(q) = ψ(q − a). Step 2, Stone’s theorem. Every strongly continuous one-parameter unitary group has the form U(a) = exp(−iaK̂) for a unique self-adjoint K̂ [20; 105, §VIII.4]. The operator K̂ has the dimension of inverse length. Step 3, wavenumber. On a generalized eigenstate of K̂ with eigenvalue k, U(a) multiplies by e^(−iak), and a displacement by λ = 2π/k restores the state exactly: k is the wavenumber and λ the wavelength. Step 4, the action h. By the McGucken Principle the wavelength of the Sphere sets the action h. A state of wavelength λ carries momentum p with pλ = h, so p = ℏk, and the momentum operator is p̂ = ℏK̂ [117, §V; 109]. ∎

**Remark H.2.1** *(Abstract form).* If only the rays of ℋ and their transition probabilities are given, the same group follows from symmetry alone. A bijection of rays that preserves transition probabilities is implemented by a unitary or antiunitary operator, unique up to a phase [101; 103]. A translation by a is the square of the translation by a/2, and the square of an antiunitary operator is unitary. The phases can be chosen so that U(a)U(b) = U(a + b) [102], and a weakly measurable one-parameter unitary group on a separable Hilbert space is strongly continuous [104].

### II.3 Proposition H.3: The Configuration Representation

**Proposition H.3** *(Momentum operator in the configuration representation).*

*In the Schrödinger (configuration) representation of quantum mechanics, the position operator q̂ acts by multiplication ((q̂ψ)(q) = q·ψ(q)) on an appropriate dense subspace of L²(ℝ), and the translation operator U(a) of Proposition H.2 acts as (U(a)ψ)(q) = ψ(q − a). Differentiating U(a) with respect to a at a = 0 yields the momentum operator as*

*p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q.*

*The factor i is the same i as in Proposition H.2, which is in turn the same i as in the Minkowski metric of Proposition H.1, which is in turn the marker of x₄’s perpendicularity to the three spatial dimensions. The factor ℏ is the action per radian of the x₄-cycle (h per cycle) at the Planck frequency.*

**Proof.** In the configuration representation on L²(ℝ), the translation U(a) acts geometrically by shifting the argument: (U(a)ψ)(q) = ψ(q − a). This is the natural action of spatial translation on wavefunctions, and it is forced by the requirement that if ψ is concentrated at q = q₀, then U(a)ψ should be concentrated at q = q₀ + a (translation moves the state forward by a in position space). By the Stone-theorem formula U(a) = exp(−iap̂/ℏ), differentiating at a = 0 gives

*(d/da)|_{a=0} U(a)ψ = −(i/ℏ) p̂ ψ.*

On the other hand, directly differentiating (U(a)ψ)(q) = ψ(q − a) with respect to a at a = 0 gives

*(d/da)|_{a=0} ψ(q − a) = −ψ′(q) = −∂ψ/∂q.*

Equating the two expressions: −(i/ℏ)(p̂ψ)(q) = −∂ψ/∂q, which rearranges to

*(p̂ψ)(q) = −iℏ ∂ψ/∂q,*

the standard Schrödinger-representation momentum operator. ∎

**Geometric reading.** The momentum operator is “position-momentum conjugate” not by algebraic convention but because position and momentum are Fourier duals on the Hilbert space. The factor i in p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q is the same factor i that appears in the Minkowski metric signature through Proposition H.1 and in the unitary exponent through Proposition H.2. All three factors of i — metric, unitary exponent, momentum operator — have one geometric source: the perpendicularity marker of x₄. Lemma H.3.1 states this exactly.

**Lemma H.3.1** *(Four-component form of the generators).*

*Let Ĥ be the self-adjoint generator of time translations, U_t = exp(−iĤt/ℏ), obtained from Theorem H.0(vii) and Stone’s theorem, so that iℏ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ. With the coordinates (x₁, x₂, x₃, x₄) and x₄ = ict, define p̂_μ = −iℏ∂/∂x_μ for μ = 1, 2, 3, 4. Then:*

*(i) p̂₄ = −(ℏ/c)∂/∂t, and on states p̂₄ψ = (i/c)Ĥψ. In eigenvalues, p₄ = iE/c and Ĥ = −ic p̂₄.*

*(ii) Σ_μ p_μ x_μ = p·x − Et, so the plane wave exp((i/ℏ)Σ_μ p_μ x_μ) is exp((i/ℏ)(p·x − Et)).*

*(iii) Σ_μ p_μ p_μ = |p|² − E²/c².*

**Proof.** (i) ∂/∂x₄ = (1/ic)∂/∂t, so p̂₄ = −iℏ/(ic) · ∂/∂t = −(ℏ/c)∂/∂t. On states ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ/(iℏ), hence p̂₄ψ = −Ĥψ/(ic) = (i/c)Ĥψ. (ii) p₄x₄ = (iE/c)(ict) = −Et. (iii) p₄² = −E²/c². ∎

**McGucken Principle anchor.** One prescription, p̂_μ = −iℏ∂/∂x_μ over the four coordinates with x₄ = ict, gives the three momentum operators and the Hamiltonian. The temporal Schrödinger form iℏ∂/∂t is the x₄-component of the generator rewritten with dx₄ = ic dt, and the Hamiltonian is the generator of translation along x₄: Ĥ = −ic p̂₄. The i of the generators and the i of x₄ = ict combine to give real observables E and p, and the Lorentz-invariant phase (p·x − Et)/ℏ. This is exactly the statement that the i of the operator formalism and the i of x₄ = ict are one i.

**Note.** The lemma acts in the Hilbert space of Theorem H.0. For free matter the generator is explicit: Ĥ = ℏΩ = (m²c⁴ + c²|p̂|²)^(1/2).

### II.4 Proposition H.4: The Canonical Commutation Relation by Direct Computation

**Proposition H.4** *(Canonical commutation relation from H.3).*

*Given the position operator q̂ acting by multiplication and the momentum operator p̂ acting as −iℏ ∂/∂q of Proposition H.3, the commutator [q̂, p̂] satisfies*

*[q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙*

*as an operator identity on the domain of both operators.*

**Proof.** For any test function f(q) in the common domain of q̂ and p̂:

*[q̂, p̂]f(q) = q̂(p̂ f)(q) − p̂(q̂ f)(q)* *= q · (−iℏ ∂f/∂q) − (−iℏ ∂/∂q)(q · f(q))* *= −iℏ q ∂f/∂q − (−iℏ)(f(q) + q ∂f/∂q)* *= −iℏ q ∂f/∂q + iℏ f(q) + iℏ q ∂f/∂q* *= iℏ f(q).*

Since this holds for every f in the domain, [q̂, p̂] acts as iℏ times the identity operator, i.e. [q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙. ∎

**Geometric reading.** The canonical commutation relation — the algebraic heart of the Hamiltonian formulation of quantum mechanics — is reached here as a three-line computation from the explicit form of p̂ established in Proposition H.3. The factor iℏ on the right-hand side is not an independent physical constant; the i is the perpendicularity marker of x₄ (same i as in the Minkowski metric and the momentum operator), and the ℏ is the action per radian of the x₄-cycle (h per cycle) at the Planck frequency (same ℏ as in the unitary exponent of Stone’s theorem). Both constants enter the canonical commutation relation because both enter the momentum operator, and both enter the momentum operator because both are inherited from dx₄/dt = ic through the preceding propositions. The derivation reaches [q̂, p̂] = iℏ in four steps from the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic and Theorem H.0.

### II.5 Proposition H.5: Representation Uniqueness via Stone–von Neumann

**McGucken Principle anchor.** Propositions H.1 to H.4 take the route from dx₄/dt = ic to [q̂, p̂] = iℏ. Proposition H.5 closes it: once the Weyl relation holds with the action ℏ of the Principle, the operator formalism is fixed up to unitary equivalence. The Weyl form is the rigorous statement of the commutation relation, because q̂ and p̂ are unbounded and the relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ𝟙 holds only on a common dense domain.

**Proposition H.5** *(Representation uniqueness).*

*Let U(a) = exp(−ia p̂/ℏ) and V(b) = exp(ib q̂/ℏ). The commutation relation of Proposition H.4 holds in the Weyl form*

*U(a)V(b) = exp(−iab/ℏ) V(b)U(a), a, b ∈ ℝ,*

*and every irreducible, strongly continuous unitary representation of this relation is unitarily equivalent to the Schrödinger representation, q̂ by multiplication and p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q on L²(ℝ). This is the Stone–von Neumann theorem [21; 20].*

**Proof.** In the Schrödinger representation (U(a)f)(q) = f(q − a) and (V(b)f)(q) = e^(ibq/ℏ) f(q), so (U(a)V(b)f)(q) = e^(ib(q − a)/ℏ) f(q − a) = e^(−iab/ℏ)(V(b)U(a)f)(q). The uniqueness statement is the Stone–von Neumann theorem, proved in [21] and in modern form in [8, Theorem 14.8]. ∎

**Scope.** The Stone–von Neumann theorem is a uniqueness theorem for irreducible regular representations of the Weyl relation with a finite number of degrees of freedom and a fixed non-zero ℏ. It fixes the representation. It does not fix the Hamiltonian: p̂²/2m and p̂²/2m + ½mω²q̂² act in the same representation with different dynamics. The dynamics is fixed on the Lagrangian route, by the action of Proposition L.3, and in [108].

The Hamiltonian route is complete at this point. §III traces the second route from the same Principle to the same commutation relation.

## III. The Lagrangian Route: Six Propositions

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension x₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: dx₄/dt = ic, with x₄ = ict, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

The Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics [163] — in which amplitudes are computed by summing over all paths connecting initial and final configurations, each path weighted by the exponential of i times its classical action divided by ℏ — descends from dx₄/dt = ic through six formal propositions. The chain is entirely distinct from the Hamiltonian chain of §II: where the Hamiltonian route proceeded through the Minkowski metric, Stone’s theorem, and direct commutator computation, the Lagrangian route proceeds through Huygens’ principle, iterated spherical expansion, phase accumulation, the Feynman kernel, and Gaussian integration of the short-time propagator. No proposition of §II is used; what the two routes share is listed in §IV.2.

### III.1 Proposition L.1: Huygens’ Principle from x₄’s Spherical Expansion

**McGucken Principle anchor.** Huygens stated the secondary-wavelet construction in 1678 as a rule for building wavefronts. The McGucken Principle supplies the physical mechanism for that rule: every spacetime point is a centre of x₄’s spherical expansion at rate c, and the McGucken Sphere of that expansion is the secondary wavelet [114, §III; 118, Part IV]. The Sphere is the physical object. The null cone Σ⁺(p₀) and the Green’s function G₀ are mathematics that descend from it.

**Proposition L.1** *(Huygens’ principle as a theorem of x₄’s spherical expansion).*

*The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic states that x₄ expands at rate c, spherically symmetrically, from every spacetime point. For an event p₀ = (x₀, t₀), the McGucken Sphere M⁺_p₀(t) is the fixed-t cross-section of that expansion, the 2-sphere |x − x₀| = c(t − t₀) for t > t₀. The Spheres M⁺_p₀(t), taken over all t > t₀, sweep out the future null cone Σ⁺(p₀). Then:*

*(i) The Sphere M⁺_p₀(t) is Huygens’ secondary wavelet from p₀.*

*(ii) The retarded Green’s function G₀ of the wave operator, defined by [(1/c²)∂²/∂t² − ∇²]G₀ = δ(t − t′)δ³(x − x′) with G₀ = 0 for t < t′, is*

*G₀(x − x′, t − t′) = δ(t − t′ − |x − x′|/c) / (4π|x − x′|),*

*and its support at each time t > t′ is exactly the Sphere M⁺_p′(t) of the source event p′ = (x′, t′).*

**Proof.** (i) The Principle selects no spatial direction, so the expansion from p₀ reaches, at time t, exactly the points at spatial distance c(t − t₀) from x₀. That set is M⁺_p₀(t). Huygens’ 1678 construction assigns to every point of a wavefront a secondary wavelet that is a sphere of radius c(t − t₀) about that point. The two sets are the same set. (ii) For r = |x − x′| > 0 and any twice-differentiable f, the function f(t − r/c)/(4πr) satisfies the homogeneous wave equation, by direct differentiation in spherical coordinates. At r = 0 the identity −∇²(1/(4πr)) = δ³(x − x′) supplies the source. With f = δ the source is δ(t − t′)δ³(x − x′), and f(t − r/c) = 0 for t < t′ gives the retarded condition. The delta function is supported where r = c(t − t′), which is M⁺_p′(t). ∎

**Note.** The Hamiltonian route used the perpendicularity of x₄ (the i of x₄ = ict, which gave the Minkowski interval and the four-component generator). The Lagrangian route starts from the spherical expansion of x₄ (which gives the Sphere and G₀). Both are stated in the one Principle.

### III.2 Proposition L.2: Iterated Huygens Generates All Paths

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The sum over paths is the iterated spherical expansion of x₄. Each link of each chain is one Sphere expansion at rate c. The Compton coupling of matter to that expansion enters as the weight −μ² at each re-expansion event. Interior points of the null cone are reached by chains with one or more re-expansions, which is how a propagation built from Spheres of radius cε reaches displacements |η| < cε [115, §III].

The massless function G₀ of Proposition L.1 is supported on the Sphere alone, at spatial distance exactly cε after a time ε. The short-time kernel of Proposition L.5 is supported at every displacement η. This subsection supplies the connection in two lemmas. The first shows that matter of mass m propagates by chains of Sphere expansions, and that those chains reach every event inside the null cone. The second shows that the matter branch of this propagation composes over time slices, which produces the chains of intermediate positions that the path integral sums.

**Matter wave equation.** Matter of mass m at spatial rest carries the phase exp(−iω_C t), with ω_C = mc²/ℏ the Compton angular frequency [116; 112]. Write μ = mc/ℏ = ω_C/c for the Compton wavenumber. The phase is a Lorentz scalar, so in a frame in which the matter moves it reads exp(i(k·x − ωt)) with ω²/c² − |k|² = μ². Every such wave, and every superposition of such waves, satisfies

*(1/c²)∂²ψ/∂t² − ∇²ψ + μ²ψ = 0.*

**Lemma L.2.1** *(Sphere-chain expansion of the matter propagator).*

*Let G_m be the retarded Green’s function of the operator (1/c²)∂²/∂t² − ∇² + μ², and let ∗ denote convolution over spacetime. Then*

*G_m = G₀ − μ² G₀∗G₀ + μ⁴ G₀∗G₀∗G₀ − ⋯ = Σ_n (−μ²)ⁿ G₀^∗(n+1), n = 0, 1, 2, …,*

*with convergence in the sense of Laplace transforms on Re s > cμ. The n-th term is the sum over all chains of n + 1 Sphere expansions joined at n intermediate events, each intermediate event carrying the weight −μ². Every term is supported in the closed future null cone of the source event. The n = 0 term is supported on the cone. The n = 1 term is*

*−μ² (G₀∗G₀)(x, t) = −(μ²c/8π) θ(ct − |x|),*

*which is non-zero at every event inside the cone.*

**Proof.** Take the Laplace transform in t (variable s = σ + iω, σ > 0) and the Fourier transform in x (variable k). The two defining equations become Ĝ₀ = 1/(s²/c² + |k|²) and Ĝₘ = 1/(s²/c² + |k|² + μ²). Put z = s²/c² + |k|². Then |z|² = ((σ² − ω²)/c² + |k|²)² + 4σ²ω²/c⁴. If ω² ≤ σ², the first bracket is at least (σ² − ω²)/c² ≥ 0, so |z|² ≥ (σ² − ω²)²/c⁴ + 4σ²ω²/c⁴ = (σ² + ω²)²/c⁴ ≥ σ⁴/c⁴. If ω² > σ², then |z|² ≥ 4σ²ω²/c⁴ > σ⁴/c⁴. In both cases |z| ≥ σ²/c², so for σ > cμ we have |μ²Ĝ₀| ≤ c²μ²/σ² < 1, uniformly in k and ω, and the geometric series

*Ĝₘ = (1/z) · 1/(1 + μ²/z) = Σ_n (−μ²)ⁿ / zⁿ⁺¹ = Σ_n (−μ²)ⁿ Ĝ₀ⁿ⁺¹*

converges uniformly. A product of transforms is the transform of a convolution, and a distribution vanishing for t < 0 is determined by its Laplace transform on a half-plane, so the identity holds for the retarded functions themselves. The closed future cone is closed under addition of vectors, so a convolution of distributions supported in it is supported in it. For the n = 1 term, the transform of (c/8π)θ(ct − r) is (c/8πs)∫e^(−sr/c) e^(−ik·x) d³x = (c/8πs) · 8π(s/c)/((s/c)² + |k|²)² = 1/z², which is Ĝ₀². ∎

**Lemma L.2.2** *(Matter branch and composition over time slices).*

*Let Ω = c(−∇² + μ²)^(1/2), the positive self-adjoint operator on L²(ℝ³) that multiplies the Fourier transform by ω(k) = c(|k|² + μ²)^(1/2). Then:*

*(i) G_m(t) = θ(t) c² Ω⁻¹ sin(tΩ) = θ(t) (c²/2iΩ)(e^(itΩ) − e^(−itΩ)).*

*(ii) Matter, whose rest phase is exp(−iω_C t), propagates on the branch W(t) = e^(−itΩ), and ω(0) = ω_C.*

*(iii) W is a strongly continuous one-parameter unitary group, W(t₁ + t₂) = W(t₁)W(t₂). With K_t the convolution kernel of W(t) and ε = (t_f − t_i)/N,*

*K_(t_f − t_i)(x_f − x_i) = ∫⋯∫ K_ε(x_f − x_(N−1)) ⋯ K_ε(x₂ − x₁) K_ε(x₁ − x_i) d³x₁ ⋯ d³x_(N−1).*

*(iv) For t > 0, W(t) = (1/c²)(∂/∂t − iΩ) G_m(t). The first-order evolution of the matter branch is obtained from the Sphere-chain propagator G_m of Lemma L.2.1 by this exact formula.*

**Proof.** (i) The spatial Fourier transform g(k, t) of G_m solves (1/c²)g″ + (|k|² + μ²)g = δ(t) with g = 0 for t < 0. The solution is continuous at t = 0 with a jump c² in g′, which gives g = θ(t)c² sin(ω(k)t)/ω(k). (ii) A wave with |k| = 0 on the branch e^(−itΩ) has time dependence exp(−iω(0)t) = exp(−iω_C t), the rest phase. On the other branch it has the conjugate phase. (iii) W(t) is multiplication by the unimodular function e^(−iω(k)t) in the Fourier representation, so it is unitary, strongly continuous, and additive in t. Writing W(t_f − t_i) = W(ε)ᴺ in the position representation gives the N-fold convolution. (iv) By (i), for t > 0, (1/c²)∂G_m/∂t = cos(tΩ) and (1/c²)iΩG_m = i sin(tΩ), and cos(tΩ) − i sin(tΩ) = e^(−itΩ). ∎

**Remark L.2.3** *(Why the composition is on W and not on G_m).* The retarded function of a second-order equation is not an evolution semigroup: in a Fourier mode it is c² sin(ωt)/ω, which vanishes at t = 0 and does not satisfy g(t + s) = g(t)g(s). The second-order equation propagates the pair (ψ, ∂ψ/∂t). On the matter branch that pair is tied by ∂ψ/∂t = −iΩψ, the evolution is first order, and W(t) is its semigroup. Part (iv) expresses W through G_m, so the composition law of (iii) is a statement about Sphere-chain propagation.

**Proposition L.2** *(Path summation from iterated Sphere expansion).*

*(a) The propagation of matter of mass m from one event to another is the sum over all chains of McGucken Sphere expansions connecting them, with the weight −μ² = −(mc/ℏ)² at each intermediate event (Lemma L.2.1). (b) On the matter branch, propagation over [t_i, t_f] is the N-fold integral over intermediate positions x₁, …, x_(N−1) at the times t_i + jε (Lemma L.2.2). Each choice of intermediate positions is a polygonal chain from x_i to x_f. As N → ∞ with t_f − t_i fixed, these chains are the paths of the Feynman sum.*

**Proof.** (a) is Lemma L.2.1 and (b) is Lemma L.2.2(iii). ∎

**Scope.** At fixed c, every chain of Sphere expansions satisfies |x(t) − x(s)| ≤ c|t − s|, and every uniform limit of such chains satisfies the same bound: the histories generated at fixed c are exactly the paths of speed at most c [166, Theorem 4.1]. The set of all continuous paths is reached in the non-relativistic limit c → ∞ taken at a fixed time step ε, in which the Sphere of radius cε contains every displacement; the limit N → ∞ is taken afterwards (Lemma L.4.1(iii) and Proposition L.4). The function G_m is supported in the closed null cone. The single branch W(t) taken alone has a kernel with tails outside the cone that decay exponentially on the scale of the reduced Compton wavelength ℏ/mc. The causal object is G_m. The non-relativistic regime of Propositions L.3 to L.6 is the regime ε ≫ ℏ/mc² and |η| ≪ cε, in which the cone contains every displacement that contributes and the number of re-expansion events per time step is of order ω_C ε ≫ 1.

### III.3 Proposition L.3: Accumulated x₄-Phase Gives the Feynman Weight

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The Feynman weight exp(iS/ℏ) is the phase of the accumulated x₄-advance, counted in reduced Compton wavelengths: phase = exp(−μX₄). The kinetic term +½mv² arises because motion lowers the Sphere-tangent share c/γ and so lowers the advance accumulated per unit coordinate time. The potential term −V arises because the potential raises the rest energy and so raises the phase rate. The action h of the Principle enters through ω_C = mc²/ℏ, the rate at which matter of mass m counts the wavelengths of x₄’s expansion [117, §V].

**Proposition L.3** *(Path weight from accumulated x₄-advance).*

*Let a path x(t), t_i ≤ t ≤ t_f, have velocity v(t) with |v| < c, and let τ[path] = ∫(1 − v²/c²)^(1/2) dt be its proper time. The accumulated x₄-advance along the path is X₄[path] = ic·τ[path], proper time up to the factor ic. Matter of mass m carries along the path the phase*

*phase(path) = exp(−iω_C τ[path]) = exp(−μ X₄[path]) = exp(i S_rel[path]/ℏ), S_rel = −mc² ∫ dτ.*

*In a potential V(q), and in the non-relativistic regime,*

*phase(path) = exp(−imc²(t_f − t_i)/ℏ) · exp(i S[path]/ℏ) · exp(iρ), S[path] = ∫ (½mv² − V) dt,*

*where the first factor is the same for every path with the given end times and the real remainder satisfies |ρ| ≤ (1/ℏ)∫ (mv⁴/(2c²) + |V|v²/c²) dt.*

**Derivation.** Step 1, rest. Matter at spatial rest carries the phase exp(−iω_C t), ω_C = mc²/ℏ [116; 112]. The sign is the one for which the rest energy mc² is positive under the convention Û(t) = exp(−iĤt/ℏ) of Lemma V.7.3.A. Step 2, motion. The Principle dx₄/dt = ic holds at every event of every worldline. What changes with motion is the Sphere-tangent share v_x₄ = (c² − v²)^(1/2) = c/γ, the standard time-dilation factor c·dτ/dt, fixed by the four-speed budget v² + v_x₄² = c² [108, Proposition III.2]. The accumulated advance is X₄ = ic·τ with dτ = dt/γ. The phase is a Lorentz scalar that equals −ω_C t in the rest frame, where t = τ, so along the path it equals −ω_C τ. Since ω_C τ = cμτ and X₄ = icτ, the phase is exp(−μX₄). Step 3, expansion. For 0 ≤ x ≤ 1, 0 ≤ 1 − x/2 − (1 − x)^(1/2) ≤ x²/2. With x = v²/c² this gives mc²/γ = mc² − ½mv² − r, 0 ≤ r ≤ mv⁴/(2c²), so

*−(mc²/γ) dt/ℏ = −(mc²/ℏ) dt + (½mv²/ℏ) dt + (r/ℏ) dt.*

Step 4, potential. This step takes as input that a static potential V(q) changes the energy of matter at rest at q from mc² to mc² + V(q), and with it the rest phase rate. For moving matter the rate is (mc² + V)/(γℏ) = (mc² − ½mv² + V)/ℏ up to the stated remainder, since 0 ≤ 1 − 1/γ ≤ v²/c². Integrating over the path gives the displayed formula. ∎

### III.4 Proposition L.4: The Full Feynman Path Integral

**McGucken Principle anchor.** Every element of the path integral is now a statement about x₄’s expansion. The sum over paths is the iterated Sphere expansion of Proposition L.2. The weight exp(iS/ℏ) is the accumulated x₄-advance of Proposition L.3. The measure is the Fresnel normalization of Lemma L.4.1. Feynman’s 1948 paper introduces the kernel as a postulate [7; 115, §IV]. Here it is derived from dx₄/dt = ic.

**Lemma L.4.1** *(Short-time kernel and its normalization).*

*In d spatial dimensions the kernel of the matter branch W(ε) of Lemma L.2.2 is K_ε(η) = (2π)^(−d) ∫ exp(ik·η − iω(k)ε) dᵈk. Write ℏω(k) = mc² + ℏ²|k|²/2m − R(k). Then 0 ≤ R(k) ≤ ℏ⁴|k|⁴/(8m³c²), and:*

*(i) The kernel of exp(−imc²ε/ℏ) exp(−iεT̂/ℏ), with T̂ = −(ℏ²/2m)∇², is*

*K_ε^NR(η) = exp(−imc²ε/ℏ) · (m/2πiℏε)^(d/2) · exp(im|η|²/2ℏε).*

*(ii) For every ψ whose Fourier transform is supported in |k| ≤ κ,*

*‖W(ε)ψ − exp(−imc²ε/ℏ) exp(−iεT̂/ℏ)ψ‖ ≤ ε ℏ³κ⁴/(8m³c²) · ‖ψ‖.*

*(iii) For the same ψ, every N, and t = Nε,*

*‖W(ε)ᴺψ − exp(−imc²t/ℏ) exp(−itT̂/ℏ)ψ‖ ≤ t ℏ³κ⁴/(8m³c²) · ‖ψ‖.*

*The bound does not depend on N and tends to zero as c → ∞ at fixed t and κ.*

**Proof.** For x ≥ 0, 0 ≤ 1 + x/2 − (1 + x)^(1/2) ≤ x²/8. With x = ℏ²|k|²/m²c² this is the bound on R. (i) Each Cartesian factor is the Fresnel integral (1/2π)∫exp(ikη − iℏk²ε/2m) dk = (m/2πiℏε)^(1/2) exp(imη²/2ℏε), obtained by completing the square. (ii) The two operators are multiplication by e^(−iω(k)ε) and by e^(−i(mc²/ℏ + ℏ|k|²/2m)ε) in the Fourier representation. Their difference is bounded in modulus by εR(k)/ℏ, since |e^(ia) − e^(ib)| ≤ |a − b|. Plancherel’s theorem gives the norm bound. (iii) The N-th powers are multiplication by e^(−iω(k)Nε) and e^(−i(mc²/ℏ + ℏ|k|²/2m)Nε), whose difference is bounded by NεR(k)/ℏ. ∎

**Remark L.4.2** *(Two computations of one exponent).* The exponent of K_ε^NR is (i/ℏ) · ½m(|η|/ε)² · ε. This is the phase that Proposition L.3 assigns to a straight segment of displacement η traversed in time ε, at velocity η/ε. Lemma L.4.1 reaches it from the dispersion relation of the matter wave. Proposition L.3 reaches it from the accumulated x₄-advance. The modulus (m/2πℏε)^(d/2) of the kernel is the same for every displacement η: all segments enter the sum with equal magnitude, and the normalization is fixed by the Fresnel integral.

**Proposition L.4** *(Feynman path integral as the continuum limit).*

*Let Ĥ = T̂ + V be essentially self-adjoint on D(T̂) ∩ D(V). In the non-relativistic regime of Lemma L.4.1, and apart from the path-independent factor exp(−imc²(t_f − t_i)/ℏ), the propagator of matter is*

*K(q_f, t_f; q_i, t_i) = lim_(N→∞) (m/2πiℏε)^(Nd/2) ∫⋯∫ exp((i/ℏ) Σ_j [½m((x_(j+1) − x_j)/ε)² − V(x_j)] ε) dᵈx₁ ⋯ dᵈx_(N−1) = ∫𝒟x(t) exp(i S[x(t)]/ℏ),*

*with x₀ = q_i, x_N = q_f, ε = (t_f − t_i)/N, and the limit taken in the strong operator sense on L²(ℝᵈ). The order of limits is: c → ∞ at fixed ε first (Lemma L.4.1), then N → ∞. The symbol ∫𝒟x(t) denotes this limit, with 𝒟x(t) = lim (m/2πiℏε)^(Nd/2) dᵈx₁ ⋯ dᵈx_(N−1).*

**Proof.** By Lemma L.2.2(iii) the free propagation over [t_i, t_f] is the N-fold composition of K_ε, and by Lemma L.4.1 each factor is K_ε^NR in the non-relativistic regime. For Ĥ = T̂ + V the Trotter product formula gives exp(−it(T̂ + V)/ℏ) = s-lim (exp(−iεV/ℏ) exp(−iεT̂/ℏ))ᴺ [105, §VIII.8].
