In the position representation exp(−iεV/ℏ) multiplies by exp(−iεV(x_j)/ℏ) at the j-th slice and exp(−iεT̂/ℏ) convolves with the Gaussian kernel of Lemma L.4.1(i). The product of the N factors is the displayed integrand. The exponent is (i/ℏ) times the Riemann sum of the action of Proposition L.3 along the polygonal chain. The Fresnel kernels are not absolutely integrable, so each integral over x_j is taken in the L² sense, as the limit of integrals over bounded regions. ∎

**Scope.** The limit ε → 0 at fixed c is a different limit. It leaves the regime ε ≫ ℏ/mc² in which K_ε is the Gaussian kernel, and its chains obey |x(t) − x(s)| ≤ c|t − s| (§III.2, Scope). An unweighted average over one Sphere of radius cε, (1/4π)∫f(x + cεn)dΩ = f(x) + (c²ε²/6)∇²f(x) + O(ε⁴), has a second moment of order ε² and a generator that vanishes as ε → 0. The Schrödinger generator requires the second moment iℏε/m of Proposition L.5. That moment is supplied by the Compton weight −μ² of the re-expansion events (Lemma L.2.1) and the phase of the matter branch (Lemma L.2.2), and it is computed in Lemma L.4.1.

### III.5 Proposition L.5: The Schrödinger Equation from the Short-Time Propagator

**Proposition L.5** *(Schrödinger equation from short-time Feynman kernel).*

*Expanding the short-time Feynman kernel*

*K_ε(q, q’) = (m/2πiℏε)^{1/2} exp{(iε/ℏ)[½m((q − q’)/ε)² − V(q)]}*

*and evaluating ψ(q, t + ε) = ∫ K_ε(q, q’) ψ(q’, t) dq’ to first order in ε by Gaussian integration yields*

*iℏ ∂ψ/∂t = −(ℏ²/2m) ∂²ψ/∂q² + V(q) ψ,*

*which is the Schrödinger equation.*

**Proof.** The Gaussian integration proceeds in three steps. First, change variables η = q − q’, so that q’ = q − η and the integral over q’ is an integral over η:

*ψ(q, t + ε) = ∫ K_ε(q, q − η) ψ(q − η, t) dη = (m/2πiℏε)^{1/2} ∫ exp{(iε/ℏ)[mη²/(2ε²) − V(q)]} · ψ(q − η, t) dη.*

Second, Taylor-expand ψ(q − η, t) around η = 0 to second order: ψ(q − η, t) ≈ ψ(q, t) − η ∂ψ/∂q + (η²/2) ∂²ψ/∂q². Third, use the Gaussian integrals (m/2πiℏε)^{1/2} ∫ exp(iα η²) dη = 1 for α = m/(2ℏε) and (m/2πiℏε)^{1/2} ∫ η² exp(iα η²) dη = iℏε/m (the latter obtained by differentiating the first with respect to α). The odd moment ∫ η · exp(iα η²) dη = 0 by symmetry. Putting these together:

*ψ(q, t + ε) ≈ exp(−iεV(q)/ℏ) · [ψ(q, t) + (iℏε/(2m)) · ∂²ψ/∂q²]*

*≈ (1 − iεV(q)/ℏ) · ψ(q, t) + (iℏε/(2m)) · ∂²ψ/∂q²*

where in the last step the exponential is expanded to first order in ε. Rearranging:

*ψ(q, t + ε) − ψ(q, t) ≈ −(iε/ℏ) V(q) ψ + (iℏε/(2m)) ∂²ψ/∂q² + O(ε²).*

Dividing by ε and taking ε → 0:

*∂ψ/∂t = −(i/ℏ) V(q) ψ + (iℏ/(2m)) ∂²ψ/∂q².*

Multiplying both sides by iℏ:

*iℏ ∂ψ/∂t = V(q) ψ − (ℏ²/(2m)) ∂²ψ/∂q² = [−(ℏ²/2m) ∂²/∂q² + V(q)] ψ.*

This is the Schrödinger equation with the Hamiltonian Ĥ = −(ℏ²/2m) ∂²/∂q² + V(q). The derivation is also carried out in [115, Corollary 5.2], which establishes that the Feynman path integral derived from the McGucken Principle reproduces the time-dependent Schrödinger equation via the same short-time kernel expansion; [115, Proposition 9.1] supplies the additional content that the first-order time derivative versus second-order spatial derivative asymmetry of the Schrödinger equation is the mathematical expression of a single uniform x₄-expansion producing a diffusive spatial spreading. ∎

**Note.** The Schrödinger equation is reached on the Lagrangian route not as an independent postulate but as the non-relativistic limit of accumulated x₄-phase in the short-time Feynman kernel. The factor iℏ on the left-hand side is the same iℏ that appears in the canonical commutation relation derived through the Hamiltonian route (Proposition H.4); both routes arrive at the same constants through completely different chains.

### III.6 Proposition L.6: The Canonical Commutation Relation from the Second Moment of the Kernel

**McGucken Principle anchor.** On the Lagrangian route the commutator is non-zero because the second moment of the displacement is proportional to the time step: the mean of η² under the kernel is iℏε/m. The paths of the sum are continuous and nowhere differentiable, with (Δq)² of order ℏε/m. That roughness follows from the equal modulus of the kernel at every displacement (Remark L.4.2), and the equal modulus follows from the spherical expansion of x₄ iterated through Propositions L.1 and L.2. Feynman’s 1948 paper obtains the commutation relation from the same second moment of his postulated kernel [7]. What the Principle adds is the origin of the kernel.

**Proposition L.6** *(Canonical commutation relation from the kernel).*

*Let K_ε(q, q′) = (m/2πiℏε)^(1/2) exp(iS_ε(q, q′)/ℏ) be the short-time kernel of Propositions L.4 and L.5 in one dimension, with S_ε(q, q′) = m(q − q′)²/2ε − εV(q). Then:*

*(i) −iℏ ∂K_ε/∂q = (∂S_ε/∂q) K_ε = [m(q − q′)/ε − εV′(q)] K_ε. The operator p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q therefore multiplies the amplitude of each segment by the segment momentum π_ε = m(q − q′)/ε, up to a term of order ε.*

*(ii) For every Schwartz function ψ, ∫ (q − q′) π_ε K_ε(q, q′) ψ(q′) dq′ → iℏ ψ(q) as ε → 0.*

*(iii) [q̂, p̂] = iℏ𝟙.*

**Proof.** (i) Differentiate the exponential. The sign of p̂ is fixed by the sign of the exponent in exp(+iS/ℏ), which Proposition L.3 fixed through the rest phase exp(−iω_C τ). (ii) With η = q − q′, (q − q′)π_ε = mη²/ε. The second-moment integral of Proposition L.5, (m/2πiℏε)^(1/2)∫η² exp(imη²/2ℏε) dη = iℏε/m, together with ψ(q − η) = ψ(q) + O(η) and exp(−iεV/ℏ) = 1 + O(ε), gives (m/ε)(iℏε/m)ψ(q) + O(ε) = iℏψ(q) + O(ε). (iii) With p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q from (i) and q̂ acting by multiplication, (q̂p̂ − p̂q̂)f = −iℏqf′ + iℏ(qf)′ = iℏf for every f in the common domain. This is the proof. Part (ii) is the same statement read in the time-sliced integral, under the standard rule that a factor evaluated at the slice t_j acts as an operator at that time, with later operators to the left: q(t_(j+1)) · π_ε corresponds to q̂p̂, π_ε · q(t_j) corresponds to p̂q̂, and their difference (q(t_(j+1)) − q(t_j)) π_ε = mη²/ε has kernel average iℏ. ∎

**Note.** No proposition of the Hamiltonian route is used. The Hamiltonian route reached p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q from the translation group. The Lagrangian route reaches the same operator from the endpoint derivative of the action in the kernel, and reaches the commutator from the second moment of the kernel.

## IIIA. The Thesis Case by Case: Relativistic Propagation, Spin-½, and Several Particles

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension x₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: dx₄/dt = ic, with x₄ = ict, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

Sections II and III prove the thesis of this paper for the simplest case, and this section states exactly how far each result extends. The thesis is that the Hilbert space, the Hamiltonian formulation with [q̂, p̂] = iℏ𝟙, and the Lagrangian path-integral formulation are theorems of dx₄/dt = ic.

### IIIA.1 What Is Proved for Which Case

**McGucken Principle anchor.** dx₄/dt = ic is one statement about every spacetime point, so its consequences must hold for every kind of matter that the expansion carries. This subsection records, case by case, which of those consequences the present paper proves and which it leaves to the cited corpus papers.

| Case | Hilbert space and Born density | Hamiltonian route (H.1–H.5) | Lagrangian route (L.1–L.6) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. One spinless particle, non-relativistic, in a potential V | Proved (Theorem H.0, Corollaries H.0.3, H.0.4) | Proved | Proved |
| 2. One spinless particle, relativistic, free | Proved (Theorem H.0 uses the exact propagation) | Proved (§IIIA.2) | L.1, L.2, L.3 proved exactly; L.4–L.6 proved in the non-relativistic regime only |
| 3. One spin-½ particle, relativistic, free | Proved (Theorems S.1–S.3, S.6) | Proved (Theorem S.4) | Sphere-chain propagation proved (Theorem S.2); path integral with spin not treated |
| 4. N spinless particles with interaction potential | Proved (Theorem N.1, Corollaries N.2, N.3) | Proved (Theorem N.1) | Proved (Theorem N.1) |
| 5. Fields, with infinitely many degrees of freedom | Not treated here | Not treated here | Not treated here |

Case 1 is complete: every step from the Principle to both formulations is proved in §§II and III. Cases 2 to 4 are proved below to the extent the table states. Case 5 lies outside this paper; the Stone–von Neumann uniqueness of Proposition H.5 does not hold for infinitely many degrees of freedom, and the field-theoretic development is in [108] and [151].

### IIIA.2 Case 2: One Spinless Particle, Relativistic

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Sphere expands at the rate c, and the exact propagation of matter is built from chains of such Spheres (Lemma L.2.1). The results below hold for that exact propagation, at every speed below c.

**Theorem R.1.** *For free matter of mass m with the exact propagation W(t) = e^(−itΩ): (i) Theorem H.0 and Corollary H.0.3 hold as stated. (ii) Propositions H.2 to H.5 hold as stated, and the generator of time translations is Ĥ = ℏΩ = (m²c⁴ + c²|p̂|²)^(1/2). (iii) The Sphere-chain expansion (Lemma L.2.1), the composition law (Lemma L.2.2), and the phase exp(−μX₄) = exp(iS_rel/ℏ) (Proposition L.3) hold exactly.*

**Proof.** (i) Theorem H.0 was proved for W(t) with c finite. (ii) The proofs of H.2 to H.5 use the translation group on ℋ, Stone’s theorem, and the Weyl relation, and none of them uses the form of Ω. By Theorem H.0(vii), W(t) = exp(−itΩ) is the time-translation group, so its generator in the convention exp(−iĤt/ℏ) is ℏΩ, and ℏω(k) = (m²c⁴ + c²ℏ²|k|²)^(1/2). (iii) The three results were proved without approximation. ∎

**Scope.** The kernel of W(ε) for a single time step is not exp(iS_rel/ℏ) evaluated on the straight segment, so the passage from L.3 to the Feynman integral (L.4), the Schrödinger equation (L.5), and the second-moment commutator (L.6) is proved in the non-relativistic regime. On the Hamiltonian route the commutation relation holds at every speed.

### IIIA.3 Case 3: One Spin-½ Particle

**McGucken Principle anchor.** Spin is what first-order propagation inside the McGucken Sphere’s cone requires. A one-component wavefront has first-order propagation only through the non-local Ω, with tails outside the cone. Demanding that the generator be a differential operator, so that the wavefront stays inside the Sphere-chain propagator’s support, forces the Clifford relations, four components, the conserved positive density ψ†ψ, and the angular momentum ℏ/2 carried by the components. The spectrum of H_D is ±ℏω(k): the four components carry both branches e^(−itΩ) and e^(+itΩ) of Lemma L.2.2, the matter branch and its conjugate. The corpus development of the Clifford algebra, the spinor, the double cover, and antimatter as the reversal of x₄-orientation is in [168, Theorems 4.1, 4.2, 9.2, 9.3 and §10]; Theorems S.1 to S.4 are proved here and do not depend on it.

The scalar matter branch ∂ψ/∂t = −iΩψ is first order in time, and Ω is not a differential operator. Its kernel has tails outside the null cone (§III.2, Scope). The question of this subsection is what the Principle requires of matter whose first-order propagation stays inside the McGucken Sphere’s cone. The answer is the Dirac equation and spin-½.

**Theorem S.1** *(Local first-order propagation forces the Clifford relations and four components).*

*Let matter be described by an n-component wavefront ψ with iℏ∂ψ/∂t = Hψ, where (a) H is a differential operator of finite order in x with constant n × n matrix coefficients, (b) H is symmetric on the Schwartz functions with respect to the overlap ∫ φ†ψ d³x, and (c) every component satisfies the matter wave equation, H² = ℏ²Ω² = c²|p̂|² + m²c⁴ with p̂ = −iℏ∇. Then:*

*(i) H = c α·p̂ + βmc², with Hermitian matrices α₁, α₂, α₃, β satisfying α_iα_j + α_jα_i = 2δ_ij 𝟙, α_iβ + βα_i = 0, β² = 𝟙.*

*(ii) n is even and n ≥ 4, and n = 4 is realized. In particular no such H exists for n = 1.*

**Proof.** (i) In the Fourier representation H is a matrix polynomial H(p) that is Hermitian for real p. Let T(p) be its homogeneous part of highest degree d. If d ≥ 2, the part of degree 2d in H(p)² is T(p)², which must vanish because H(p)² has degree 2. A Hermitian matrix with zero square is zero, so T = 0. Hence d ≤ 1 and H(p) = c α·p + B with α_i and B Hermitian. Comparing H(p)² = c²Σ_ij ½(α_i α_j + α_j α_i) p_i p_j + cΣ_i (α_i B + Bα_i) p_i + B² with c²|p|² + m²c⁴ gives the stated relations, with β = B/mc². (ii) From the relations, tr β = tr(βα₁²) = −tr(α₁βα₁) = −tr β, so tr β = 0, and likewise tr α_i = 0. Each matrix squares to 𝟙, so its eigenvalues are ±1 in equal numbers and n is even. For n = 2 the traceless Hermitian matrices form a real space of dimension 3, while four mutually anticommuting invertible matrices are linearly independent: if Σ a_A M_A = 0, the anticommutator with M_B gives 2a_B 𝟙 = 0. So n ≠ 2. For n = 4 the matrices α_i = [[0, σ_i], [σ_i, 0]] and β = [[𝟙, 0], [0, −𝟙]], with σ_i the Pauli matrices, satisfy the relations. For n = 1 three numbers cannot anticommute to 2δ_ij. ∎

**Theorem S.2** *(Hilbert space, conserved overlap, and propagation inside the cone).*

*Let H_D = c α·p̂ + βmc² with n = 4. Then:*

*(i) The wavefronts ψ(t) = exp(−iH_D t/ℏ)f of profiles f in the Schwartz space of four-component functions form, for each t, that same space. The overlap ⟨φ, ψ⟩ = ∫ φ†ψ d³x is sesquilinear, conjugate-symmetric, positive-definite, and conserved, and the completion is the separable Hilbert space ℋ_s ≅ L²(ℝ³; ℂ⁴) ≅ ℋ ⊗ ℂ⁴.*

*(ii) The density ρ = ψ†ψ ≥ 0 and the current j = c ψ†αψ satisfy ∂ρ/∂t + ∇·j = 0.*

*(iii) The retarded Green’s function of iℏ∂/∂t − H_D is S = −(1/ℏ²c²)(iℏ∂/∂t + H_D) G_m, with G_m the Sphere-chain propagator of Lemma L.2.1. It is supported in the closed future null cone. If f vanishes outside a set K, then ψ(t) vanishes outside the set of points within distance c|t| of K.*

**Proof.** (i) In the Fourier representation exp(−iH_D(p)t/ℏ) is a unitary 4 × 4 matrix whose entries, by H_D(p)² = ℏ²ω², are cos(ωt)𝟙 − i sin(ωt)H_D(p)/ℏω, smooth with polynomially bounded derivatives. The argument of Theorem H.0(i), (ii), (iii), (vi) then applies componentwise, with Plancherel’s theorem and unitarity of the matrix giving conservation. (ii) Differentiate ψ†ψ using iℏ∂ψ/∂t = H_Dψ and the Hermiticity of α_i and β: the β terms cancel and the α terms give −c∇·(ψ†αψ). (iii) (iℏ∂_t − H_D)(iℏ∂_t + H_D) = −ℏ²∂_t² − H_D² = −ℏ²c²[(1/c²)∂_t² − ∇² + μ²], so applying iℏ∂_t − H_D to S gives the delta function, and S vanishes for t < 0. A differential operator does not enlarge supports, so S is supported where G_m is. For the Cauchy problem, each component solves the matter wave equation with data ψ(0) = f and ∂ψ/∂t(0) = −(i/ℏ)H_D f, both supported in K, and the solution is (1/c²)[∂_t G_m(t) ∗ f + G_m(t) ∗ ∂ψ/∂t(0)], a spatial convolution with distributions supported in the ball of radius c|t|. ∎

**Theorem S.3** *(Spin-½).*

*Let L = x × p̂ and Σ_k = −(i/2)ε_kij α_iα_j, which in the representation above is Σ_k = [[σ_k, 0], [0, σ_k]]. Then: (i) [H_D, L] = −iℏc α × p̂ and [H_D, (ℏ/2)Σ] = +iℏc α × p̂, so L is not conserved and J = L + (ℏ/2)Σ is. (ii) Each component of (ℏ/2)Σ has the eigenvalues ±ℏ/2. (iii) A rotation by the angle φ about the unit vector n acts on the four components by exp(−iφ n·Σ/2), and the rotation by 2π acts as −𝟙.*

**Proof.** (i) [α·p̂, L_k] = α_i[p̂_i, ε_klm x_l p̂_m] = −iℏ ε_kim α_i p̂_m = −iℏ(α × p̂)_k, and β commutes with L. From the Clifford relations, [α_i, Σ_k] = 2i ε_ikl α_l, so [α·p̂, Σ_k] = 2i(α × p̂)_k, and β commutes with Σ. (ii) Σ_k² = 𝟙 and tr Σ_k = 0. (iii) J generates rotations, and on the components exp(−iφ n·J/ℏ) reduces to exp(−iφ n·Σ/2) = cos(φ/2)𝟙 − i sin(φ/2) n·Σ, which is −𝟙 at φ = 2π. ∎

**Theorem S.4** *(The Hamiltonian route with spin).*

*On ℋ_s, the translations (U(a)ψ)(x) = ψ(x − a) form a strongly continuous unitary group that commutes with exp(−iH_D t/ℏ). Propositions H.2 to H.4 hold componentwise, with [q̂_i, p̂_j] = iℏδ_ij 𝟙. The Weyl relation holds, and by the Stone–von Neumann theorem every strongly continuous unitary representation of it is a direct sum of copies of the Schrödinger representation: ℋ_s ≅ L²(ℝ³) ⊗ ℂ⁴, with the Weyl operators acting on the first factor and the matrices α_i, β, Σ_k on the second.*

**Proof.** The translation multiplies the Fourier transform by e^(−ik·a)𝟙, which commutes with the matrix exp(−iH_D(p)t/ℏ) and is unitary. The proofs of H.2 to H.4 act on each component. The Stone–von Neumann theorem in its general form states that every regular representation is a multiple of the irreducible one [21; 8, Theorem 14.8]. ∎

**Lemma S.5** *(The quadratic density is the only conserved one, for four-component wavefronts).*

*Let F be continuous on [0, ∞) and twice continuously differentiable on (0, ∞), with F(0) = 0 and with F′ and sF″ bounded on bounded sets. Write |ψ| = (ψ†ψ)^(1/2). If the derivative at t = 0 of ∫ F(|ψ(x, t)|) d³x vanishes for every wavefront ψ(t) = exp(−iH_D t/ℏ)f with f smooth and of compact support, then F(s) = κs².*

**Proof.** Put ρ = ψ†ψ and G(ρ) = F(ρ^(1/2)), and K(s) = sG′(s) − G(s), so that K′(s) = sG″(s) and K(0) = 0. Step 1, the derivative. By Theorem S.2(ii), ∂ρ/∂t = −c∇·(ψ†αψ). The difference quotients of F(|ψ|) are dominated by sup|F′| · sup|∂ψ/∂t|, which is integrable. Where f ≠ 0 they converge to G′(ρ)∂ρ/∂t. Where f = 0, also ∂ψ/∂t = −(i/ℏ)H_D f = 0 at t = 0 because H_D is a differential operator, so |ψ(x, t)| is of order t² and the quotient tends to zero. Hence the derivative at t = 0 is −c∫ G′(ρ) ∇·(ψ†αψ) d³x. Step 2, a family of profiles. Let u₊ = (1, 0, 0, 1)/√2 and u₋ = (1, 0, 0, −1)/√2, the eigenvectors of α₁ with eigenvalues +1 and −1. Take f = g(x)[cos χ(x) u₊ + sin χ(x) u₋] with g ≥ 0 smooth of compact support and χ real and smooth. A direct computation with the matrices of Theorem S.1 gives ρ = g² and ψ†αψ = g²(cos 2χ, 0, 0). Step 3, integration by parts. −c∫ G′(g²) ∂₁(g² cos 2χ) d³x = c∫ ∂₁(K(g²)) cos 2χ d³x. The boundary terms vanish because g has compact support and sG′(s) → 0 as s → 0. Step 4, conclusion. If this vanishes for every χ, then ∫ ∂₁(K(g²)) w d³x = 0 for every smooth w with |w| < 1 (take χ = ½ arccos w), so K(g(x)²) does not depend on x₁. It equals K(0) = 0 outside the support of g, so it vanishes everywhere. Multiplying g by constants covers every value s > 0, so K = 0, hence G″ = 0, G(ρ) = κρ, and F(s) = κs². ∎

**Theorem S.6** *(The Born density for spin-½, including rules that renormalize).*

*(i) If the detection density is ρ = F(|ψ|) with F as in Lemma S.5 and its total is conserved by the propagation, then ρ = κψ†ψ.*

*(ii) Consider instead the renormalized rules ρ_F = F(|ψ|) / ∫F(|ψ|) d³x, with F as in Lemma S.5, F ≥ 0 and not identically zero. Impose regional constancy: if at t = 0 the wavefront is f₁ + f₂ with f₁, f₂ smooth, supported in balls B₁ and B₂ whose distance exceeds 2cT, then for |t| < T the probability of detection within distance cT of B₁ does not depend on t. Then F(s) = κs² and ρ_F = ψ†ψ/⟨ψ, ψ⟩.*

*(iii) The density ψ†ψ/⟨ψ, ψ⟩ satisfies regional constancy.*

**Proof.** (i) Lemma S.5. (ii) Write Φ(ψ) = ∫F(|ψ|) d³x. By Theorem S.2(iii), for |t| < T the two pieces ψ₁(t), ψ₂(t) stay within distance cT of B₁ and B₂ and have disjoint supports. Since F(0) = 0, the probability of the first region is Φ(ψ₁(t))/(Φ(ψ₁(t)) + Φ(ψ₂(t))). Its constancy means Φ(ψ₁(t))/Φ(ψ₂(t)) is constant. Φ is invariant under translations, so any two profiles of compact support can be placed in such balls, and the logarithmic derivative Λ(f) of Φ(ψ(t)) at t = 0 is the same number for every profile f of compact support. Let (Θf)(x) = α₁α₃ f^∗(x), with f^∗ the complex conjugate. In the representation of Theorem S.1, α₁, α₃ and β are real and α₂ is imaginary, and the matrix α₁α₃ is unitary, anticommutes with α₁ and α₃, and commutes with α₂ and β. Therefore H_D Θ = Θ H_D, and since Θ is antilinear, exp(−iH_D t/ℏ)Θf = Θ exp(+iH_D t/ℏ)f. Also |Θf| = |f| pointwise. Hence Φ((Θf)(t)) = Φ(f(−t)) and Λ(Θf) = −Λ(f). Since Λ is the same for all profiles, Λ = 0, and Lemma S.5 gives F(s) = κs². (iii) For F(s) = s², Φ(ψ_j(t)) = ⟨ψ_j, ψ_j⟩ is conserved for each piece by Theorem S.2(i). ∎

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Sphere expands at the finite rate c, so the probability of detection in a region can change only when a wavefront crosses the boundary of that region. A rule |ψ|^p/∫|ψ|^p with p ≠ 2 violates this: the probability in one region changes as a distant packet spreads, with nothing passing between them. Theorem S.6 shows that the finite rate of x₄’s expansion, together with the dependence of the density on the modulus of the wavefront, leaves ψ†ψ as the only rule. No bilinearity is assumed. The time-reversal map Θ used in the proof reverses the orientation of the propagation and leaves the modulus unchanged.

**Scope.** Theorems S.1 to S.6 are for a free particle. Lemma S.5 and Theorem S.6 take the density to be a function of ψ†ψ. The path integral for spin-½ is not treated. The representation of the α_i and β is unique up to a change of basis by Pauli’s fundamental theorem, which is used in [168, Theorem 9.2] and is not re-proved here.

### IIIA.4 Case 4: Several Particles

**McGucken Principle anchor.** dx₄/dt = ic holds at every spacetime point for each particle separately. The joint wavefront of several particles is therefore carried forward by the product of their propagations, and each particle’s translations are separately symmetries.

**Theorem N.1** *(N spinless particles).*

*Let particles j = 1, …, N have masses m_j. (i) The joint wavefronts of profiles f in the Schwartz space of ℝ^(3N), propagated by W_N(t) = W₁(t) ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ W_N(t), form that same space for each t; the overlap ∫ φ^∗ψ d^(3N)x is conserved; and the completion is ℋ_N ≅ L²(ℝ^(3N)) ≅ ℋ ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ ℋ. (ii) Translation of particle j alone is a strongly continuous unitary group with generator p̂_j = −iℏ∇_j, and [q̂_(j,a), p̂_(k,b)] = iℏ δ_jk δ_ab 𝟙. The Weyl relations for the 3N degrees of freedom have, up to unitary equivalence, the single irreducible regular representation on L²(ℝ^(3N)). (iii) In the non-relativistic regime, for Ĥ = Σ_j |p̂_j|²/2m_j + V(x₁, …, x_N) essentially self-adjoint on the intersection of the domains, the propagator is the time-sliced integral over chains (x₁(t), …, x_N(t)) of exp(iS/ℏ) with S = ∫(Σ_j ½m_j|v_j|² − V) dt, and its second moments give the commutators of (ii).*

**Proof.** (i) W_N(t) multiplies the Fourier transform on ℝ^(3N) by exp(−it Σ_j ω_j(k_j)), and the proof of Theorem H.0 applies with ℝ³ replaced by ℝ^(3N). L²(ℝ^(3N)) is the Hilbert-space tensor product of N copies of L²(ℝ³). (ii) The Principle is the same at every point for each particle separately, so each particle’s translations are symmetries; the proofs of H.2 to H.5 apply to each coordinate, and operators of different particles act on different variables and commute. The Stone–von Neumann theorem holds for any finite number of degrees of freedom. (iii) The kernel of exp(−iεΣ_j T̂_j/ℏ) is the product of the Fresnel kernels of Lemma L.4.1, the Trotter product formula applies to Σ_j T̂_j + V, and the phase of a chain is the sum of the phases of Proposition L.3 with the potential shifting the rest energy of the configuration. The second-moment computation of Proposition L.6 is carried out in each coordinate, and mixed moments vanish because the kernel is a product. ∎

**Corollary N.2** *(Born density for joint wavefronts).*

*Let F be as in Theorem H.0(v). If ∫ F(|ψ(x₁, …, x_N, t)|) d^(3N)x is independent of t for every joint wavefront under W_N(t), then F(s) = κs². The joint detection density is ρ = |ψ(x₁, …, x_N)|²/⟨ψ, ψ⟩.*

**Proof.** The proof of Lemma H.0.2 applies on ℝ^(3N) with three changes. The main term of the generator is Σ_j (c/2μ_j)(−∇_j²), and the remainder multiplier Σ_j r_j(k_j) obeys the same bounds. The weight in Step 4 is (1 + |x|²/λ²)^s with an integer s > 3N/4, and the main term then scales as λ^(3N−2) against a remainder of order λ^(3N−4). In Step 5 the function H(g) is annihilated in the weak sense by the constant-coefficient elliptic operator Σ_j (1/μ_j)∇_j², which becomes the Laplacian in the coordinates μ_j^(1/2) x_j, and the maximum principle applies. ∎

**Corollary N.3** *(The Born rule for an observable measured by a position record).*

*Let a system have Hilbert space ℋ_S and let Â be a self-adjoint operator on ℋ_S with discrete spectrum and eigenprojectors P_n. Let a pointer particle have the normalized wavefront φ₀ ∈ ℋ. Suppose the measurement interaction is a unitary U on ℋ_S ⊗ ℋ with U(χ ⊗ φ₀) = Σ_n (P_n χ) ⊗ φ_n for every χ, where the φ_n are normalized and supported in pairwise disjoint regions D_n of the pointer’s position. Then for a normalized χ the probability that the pointer is detected in D_n is*

*P(a_n) = ‖P_n χ‖², which is |⟨a_n|χ⟩|² when the eigenvalue a_n is non-degenerate.*

**Proof.** The joint wavefront after the interaction is Ψ = Σ_n (P_n χ) ⊗ φ_n. By Corollary N.2 (and Theorem S.6 when the system carries spin) the joint density is |Ψ|². Integrating over the system variables and over the pointer position in D_n, only the n-th term contributes, because the φ_n have disjoint supports, and the result is ‖P_n χ‖² ‖φ_n‖² = ‖P_n χ‖². ∎

**McGucken Principle anchor.** Every measurement record is a position record: a pointer, a track, a spot on a screen. Corollary N.3 shows that the probability rule for an arbitrary observable is the position rule of Theorem H.0 applied to the wavefront of the pointer. The corpus statement is [169, Corollary 11.3], posted in [170], whose statement reads: “Let Â be a self-adjoint observable with spectral decomposition Â = ∑_n a_n |a_n⟩⟨a_n| (for the discrete case; the continuous case extends analogously via the spectral measure). Let ψ be a normalized state. Then the probability of obtaining outcome a_n on measurement is P(a_n) = |⟨a_n|ψ⟩|².”

**Scope.** Corollary N.3 assumes the measurement interaction has the stated form. It does not derive that a single outcome occurs.

**Scope.** Theorem N.1 is for distinguishable spinless particles. The joint space contains entangled wavefronts, those that are not products; their correlations are the subject of §V.8. The restriction to symmetric or antisymmetric wavefronts for identical particles is not derived here.

## IV. Comparison of the Two Routes

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension x₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: dx₄/dt = ic, with x₄ = ict, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

The two derivations of §§II–III reach the same algebraic identity [q̂, p̂] = iℏ from the same starting principle dx₄/dt = ic, through completely different intermediate structures. This section catalogs the agreements and disagreements between the two routes in detail, identifies what each route uniquely illuminates, and establishes the basis for the overdetermination principle developed in §VII.

### IV.1 Point-by-Point Comparison

The comparison table below summarizes the content of the two routes step by step. Each row identifies an element; each column identifies which route uses it and how.

| Element     | Hamiltonian route (§II)                                                                  | Lagrangian route (§III)                                                                                          |
|------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Starting principle     | dx₄/dt = ic                                                                              | dx₄/dt = ic                                                                                                      |
| First intermediate     | Minkowski metric ds² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² − c² dt² from x₄ = ict (Prop. H.1)             | Huygens’ principle: the McGucken Sphere M⁺_p₀(t) is the secondary wavelet (Prop. L.1)                                    |
| Second intermediate    | Stone’s theorem on translation group: U(a) = exp(−ia p̂/ℏ) (Prop. H.2)                    | Iterated Huygens: Sphere-chain expansion and time-slice composition give the sum over all paths (Prop. L.2)                   |
| Third intermediate     | Configuration representation: p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q by direct differentiation of U(a) (Prop. H.3) | Accumulated x₄-advance along path: exp(−μX₄) = exp(iS/ℏ) in the non-relativistic regime (Prop. L.3) |
| Fourth intermediate    | Commutator [q̂, p̂] = iℏ by direct three-line computation (Prop. H.4)                    | Full Feynman path integral K = ∫𝒟x exp(iS/ℏ) as continuum limit (Prop. L.4)                                      |
| Fifth intermediate     | Stone-von Neumann uniqueness of the Schrödinger representation (Prop. H.5)               | Schrödinger equation iℏ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ from Gaussian integration of short-time propagator (Prop. L.5)                 |
| Sixth intermediate     | (not needed; route closes at fifth step)                                                 | Canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ from the second moment of the kernel (Prop. L.6)                                  |
| Final destination      | [q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙                                                                          | [q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙                                                                                                  |
| Where i enters         | Minkowski signature → unitary exponent → momentum operator                               | Accumulated x₄-oscillation phase → path-integral weight → Schrödinger equation                                   |
| Where ℏ enters         | Action per radian of the x₄-cycle (h per cycle) scale of unitary representation                                      | Action per radian of the x₄-cycle (h per cycle) in denominator of path-integral exponent                                                     |
| Mathematical machinery | Completion of wavefronts (Theorem H.0), Stone’s theorem, direct differentiation, Stone–von Neumann theorem                       | Laplace–Fourier transform, Neumann series, Fresnel integration, Trotter product formula, Taylor expansion                                |
| Geometry used | Perpendicularity of x₄ (i² = −1), translation invariance                                 | Spherical symmetry of x₄’s expansion, Compton-frequency coupling                                                 |
| Dual-channel aspect    | Algebraic-symmetry channel of dx₄/dt = ic                                                | Geometric-propagation channel of dx₄/dt = ic                                                                     |

The first and last rows show where the routes start and end together. Every row in between shows a difference in method; §IV.2 states exactly what the routes share. The routes use disjoint mathematical machinery (Stone’s theorem versus Huygens convolution; direct commutator computation versus Gaussian integration of Feynman kernels; Stone-von Neumann versus Schrödinger derivation). They pass through disjoint intermediate structures (Minkowski metric versus Huygens’ principle; unitary translation generator versus path-integral weight; direct algebra versus integral representation). They use disjoint content of the starting principle (algebraic symmetry in the Hamiltonian route; geometric propagation in the Lagrangian route).

### IV.2 The Derivational Disjointness of the Two Routes

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The one Principle dx₄/dt = ic states both the perpendicularity of x₄ (the i) and its spherical expansion (the Sphere). The Hamiltonian route unpacks the first through the dx₄/dt = ic Algebraic Channel and the Lagrangian route unpacks the second through the dx₄/dt = ic Geometric Channel. This subsection states exactly what the two routes share and what they do not.

The claim of this section is exact. No proposition among H.1 to H.5 is used in the proof of any of L.1 to L.6, and none of L.1 to L.6 is used in the proof of any of H.1 to H.5. The Hamiltonian route uses Stone’s theorem, differentiation of the translation group, the Weyl relation, and the Stone–von Neumann theorem. The Lagrangian route uses the retarded Green’s function of the Sphere, the Sphere-chain expansion, the composition of the matter branch, the accumulated x₄-advance, the Fresnel kernel, the Trotter product formula, and the second moment of the kernel. None of the first list appears in §III, and none of the second appears in §§II.2 to II.5.

The two routes share four things, and the paper states them. (1) The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic, with the action h in it. (2) The Minkowski interval of Proposition H.1: the Hamiltonian route uses it for the four-component generator of Lemma H.3.1, and the Lagrangian route uses it for the time-dilation factor c/γ of Proposition L.3. (3) The Hilbert space ℋ of Theorem H.0, the completion of the matter wavefronts W(t)f under their overlap. The propagation W is the matter branch of Lemma L.2.2, so the arena of the Hamiltonian route is built from the dx₄/dt = ic Geometric Channel. The Lagrangian route propagates amplitudes in the same space. (4) The final identity [q̂, p̂] = iℏ𝟙.

The momentum operator p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q is reached twice by different arguments. On the Hamiltonian route it is the derivative of the translation group at the identity (Proposition H.3). On the Lagrangian route it is the operator that multiplies the kernel by the endpoint derivative of the action, ∂S_ε/∂q (Proposition L.6(i)). The commutator is likewise reached twice: by differentiation of a product on the Hamiltonian route (Proposition H.4), and from the second moment of the kernel on the Lagrangian route (Proposition L.6(ii) and (iii)). Huygens’ principle, the sum over paths, and the kernel are absent from §II. Stone’s theorem and the Weyl relation are absent from §III.

### IV.3 What Each Route Uniquely Illuminates

Although the two routes reach the same destination, each route illuminates features of the canonical commutation relation that the other route leaves implicit.

**The Hamiltonian route** emphasizes the *algebraic-symmetry* content of the commutation relation. [q̂, p̂] = iℏ is derived through Stone’s theorem on spatial-translation unitary representations, and the Stone-von Neumann closure establishes that the representation is unique up to unitary equivalence. The commutation relation’s algebraic structure, its relationship to the Heisenberg Weyl group, its uniqueness up to unitary equivalence, and its direct derivation from symmetry — all of these are foregrounded by the Hamiltonian route. The route reveals the commutation relation’s character as an algebraic consequence of translation invariance on a Hilbert space.

**The Lagrangian route** emphasizes the *geometric-propagation* content of the commutation relation. [q̂, p̂] = iℏ is derived through Huygens’ principle on spacetime, iterated spherical expansion of x₄, and the non-relativistic limit of accumulated path phase. The commutation relation’s origin in the phase structure of matter coupled to x₄’s oscillation at the Compton frequency, its emergence through the path integral’s Gaussian integration, and its connection to the geometric propagation of wavefronts in spacetime — all of these are foregrounded by the Lagrangian route. The route reveals the commutation relation’s character as a geometric consequence of spherical expansion in spacetime.

Both characterizations are correct. The commutation relation is *both* an algebraic consequence of translation invariance *and* a geometric consequence of spherical expansion. The two routes are two proofs of the same theorem from two different vantage points, and each proof reveals an aspect of the theorem that the other proof leaves implicit.

### IV.4 Agreement on the Constants

The two routes agree on the factor i and the factor ℏ that appear in the commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ, and they agree on the physical origin of both factors.

**The factor i** on the Hamiltonian route enters at Proposition H.1 as the i in x₄ = ict (the perpendicularity marker of x₄), propagates through the Minkowski metric signature, enters the unitary exponential of Stone’s theorem as the i in U(a) = exp(−ia p̂/ℏ), enters the momentum operator p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q as the same i, and appears in the commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ by direct computation. On the Lagrangian route, the factor i enters at Proposition L.3 as the i in the accumulated x₄-phase exp(−iω_C τ) from the Compton-frequency coupling (which itself carries the i from x₄ = ict), propagates through the full path integral weight exp(iS/ℏ), appears in the Schrödinger equation iℏ ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ, and appears in the commutation relation through the second moment of the kernel. Both routes trace the i to the same geometric fact: the perpendicularity of x₄ to the three spatial dimensions, encoded algebraically as the imaginary unit i.

**The factor ℏ** on both routes traces to the same physical origin: the quantum of action per radian of the x₄-cycle (h per cycle) at the Planck frequency f_P, as established in [117, §V]. On the Hamiltonian route, ℏ appears in the unitary exponential as the natural scale of the representation (determined by Stone’s theorem with a scale parameter, fixed physically by the requirement that U(a) agree with the physical translation at scale a in units where the Planck action is ℏ). On the Lagrangian route, ℏ appears in the path-integral weight as the scale of the Compton-frequency coupling ω_C = mc²/ℏ in the accumulated phase, which is directly the Planck-action scale. Both routes identify ℏ with the same physical quantity: the action per radian of the x₄-cycle (h per cycle) at the Planck scale.

### IV.5 Summary of the Comparison

The two routes agree on the starting principle (dx₄/dt = ic), on the final destination ([q̂, p̂] = iℏ), and on the physical origins of the factors i and ℏ in that destination. They differ in every theorem used between start and end (§IV.2). The disjointness of intermediate structure combined with the agreement on start and end points is the precise signature of a correct physical foundation developed in §VII: when two independent chains from a single principle reach the same algebraic identity through disjoint routes, the identity is not an artifact of derivational choice but a genuine consequence of the principle, and each chain confirms the identity by a disjoint body of evidence.

## V. Why dx₄/dt = ic Has This Property

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension x₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: dx₄/dt = ic, with x₄ = ict, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

The two-route derivation of §§II–III establishes that both the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian formulations of quantum mechanics [163] descend as theorems from dx₄/dt = ic. This section addresses the deeper question: *why does this principle, specifically, possess this property?* The answer developed here is that dx₄/dt = ic is a principle with *dual-channel content* — a single geometric statement that simultaneously specifies two logically distinct pieces of information, each of which drives one of the two routes. No prior proposed foundation for quantum mechanics has had this dual-channel content, and the principle’s possession of it is what makes the two-route derivation possible.

### V.1 The Two Channels Identified

The geometric statement *“dx₄/dt = ic”* combined with the physical interpretation *“x₄ advances at the velocity of light from every spacetime point, spherically symmetrically about each point”* contains two logically distinct pieces of information. The decomposition of the principle into its two components can be stated as follows.

**The dx₄/dt = ic Algebraic Channel (the Algebraic Channel).** The principle specifies that x₄’s advance has a *uniform rate* ic that is *invariant* under spacetime isometries: the advance rate is the same at every spacetime point (translation invariance), independent of direction in the three spatial dimensions (rotation invariance), and form-invariant under Lorentz boost (Lorentz invariance). These invariances generate the Poincaré-group symmetries of the Minkowski spacetime derived in Proposition III.1 of [108] and the ten Poincaré conservation laws derived in [112]. The channel’s content — uniformity plus invariance — is precisely the content needed to apply Stone’s theorem to the unitary representations of the spacetime symmetry group, which drives the Hamiltonian route of §II. The factor i in the unitary generators, the factor ℏ as the scale of the representation, the canonical commutation relation as a consequence of translation invariance — all of these are consequences of the algebraic-symmetry channel.

**The dx₄/dt = ic Geometric Channel (the Geometric Channel).** The principle specifies that x₄’s advance is *spherically symmetric* about every spacetime point — the advance at rate c radiates equally into all three-dimensional directions from each point of emission. This spherical symmetry generates the McGucken Sphere of [118, Part IV], which is Huygens’ secondary wavelet and whose successive Spheres sweep out the future null cone of Minkowski spacetime. The channel’s content — spherical emission from every point with radial rate c — is precisely the content needed to generate Huygens’ principle as a theorem (Proposition L.1) and to iterate the Huygens expansion into a sum over paths (Proposition L.2), which drives the Lagrangian route of §III. The Feynman path integral as a sum over chains of McGucken Spheres, the accumulated x₄-phase along each path, the Schrödinger equation from Gaussian integration — all of these are consequences of the geometric-propagation channel.

Both channels are present in the principle’s single geometric statement. “x₄ advances at ic from every point” is the algebraic symmetry (uniform rate, invariance under spacetime translations). “Spherically symmetrically about each point” is the geometric propagation (isotropic wavefront expansion, Huygens’ secondary wavelets). The two channels are *simultaneously valid readings*, each unpacking a different aspect of the same geometric fact.

### V.2 Why Both Channels Are Needed

The two quantum formulations require different content from the foundation. The Hamiltonian formulation requires algebraic symmetry: a Hilbert space carrying unitary representations of a translation group, with Stone’s theorem generating the momentum operator. The Lagrangian formulation requires geometric propagation: a wavefront structure on spacetime, with Huygens’ secondary wavelets generating the path-summation representation. A foundation that provides only one of these contents can generate only one of the quantum formulations (as derived result); it can reach the other formulation only through the equivalence theorems established after the fact (Feynman 1948, Stone-von Neumann 1931-1932), but not as an independent derivation from the foundation itself.

A foundation with *both* channels can generate both formulations as independent derivations. dx₄/dt = ic is such a foundation. Its algebraic symmetry generates the Hamiltonian route; its geometric propagation generates the Lagrangian route. The derivations are independent — disjoint intermediate structures, disjoint mathematical machinery — because they use disjoint contents of the principle.

### V.3 The Unique Dual-Channel Property

The uniqueness claim of this section is as follows: dx₄/dt = ic is the only known foundation for quantum mechanics whose single geometric statement contains both channels simultaneously. This section develops the claim through a systematic analysis of proposed foundations and their channel structure.

**Classical Lagrangian variational mechanics.** The principle of least action δS = 0 combined with a classical Lagrangian L(q, q̇, t) generates Newton’s equations through the Euler-Lagrange equations. This is a variational principle with propagation (paths extremize the action), but it does not generate a symmetry structure on a Hilbert space — the quantum operator formalism is not derived, and the commutation relations are not consequences of the principle. Classical Lagrangian mechanics has content analogous to the Geometric Channel (propagation) but lacks the algebraic symmetry of the Algebraic Channel. To quantize classical Lagrangian mechanics, additional structure (canonical quantization, or geometric quantization, or path-integral quantization) must be imposed. The principle of least action does not by itself generate quantum mechanics.

**Classical symplectic geometry.** A phase space (M, ω) with symplectic form ω supports Hamiltonian dynamics through the vector field X_H defined by ω(X_H, ·) = dH, for a Hamiltonian function H: M → ℝ. This is an algebraic structure on phase space that supports the Hamiltonian formulation of classical mechanics (Poisson brackets, canonical transformations, phase flow). Geometric quantization [13; 19; 26] constructs a Hilbert space and quantum operators from (M, ω) through prequantization (a line bundle with curvature ω), polarization (a foliation selecting half the coordinates), and metaplectic correction. This generates the Hamiltonian operator formulation with the factor i in the commutators and the factor ℏ as the scale of the prequantization line bundle’s curvature. Classical symplectic geometry has content analogous to the Algebraic Channel (algebraic-symmetry, through the symplectic form’s algebraic structure) but lacks the geometric propagation of the Geometric Channel — the path integral does not emerge naturally from symplectic geometry; it must be constructed separately (the Feynman-Kac formula from stochastic analysis, or a path-integral quantization applied after the fact). Geometric quantization reaches the Hamiltonian formulation but does not reach the Lagrangian formulation as an independent derivation; both formulations are equivalent at the end (by Stone-von Neumann and by Feynman’s proof that the path integral reproduces the Schrödinger equation), but only one is derived from geometric quantization’s starting point.

**Stochastic dynamics (Nelson, Lindgren-Liukkonen).** Stochastic mechanics postulates that particles undergo a classical-mechanical motion with superimposed Brownian-like fluctuations whose diffusion coefficient is ℏ/(2m). From the resulting drift-diffusion equations, the Schrödinger equation is derived. Lindgren-Liukkonen’s stochastic optimal control requires a Lorentz-invariant stochastic action, which forces the Lagrangian to be imaginary (√(det g) = i in Minkowski signature) and the noise variance to be imaginary (σ² = i/m). This generates the Schrödinger equation through a route analogous to the Geometric Channel (propagation, via stochastic fluctuations), but does not generate the operator formalism as an independent derivation. The stochastic frameworks have partial Geometric Channel content but lack the Algebraic Channel; they reach the Schrödinger equation, but they do not separately derive the canonical commutation relation or the Hilbert-space representation structure. Moreover, Nelson’s framework takes ℏ as an input to the diffusion coefficient rather than deriving it, and Lindgren-Liukkonen takes the analytic continuation (the i) as a mathematical requirement of Lorentz invariance rather than identifying it with a physical fact.

**Geometric algebra (Hestenes).** Hestenes’s spacetime algebra reinterprets i as a bivector iσ₃ = γ₂γ₁ in Cl(1,3), giving i a static geometric identity. The Dirac equation, the canonical commutation relation, and the Schrödinger equation can all be reformulated in Hestenes’s algebra. But geometric algebra is a reinterpretation of existing physics, not a derivation of quantum mechanics from a deeper principle: the Dirac equation, the canonical commutation relation, and the Schrödinger equation are inputs to be reformulated rather than outputs to be derived. Hestenes’s framework has static Geometric-Channel-like content (geometric identity of i) but lacks a dynamical principle that would generate either Algebraic Channel or the Geometric Channel from deeper structure.

**Trace dynamics (Adler).** Adler’s program derives quantum mechanics as a statistical thermodynamic average of a deeper classical matrix dynamics. The canonical commutation relation emerges as a Ward identity of the canonical ensemble’s equipartition theorem: ⟨C̃⟩ = iℏ𝟙. This has partial Algebraic Channel content (algebraic structure, inherited from the matrix dynamics) but the i is taken as built into the matrix algebra rather than derived, and ℏ emerges as a temperature-like parameter of the equilibrium rather than as a geometric quantum of action. Trace dynamics does not generate the path integral as an independent derivation; it is one-directional from matrix dynamics to quantum mechanics via statistical averaging.

**Cellular automata (‘t Hooft).** ‘t Hooft proposes that quantum mechanics emerges from a deterministic cellular automaton at the Planck scale. The Schrödinger equation and canonical commutation relation are emergent features of the automaton-to-quantum mapping. This has neither Algebraic Channel nor the Geometric Channel in the sense developed here: the principle is discrete-determinism rather than continuous-geometric, and the i and ℏ of quantum mechanics are emergent features rather than derivations from the underlying structure.

**Bohmian mechanics.** Bohm’s hidden-variable formulation supplements the Schrödinger equation with a guiding equation for particle trajectories. Both the Schrödinger equation and the guiding equation are postulates; Bohmian mechanics is an interpretation of existing quantum mechanics, not a derivation from a deeper principle.

**Loop quantum gravity (Ashtekar and successors).** Ashtekar’s formulation of general relativity [164] in terms of connection variables provides a Hamiltonian formulation of gravity whose canonical quantization generates the spin-network structure of loop quantum gravity. This is a Hamiltonian-formulation derivation within gravity but does not generate the Lagrangian path integral as an independent derivation; moreover, the starting point (Ashtekar’s connection variables) is itself a classical Hamiltonian reformulation of general relativity rather than a deeper geometric principle that would generate both formulations independently.

**Twistor theory (Penrose and Witten).** Penrose’s twistor theory and Witten’s twistor string reformulate spacetime physics in terms of the complex projective space CP³. This is a geometric reformulation with elegant mathematical properties (Proposition X.1 of [119]), but twistor theory does not by itself generate the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian formulations of quantum mechanics from a principle; the quantum mechanics is imported from standard physics and reformulated in twistor variables.

**Constructive gravity (Schuller).** Schuller’s 2020 constructive-gravity programme [arXiv:2003.09726] shows that given the matter Lagrangians’ principal polynomials, the compatible gravitational dynamics is uniquely determined as the Einstein-Hilbert action. This is a *consequence* derivation — given matter, determine gravity uniquely — which presupposes the matter Lagrangians already in Lagrangian form. Schuller’s programme has partial Geometric Channel content (propagation structure determined by principal polynomials) but does not generate the quantum-mechanical Hamiltonian formulation as an independent derivation; the canonical commutation relation and operator algebra are inputs from quantum-mechanical matter Lagrangians rather than outputs from the constructive-gravity starting point.

**Weinberg’s Lagrangian QFT.** Weinberg’s derivation requires the S-matrix to be Lorentz-invariant and to satisfy cluster decomposition, from which he derives the necessity of quantum fields (as cluster-decomposition-compatible Fourier modes of creation/annihilation operators) and the Lagrangian formalism as the natural generator of Lorentz-invariant S-matrix elements. This presupposes the operator formalism (commutation relations for bosons, anticommutation for fermions, unitary representation of the Poincaré group) and derives the Lagrangian on top of it. Weinberg’s approach is *one-directional*: from operator formalism to Lagrangian, not from a common foundation to both. Moreover, Lorentz invariance and cluster decomposition are themselves physical postulates.

The pattern across these proposed foundations is that each possesses partial content matching one channel but not the other, or possesses neither channel but instead a different structure (emergent, interpretational, reinterpretational). No prior proposed foundation for quantum mechanics possesses both channels simultaneously in a single geometric-dynamical statement.

### V.4 Why dx₄/dt = ic Is the Unique Dual-Channel Foundation

The uniqueness of dx₄/dt = ic among proposed quantum foundations — in possessing both channels simultaneously — is not accidental. It reflects the specific structure of the geometric statement: *a physical axis advancing at the velocity of light, uniformly from every point, spherically symmetrically.*

The uniformity of the advance (rate ic at every point, invariant under spacetime translations and Lorentz boosts) is the algebraic symmetry. The spherical symmetry of the advance (isotropic expansion from each point, generating Huygens’ secondary wavelets at each point) is the geometric propagation. The two contents are logically independent — one can imagine (pathologically) a principle with uniform-rate non-spherical advance, or spherical advance at non-uniform rate — but they are both required components of dx₄/dt = ic as a physical principle describing the dynamics of a real geometric axis. The reason the principle has both contents is that real physical axes in three-dimensional space, when they advance, must advance *from every point* (uniformity) *in every direction* (isotropy), because space is homogeneous and isotropic. The dual-channel content of dx₄/dt = ic is therefore *forced by the minimal physical interpretation of the mathematical statement*: a geometric axis advancing at rate ic must advance uniformly and spherically, and these are the two channels.

This is why no prior proposed foundation has the dual-channel property. Classical symplectic geometry is an algebraic structure on phase space; it has no “advance” of any kind, so it cannot have the spherical-propagation. Stochastic dynamics is a time-evolution structure on configuration space; it has propagation but no symmetry structure built into its starting postulate. Geometric algebra is a static reformulation of Minkowski spacetime; it has algebraic structure but no dynamics. ‘t Hooft’s cellular automaton is a discrete-determinism structure; it has neither continuous propagation nor continuous symmetry content.

dx₄/dt = ic is the first foundation for quantum mechanics whose statement is both *geometric* (specifying a real physical axis in spacetime) and *dynamical* (specifying the advance of that axis at a definite rate), with the minimal physical interpretation of that statement requiring both channels simultaneously. It is the first foundation whose dual-channel content makes the two-route derivation possible.

### V.5 The Compression of Physics

The dual-channel structure identified here has a deeper implication for the foundational structure of physics. The Hamiltonian and Lagrangian formulations of quantum mechanics are the two distinct algebraic expressions of the two distinct informational channels of a single underlying geometric fact. When the standard textbook presents them as alternatives, it obscures the fact that they are theorems of one principle. When the McGucken Principle presents both as consequences, it reveals the fact that they are shadows of a single deeper structure cast in two different mathematical languages.

This is the compression of physics that a correct foundation accomplishes. Two formulations collapse to one principle. Every factor of i and every factor of ℏ in both formulations collapses to a single geometric fact: the perpendicularity and the oscillation-scale of x₄’s advance. The operator formalism and the path-integral formalism collapse to two readings of one equation. Einstein’s standard — “the more impressive the greater the simplicity of its premises, the more different kinds of things it relates, and the more extended is its area of applicability” — is met: one equation dx₄/dt = ic relates the full algebra of the operator formulation, the full propagation of the path-integral formulation, and every derived structure of quantum mechanics from the Schrödinger equation to the canonical commutation relation to the uncertainty principle to the Born rule to the Dirac equation. The simplicity of the premise and the range of applicability stand in the relation Einstein identified as the mark of a correct theory.

### V.6 Wave/Particle Duality as the Dual-Channel Reading at the Ontological Level

The dual-channel analysis of §V.1 was developed to explain why the McGucken Principle generates both the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian *formulations* of quantum mechanics. The same analysis applies, with a single shift in focus, to explain the phenomenon that has served as the central philosophical puzzle of quantum mechanics for the past century: *wave/particle duality*.

#### V.6.1 The Century-Old Puzzle

Wave/particle duality is the empirical observation that quantum objects — photons, electrons, neutrons, atoms, and in modern experiments molecules up to C₆₀ and larger — simultaneously exhibit wave-like behavior (interference in double-slit geometries, diffraction through apertures, coherent superposition) and particle-like behavior (localized detection events, discrete energy and momentum exchange, quantized absorption and emission). The duality was identified in its modern form by Einstein in his 1905 photoelectric paper [27], which established that light exhibits particle-like energy quantization E = hν even though Young’s 1801 double-slit experiment had established its wave-like interference. It was extended to matter by de Broglie’s 1924 doctoral dissertation [28], which postulated that every particle of mass m and momentum p has an associated wavelength λ = h/p — the *de Broglie relation* — and was confirmed experimentally by the Davisson-Germer 1927 electron-diffraction observations [30] and the Thomson 1928 electron-diffraction images [31].

The theoretical response to the duality across the century since de Broglie has been, almost uniformly, to treat it as a *puzzle to be interpreted*. Bohr’s 1927 Como lecture [41] introduced the *principle of complementarity*: wave and particle aspects are *incompatible* descriptions applying in mutually exclusive experimental contexts, and a complete description of any quantum experiment requires specifying which context is realized. Heisenberg’s 1927 uncertainty relation [40] supplied the quantitative complement to complementarity: sharp position (particle aspect) and sharp momentum (wave aspect) cannot be simultaneously specified, with the trade-off Δx·Δp ≥ ℏ/2. Born’s 1926 probabilistic interpretation [39] mediated between the two aspects by treating the wavefunction’s squared modulus as the probability density for particle localization, so that the wave evolves deterministically until measurement forces a particle-like outcome.

Alternative interpretations arose in response to the perceived inadequacy of Copenhagen’s “complementarity” as an explanation: de Broglie’s pilot-wave interpretation [29], developed by Bohm [4] and extended by Dürr-Goldstein-Zanghì [53] and others, treats particles as guided by a physically real wavefunction; Everett’s relative-state formulation [44], developed into the many-worlds interpretation by DeWitt [45] and Wallace [46], treats the wavefunction as physically real with particle-like localization corresponding to branches of the universal wavefunction; Wheeler’s delayed-choice experiments [47; 48], realized experimentally by Jacques et al. [49], sharpen the puzzle by showing that the decision to treat a photon as “particle” or “wave” can be made after it has already traversed the apparatus; Scully and Drühl’s quantum-eraser analysis [50], realized experimentally by Kim et al. [51] and Walborn et al. [52], further sharpens the puzzle by showing that “which-path” information can be erased after the fact, restoring interference.

Modern experimental work has established the duality in ever more exotic regimes. Zeilinger’s program has observed interference for neutrons [54; 55], atoms [56; 57], C₆₀ and C₇₀ fullerenes [58; 59], phthalocyanine [60], and even larger molecules up to ~2000 atomic mass units [61]. In every such experiment, the quantum object behaves as a wave (producing an interference pattern) when propagating through the apparatus *and* as a particle (producing a discrete detection event at a specific pixel of the detector) when observed. The duality is universal, not restricted to “pure” photons or electrons; it is a feature of quantum mechanics at every mass scale accessible to experiment.

Across the century of theoretical engagement with wave/particle duality, no prior framework has dissolved the puzzle — each has interpreted, reframed, or accepted the duality as an irreducible feature of quantum reality, but none has explained *why* quantum objects must simultaneously exhibit both aspects, and none has derived the two aspects as simultaneous consequences of a single deeper geometric principle.

#### V.6.2 The Dual-Channel Reading

Under the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic, wave/particle duality is the direct consequence of the Principle’s dual-channel content, applied at the ontological level.

**The Geometric Channel generates the wave aspect.** The spherical symmetry of x₄’s expansion from every spacetime point is, by Proposition L.1, Huygens’ principle — every spacetime point is the center of a secondary wavelet, and iterated Huygens composition (Proposition L.2) generates wave-front propagation through spacetime. The interference patterns observed in the double-slit experiment are the constructive and destructive superposition of these Huygens wavelets from the two slits. The diffraction patterns observed in single-slit geometries are the same Huygens wavelets expanded from each point of the slit aperture. The matter-wave wavelength λ_dB = h/p observed in Davisson-Germer, Thomson, and all subsequent matter-wave experiments is, by the derivation of [109, Theorem 4], the x₄-phase accumulation rate of matter per unit of spatial motion — the Compton-frequency coupling of the Geometric Channel producing oscillatory wavefronts with the specific wavelength that de Broglie postulated in 1924. The wave aspect of quantum objects is *entirely* the Geometric Channel reading of dx₄/dt = ic: propagating wavefronts produced by iterated x₄-sphere expansion from every spacetime point.

**The Algebraic Channel generates the particle aspect.** The invariance of x₄’s advance under spacetime translations is, by Proposition H.2, Stone’s theorem applied to the translation group — and the self-adjoint generators of those translations are, by Proposition H.3, the four-momentum operators p̂^μ whose eigenvalues are the localized four-momenta of particle states. The discrete detection events observed at specific pixels of the detector screen are eigenvalue events of the position observable q̂ — sharp eigenvalues at localized spacetime points. The quantized energy and momentum exchanges observed in the photoelectric effect (Einstein 1905), Compton scattering (Compton 1923), and every other “particle-like” process are the eigenvalue exchanges of the Algebraic Channel’s algebraic observables: discrete values of energy and momentum conserved in individual scattering events. The Heisenberg uncertainty relation Δx·Δp ≥ ℏ/2 — the quantitative expression of wave/particle complementarity — is, by [110], a theorem about the Fourier-dual structure of the x₄-phase whose algebra is the Algebraic Channel and whose propagation is the Geometric Channel. The particle aspect of quantum objects is *entirely* the Algebraic Channel reading of dx₄/dt = ic: localized eigenvalue structure generated by the algebraic symmetry of x₄’s advance. Position on ℝ has continuous spectrum, so the localized detection event also uses the measurement and probability results of [149], which this section cites and does not re-derive.

A photon traveling through a double-slit apparatus *does* both simultaneously. Its the Geometric Channel is the spherical Huygens wavelets emanating from every spacetime point the photon’s wavefront reaches — including both slits, producing the interference pattern on the screen. Its the Algebraic Channel is the localized detection event at a specific screen pixel — the eigenvalue of the position observable at the moment of detection. Both are real; both are simultaneous; both are consequences of the same dx₄/dt = ic. There is no contradiction because the two readings are two simultaneous readings of one geometric principle, corresponding to two distinct informational contents present in the principle’s statement.

Applied systematically, the dual-channel reading resolves each of the classical puzzles of wave/particle duality.

**The double-slit puzzle.** Why does the interference pattern require both slits to be open? The Geometric Channel reading: because the Huygens wavelets from both slits interfere constructively and destructively at each point of the screen, and closing one slit removes one set of wavelets, destroying the interference. Why does the pattern vanish when which-slit information is obtained? The Algebraic Channel reading: because a which-slit measurement is an eigenvalue event of the slit-position observable, and an eigenvalue event is an Algebraic Channel phenomenon that is orthogonal to the Geometric Channel propagation that produces interference. Under the dual-channel reading, obtaining which-slit information forces the system into the Algebraic Channel eigenvalue-registration mode, suppressing the Geometric Channel interference. This is the consequence of having chosen to read one channel rather than the other at the measurement.

**The delayed-choice puzzle** [47; 49]. Why can the decision to observe wave or particle behavior be made *after* the photon has traversed the apparatus? Because both readings are simultaneously available at every spacetime point along the photon’s path, not produced retroactively by the measurement. The photon’s the Geometric Channel wavefront is present throughout the apparatus; the Algebraic Channel eigenvalue event is produced at the detector. The “delayed choice” is a choice of which channel to read at the final detector, not a retroactive alteration of what happened earlier. Wheeler’s puzzle dissolves because there was never a moment at which the photon “had to be” a wave or a particle; both channels were present throughout, and the detection selects which one to register.

**The quantum-eraser puzzle** [50; 51]. Why can interference be restored after which-path information has been obtained and then erased? Because the Algebraic Channel and the Geometric Channel are not temporally ordered — the eigenvalue structure and the wavefront structure coexist at every spacetime point. “Obtaining” and “erasing” which-path information correspond to operational choices about which channel to correlate with the final detection; they do not alter the underlying dual-channel content of dx₄/dt = ic. The quantum eraser experiments are demonstrations of the simultaneity of the two channels, not of retroactive causation.

**The matter-wave puzzle.** Why do electrons, neutrons, atoms, and molecules exhibit wave behavior with wavelength λ = h/p? Because the Geometric Channel of dx₄/dt = ic applies to any matter coupled to x₄’s oscillation, and the de Broglie relation λ = h/p is (by [109]) the x₄-phase accumulation rate of matter at its Compton frequency ω_C = mc²/ℏ. The matter-wave wavelength is the wavelength of the oscillatory wavefront that every massive particle carries through the Geometric Channel of the Principle.

**The molecular-interference puzzle** [58; 61]. Why do C₆₀ and larger molecules still exhibit interference despite their large mass and internal structure? Because the Geometric Channel applies to every object with non-zero rest mass, with the de Broglie wavelength λ = h/(Mv) scaled by the total mass; for sufficiently low velocities, the wavelength remains comparable to the slit spacing even for very large molecules, and the Geometric Channel’s interference is observable. There is no lower limit to wave behavior imposed by “quantumness” because there is no separate “quantum” realm — all matter carries the Geometric Channel by virtue of coupling to x₄’s oscillation.

#### V.6.3 Novelty and Uniqueness at the Ontological Level

The explanation of wave/particle duality as the dual-channel reading of dx₄/dt = ic is novel in the precise sense that no prior framework has explained both aspects as simultaneous consequences of a single deeper geometric principle. The landscape of prior explanations may be summarized as follows.

**Copenhagen (Bohr 1928; Heisenberg 1927).** Complementarity is an interpretational doctrine, not a derivation: it asserts that wave and particle aspects are incompatible and must be specified by experimental context, but it does not derive either aspect from a deeper principle. The uncertainty relation Δx·Δp ≥ ℏ/2 is a theorem of the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ, which Copenhagen takes as a postulate of quantum mechanics.

**Pilot-wave (de Broglie 1927; Bohm 1952; Dürr-Goldstein-Zanghì 1992).** The wave aspect is physically real (the wavefunction guides the particle); the particle aspect is physically real (the particle has a definite trajectory). Both are postulates of the framework; the wavefunction’s Schrödinger dynamics and the guiding equation are both added as independent postulates. The pilot-wave framework does not explain why the wavefunction should exist or why it should have the specific Schrödinger dynamics; both are inherited from standard quantum mechanics.

**Many-worlds (Everett 1957; Wallace 2012).** The wavefunction is the only physically real structure; the particle aspect is an emergent feature of branching (decoherence producing effectively-classical branch histories within the universal wavefunction). This explains the particle aspect as a branching phenomenon but takes the wavefunction and its Schrödinger dynamics as postulates. The dual aspect is not derived from a deeper principle.

**Decoherence (Zurek 1982; Joos-Zeh 1985).** Environmental decoherence explains the *appearance* of classical particle-like behavior in macroscopic systems by tracing out environment degrees of freedom, producing an effective classical distribution from quantum superposition. This is important for understanding the classical limit but does not explain why the underlying quantum structure exhibits dual aspect at the microscopic level; the wavefunction and its dynamics are again postulates.

**Relational quantum mechanics (Rovelli 1996).** Quantum states are relational — they describe correlations between systems rather than intrinsic properties — and the wave/particle distinction is a relational artifact. This is a philosophical reframing, not a derivation from a deeper physical principle.

**QBism (Fuchs-Mermin-Schack 2014).** The wavefunction is an epistemic object describing an agent’s personal probability assignments. Wave/particle duality is a feature of the probabilistic structure, not an ontological feature of physical systems. This is an epistemological reinterpretation, not a derivation from a deeper physical principle.

**Stochastic mechanics (Nelson 1966; Lindgren-Liukkonen 2019).** The wave aspect is derived from stochastic dynamics in configuration space; the particle aspect is the underlying classical particle position undergoing stochastic fluctuations. The i of the Schrödinger equation enters, in Nelson, through the complex combination of forward and backward real equations and, in Lindgren–Liukkonen, through the Minkowski volume form (§VI.3, §VI.4). Neither identifies it with a physical fact about spacetime. The frameworks derive the wave aspect through a single route but do not explain the dual aspect as two readings of a single geometric fact.

**Bohmian field-theoretic extensions and others.** A number of frameworks (Bohm-Hiley 1993 implicate-order analysis; Valentini 2004 quantum nonequilibrium program; Struyve 2011 and Dürr 2014 on Bohmian quantum field theory) extend pilot-wave mechanics to fields and gauge theories. These extensions preserve the pilot-wave framework’s status as an interpretation rather than a derivation; they do not alter the fundamental status of the wave and the particle as postulated rather than derived.

Across the century of theoretical engagement with wave/particle duality, *none* of these frameworks explains wave and particle as simultaneous readings of a single geometric-dynamical principle, with the wave aspect forced by one channel of that principle’s content and the particle aspect forced by the other. The McGucken Principle is the first framework to achieve this dual-channel reading. This uniqueness has a reason: no framework in the historical record possesses dual-channel content in a single geometric-dynamical statement (as established in §V.3), and therefore no framework can generate both aspects as simultaneous consequences of a single principle. The McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework is the first to do so because it is the first to possess the requisite dual-channel structure.

The explanation is novel in a further sense: it *dissolves* the puzzle rather than merely interpreting it. Complementarity, pilot waves, many worlds, decoherence, relational states, epistemic reinterpretation — each prior approach accepts the dual aspect as an irreducible feature and proposes an interpretive scheme for living with it. The McGucken reading does not accept the duality as irreducible; it derives the duality as the consequence of a single geometric fact. The puzzle of wave/particle duality, under the McGucken Principle, is the expected consequence of having a principle with dual-channel content, applied at the ontological level to the question of what quantum objects *are*.

### V.7 Schrödinger and Heisenberg Pictures as Dual-Channel Readings at the Dynamical Level

The dual-channel structure of dx₄/dt = ic applies a third time, at the *dynamical* level, to the distinction between the Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures of quantum-mechanical time evolution. Where §§II–III develop the dual reading at the *foundational* level (Hamiltonian versus Lagrangian formulation) and §V.6 develops it at the *ontological* level (wave versus particle aspect), this section develops the reading at the dynamical level.

#### V.7.1 The Schrödinger and Heisenberg Pictures

Quantum-mechanical time evolution has two equivalent representations. In the *Schrödinger picture*, physical states |ψ(t)⟩ evolve in time under the Schrödinger equation

*iℏ d|ψ(t)⟩/dt = Ĥ |ψ(t)⟩,*

while operators Â are fixed. Expectation values are ⟨ψ(t)| Â |ψ(t)⟩, with all time dependence carried by the state. In the *Heisenberg picture*, operators Â(t) evolve in time under the Heisenberg equation

*iℏ dÂ(t)/dt = [Â(t), Ĥ],*

while states |ψ⟩ are fixed. Expectation values are ⟨ψ| Â(t) |ψ⟩, with all time dependence carried by the operator. The two pictures are related by the unitary transformation Û(t) = exp(−i Ĥ t/ℏ): states in the Schrödinger picture are Û(t) times states in the Heisenberg picture, and operators in the Heisenberg picture are Û(t)† Â Û(t) of operators in the Schrödinger picture. They agree on all expectation values and therefore on all measurable quantities.

The historical development of the two pictures is as follows. Heisenberg’s 1925 matrix-mechanics paper [33] formulated quantum mechanics as an algebra of operators whose matrix elements describe transitions between stationary states; the picture is fundamentally algebraic, with observables as the primary dynamical objects. The algebra was worked out in full by Born, Heisenberg, and Jordan in the *Dreimännerarbeit* [34], which established the matrix-mechanical framework with operators satisfying [q̂, p̂] = iℏ and time evolution generated by commutators with the Hamiltonian. Schrödinger’s 1926 four-paper series [35; 36; 37; 38] formulated quantum mechanics as a wave theory, with physical states represented by wavefunctions ψ(x, t) evolving according to the Schrödinger equation; the picture is fundamentally propagational, with wavefunctions as the primary dynamical objects. The equivalence of the two formulations was established by Schrödinger himself in his third 1926 paper [36], which showed that the matrix elements of Heisenberg’s operators equal the integrals ∫ψ*(x)·Â·ψ(x)dx of Schrödinger’s wavefunctions, and by von Neumann’s 1932 *Mathematische Grundlagen der Quantenmechanik* [43], which placed both formulations on the common algebraic foundation of operators on Hilbert space.

Since 1932, the two pictures have coexisted in the physics literature as equivalent representational tools, each adopted in contexts where it is most convenient. The Schrödinger picture is standard for introductory treatments of quantum mechanics (e.g., Sakurai [73], Griffiths [74], Cohen-Tannoudji-Diu-Laloë [75], Messiah [76]) because it makes explicit the role of the wavefunction and the computational simplicity of diagonalizing the Hamiltonian; the Heisenberg picture is standard for quantum field theory (e.g., Weinberg [22], Peskin-Schroeder [77]) because it makes explicit the role of the operator algebra and the covariance of operators under Lorentz transformations. The interaction picture, developed in the 1940s by Tomonaga [70], Schwinger [71], and Feynman [72] for renormalization in quantum electrodynamics, is a hybrid picture in which part of the Hamiltonian acts on states (as in Schrödinger) and part acts on operators (as in Heisenberg); it is the standard workhorse for perturbative QFT calculations but does not add a new dimension beyond the Schrödinger/Heisenberg dichotomy.

The question of *why* these two pictures exist as equivalent representations — why quantum mechanics should have two distinct formulations of dynamics — has not been addressed in the literature. Textbook treatments present the equivalence as a mathematical fact (Schrödinger 1926b proved it; Stone-von Neumann confirmed that the representation is essentially unique) without explaining why the dynamics of quantum mechanics should decompose into state-dynamics and operator-dynamics as two readings of a single underlying dynamics. The McGucken Principle supplies that explanation.

#### V.7.2 The Dual-Channel Reading at the Dynamical Level

Under the dual-channel analysis of §§V.1–V.5, the Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures are the two readings of the dynamics of dx₄/dt = ic.

**Heisenberg picture as the Algebraic Channel at the dynamical level.** Time evolution in the Heisenberg picture is generated by commutators with the Hamiltonian: dÂ/dt = (i/ℏ)[Ĥ, Â]. This is the algebraic-symmetry reading of time evolution.
