Thus U is either invertible or zero, and U = 0 for every F would give ∑_A Γ'_A FΓ_A⁻¹ = 0 for all F; taking F = Eⱼₖ (matrix units) and reading off entries gives ∑_A (Γ'_A)ᵢⱼ(Γ_A⁻¹)_{kl} = 0 for all i,j,k,l, which contradicts the linear independence of the Γ'_A (Theorem 4.2(b)) once one contracts with the dual basis of the Γ_A⁻¹ under the trace form. Hence some F gives invertible U with γ'^μ = Uγ^μ U⁻¹. Covariance (ii) is realized by S(Λ) = exp(¼ω_{μν}γ^μγ^ν), the exponential of bivectors (Theorem 4.4), acting by the single-sided left action of Theorem 7.2, with S⁻¹γ^μ S = Λ^μ_νγ^ν proved in Theorem 7.5(a). ∎ **G1.** Atiyah states the same uniqueness from the retardation problem: "there's no other solution… Dirac discovered this was the only operator you can use" [24 in the source paper’s numbering].

**Theorem 9.3 (The Dirac equation).** *The matter field carried by the x₄-expansion, first-order in all four coordinates, Lorentz-covariant, squaring to the Klein–Gordon operator (3.2), and coupling the two chiralities through the Compton frequency, satisfies*

(iγ^μ∂_μ - (mc)/ħ)ψ = 0, (9.1)

*equivalently, in the (-,+,+,+) generators e_μ = -iγ_μ of Convention 4.3, (e^μ∂_μ + mc/ħ)ψ = 0. Every solution of (9.1) satisfies (3.2).*

*Proof.* Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄ at c, dx₄/dt = ic. By Theorem 5.1, (iγ^μ∂_μ)² = -g^{μν}∂_μ∂_ν, so

(iγ^μ∂_μ - κ)(iγ^μ∂_μ + κ) = -(g^{μν}∂_μ∂_ν + κ²), κ = (mc)/ħ, (9.2)

and the right side applied to ψ is -((1/c²)∂ₜ² - ∇² + m²c²/ħ²)ψ, the Klein–Gordon operator of (3.2) with x⁰ = ct. Each factor on the left is a first-order root; the choice of sign of κ is the choice of which factor annihilates ψ, conventionally (9.1). Applying the other factor to (9.1) shows every solution satisfies (3.2). In the Weyl basis (4.2), γ^μ∂_μ = [0, σ^μ∂_μ; σ̄^μ∂_μ, 0] with σ^μ = (𝟙,σ), σ̄^μ = (𝟙,-σ) (the off-diagonal blocks of (4.2) are σⁱ above and -σⁱ below, and γ⁰ contributes 𝟙 to both), so with ψ = (ψ_L, ψ_R)ᵀ,

(iγ^μ∂_μ - κ)ψ = [-κ ψ_L + iσ^μ∂_μψ_R; iσ̄^μ∂_μψ_L - κ ψ_R] = 0 (9.3)

(Appendix A, check 22). The derivative blocks are off-diagonal and the mass block is diagonal: the only term linking ψ_L to itself or ψ_R to itself is κ = mc/ħ, the Compton frequency over c, so the two chiralities are coupled by the Compton clock alone, and at m = 0 (9.3) splits into the two Weyl equations iσ^μ∂_μψ_R = 0, iσ̄^μ∂_μψ_L = 0. ∎ **G1.**

The elements of (9.1) are each derived above: the i in front of γ^μ∂_μ is the i of dx₄/dt = ic that makes the generator unitary (Theorem 9.1); the γ^μ are forced by Theorem 4.1 and are the intertwiners between the two chiralities — γ⁰ exchanges ψ_L and ψ_R and γⁱ exchanges them with the Pauli twist; the first-order form is the generator form of Theorem 9.1; the mass is the Compton frequency of Theorem 3.3; the spinor transforms by the single-sided action of Theorem 7.2 and hence by half-angles (Theorem 7.3); the antiparticle solutions are the x₄-reversed fields of Section 10. In the Algebraic Channel, (9.1) is the linear square root of the mass-shell Casimir on the spinor representation; in the Geometric Channel, it is the first-order propagation equation of the Sphere's null-direction coordinate carrying the Compton clock (Theorems 8.1–8.2); the two channels agree [4 in the source paper’s numbering].

**The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic reading.** Theorem 9.2 above supplies the uniqueness, up to a change of basis of ℂ⁴, of the matrices α_i and β used in Theorems S.1 to S.6, and Theorem 9.3 is the covariant form of the equation iℏ∂ψ/∂t = H_Dψ of Theorem S.2.

## Appendix E. The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic Reading of the Four x₄-Rate Quantities in the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian Routes: Appendix A of [167], Reproduced Verbatim and in Full, and Carried Through the Present Paper

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension x₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: dx₄/dt = ic, with x₄ = ict, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

**McGucken Principle anchor.** dx₄/dt = ic is the physical expansion of the fourth dimension at the rate c from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h. Four quantities measure that expansion and the part a worldline takes in it: the coordinate rate dx₄/dt = ic, the share v_{x₄} = √(c² − v²), the proper-time component dx₄/dτ = icγ, and the accumulated x₄-advance X₄ = ic·τ. Appendix A of [167] defines the four and states which one is the Principle. §E.1 reproduces that appendix verbatim and in full. §E.2 proves which of the four each step of the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian routes of the present paper uses. §E.3 works two examples.

### E.1 Appendix A of [167], “Reading the McGucken Principle: A Note on dx₄/dt, dx₄/dτ, and the Photon — Clarifying Common Reader Misconceptions,” Reproduced Verbatim and in Full

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The reproduced text states dx₄/dt = ic as the physical fact from which every quantity in it descends. Section, theorem and lemma numbers inside the reproduced text are those of [167].

**About this appendix.** Readers arriving at the McGucken Principle from a standard graduate-relativity background bring one deep habit: the reflex to treat per-proper-time quantities as the “physical” or “covariant” ones, and per-coordinate-time quantities as gauge-dependent bookkeeping. That habit points the wrong way here, because the Principle is a statement about the manifold itself — an expansion happening at every event, at the same rate, regardless of which worldline crosses that event. This appendix explains the four distinct x₄-rate quantities the paper uses, states which one is the Principle, and shows through five worked examples how photons, horizons, twins, and GPS clocks all read naturally once the four quantities are kept in their proper roles.

**A.0 The Physical Fact**

Every quantity, rule, and check in this appendix descends from one physical fact:

> The fourth dimension x₄ is expanding in a spherically symmetric manner at the rate of c from every spacetime event, or dx₄/dt = ic, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h.

That is the McGucken Principle. Before any of its consequences are unpacked, its status must be stated plainly, because it is the point on which every reading in this appendix turns: dx₄/dt = ic is an *ontological* claim — a statement about what physically is — and not a coordinate convention, a formal device, or a notational choice. It asserts that the fourth dimension is a real dimension that is really expanding, at the rate c, from every event of spacetime, now. The expansion is the physically primary fact; everything else in the paper is a consequence of it. Minkowski spacetime is what the expansion looks like after its rate is integrated into the coordinate x₄ = ict and squared into the Lorentzian metric x₄² = −c²t²; Hilbert space is what the same expansion looks like when its per-increment rate ic is read as oscillation; the light cone is the locus the expanding wavefront traces; the metric, the causal order, the Poincaré group, the Born rule, and the Schrödinger equation are all downstream of the one expansion. None of these is postulated independently. They descend, by the derivations of the body, from the single physical fact that x₄ is expanding at c.

This is the reversal the reader must hold onto throughout. Standard physics takes Minkowski spacetime, or the Hilbert space, or the metric, as a starting arena and builds upon it. Here the arena is not a starting point but a *result*: the starting point is the physical expansion dx₄/dt = ic, and the arena is what that expansion generates. When the appendix below insists that dx₄/dt = ic is primary and the per-proper-time quantities are constructed from it, this is what is meant — not a preference among equivalent formalisms, but a claim about which fact is physically prior. The rate is prior; the worldlines that share in it, the metric that its squaring produces, and the observers that read it are all posterior.

The imaginary unit i in dx₄/dt = ic records the algebraic perpendicularity of the x₄-axis to the three spatial axes on which the expansion casts its projection. Its geometric realisation at each event p ∈ 𝓜_G (the McGucken source space, the spacetime of expanding McGucken Spheres at every point) is the McGucken Sphere M_p^+(t) — the spherical wavefront of radius ct swept out around p by the x₄-expansion. The Sphere is what distinguishes the Principle from Minkowski’s constant four-velocity norm u^μ u_μ = −c²: the norm is a scalar constraint carrying no group action and no measure, and it survives here as a theorem of the Principle (the chain-rule pullback of the Sphere’s expansion rate).

The Sphere carries structure that supplies the Born rule downstream by five independent routes, established in the body of the paper and consolidated here. The two most fundamental are the Metric Route (the exponent 2 is the rank of the Minkowski metric that x₄ = ict induces; Lemma 7.1) and the Haar Route (the Sphere’s transitive SO(3) action forces a unique invariant probability measure; Lemma 7.1.5). That these derivationally-disjoint derivations return the same Born density |ψ|² is an instance of the convergent overdetermination catalogued in Remark 7.17 and §17.5bis; see Remark A.0.1.

*Remark A.0.1 (Five independent derivations of the Born rule).* The Born density P = |ψ|² is derived in this framework by five routes that share no premises beyond the McGucken Sphere itself, each proceeding from a distinct property of the one Sphere. They are given here in order of foundational priority, matching the catalogue of Remark 7.17.

*(1) Metric Route — the Sphere’s metric rank* (Lemma 7.1; Theorem 7.2). The x₄-flux density at a detection event is the metric pairing of the forward +ic advance with its conjugate −ic advance. The Minkowski metric induced by x₄ = ict is a rank-2 tensor, so the pairing is bilinear in (ψ, ψ^∗); rank-4 quartic densities are excluded by rank-counting, and U(1) phase invariance, reality, non-negativity, and normalization then force P = |ψ|² uniquely. The exponent 2 is the rank of the metric. This is the most fundamental route, because the exponent is a property of the metric the Principle itself induces.

*(2) Haar Route — the Sphere’s symmetry group* (Lemma 7.1.5). The Sphere at the detection event is a round S² ≅ SO(3)/SO(2) carrying the transitive SO(3) action fixed by the isotropy of the x₄-expansion. By the uniqueness of the invariant measure on a homogeneous space of a compact group, there is exactly one SO(3)-invariant probability measure on the Sphere, the round measure dμ = sin θ dθ dφ/(4π), and this is the Born weight over detection directions. This is the route that answers why a probability *measure* exists at all: a measure is physically forced only where a symmetry singles it out uniquely, and SO(3) singles out exactly this one. Minkowski’s norm u^μ u_μ = −c² carries no group action and therefore no such measure — which is why the Sphere, not the bare norm, is what grounds quantum probability.

*(3) Norm Route — the Sphere’s sesquilinear inner product* (Lemma 7.6). The rank-2 sesquilinear inner product satisfies the parallelogram identity as a two-line algebraic consequence, giving the L²-norm structure on Hilbert space ℋ with no Jordan–von Neumann theorem needed as upstream input.

*(4) Collision Route — two physical Spheres* (Theorem 7.7; Lemma 7.7.5). The number of physical participants in a head-on detection event is exactly two — emitter Sphere and absorber Sphere — and the coincidence rate is sesquilinear in the two amplitudes, with sum, max, min, and arithmetic/geometric/harmonic means excluded by action-additivity.

*(5) PT-Symmetry Route — the Sphere’s reflection* (Theorem 7.11). The Sphere’s discrete PT_E-symmetry through its source event gives ψ^∗(P) = ψ(PT_E · P) exactly, so the squaring in the Born density is the cascade-level expression of that symmetry, and complex conjugation itself is grounded in it.

Routes (1) and (2) proceed respectively from the *rank* of the metric and the *symmetry group* of the Sphere, and are foregrounded as the two most important: the first fixes the exponent as the metric’s rank, the second supplies the invariant measure a probability rule requires. Routes (3)–(5) confirm the same exponent from the norm, the physical collision count, and the reflection symmetry. No route uses another’s premise — rank 2, the SO(3) action, the parallelogram identity, the two-participant count, and the PT-reflection are five logically independent facts about the same Sphere — so their agreement on P = |ψ|² is genuine convergence, not one argument told five times. This is the sense in which the Born rule is convergently overdetermined by dx₄/dt = ic (Remark 7.17; §17.5bis): one downstream fact fixed by five upstream properties of the Sphere.

**A.1 The Four x₄-Rate Quantities**

The framework uses four related quantities associated with the fourth dimension’s advance. Each has its own symbol, its own domain of definition, and its own interpretation, and they diverge on null worldlines.

*(R1) The coordinate rate* dx₄/dt = ic, parameter: coordinate t; range: fixed value ic; universal — holds at every event of 𝓜_G. The Principle. With the integrated shadow x₄ = ict, this rate holds identically along every worldline of 𝓜_G — timelike, null, accelerated, at any gravitational potential — because x₄ is a coordinate function of t alone. Every statement about the universality of the expansion, its metric-independence (GR T2), or its exception-freeness is a statement in (R1).

*(R2) The Sphere-tangent velocity component* v_{x₄} ≡ √(c² − v²) (the worldline’s share of the x₄-expansion, per coordinate time); parameter: coordinate t; range: [0, c]; worldline-dependent. A bounded quantity (Lemma 5.1.3.2). The partition v² + v_{x₄}² = c² holds for every worldline, null ones included: at spatial rest v_{x₄} = c; on a null worldline v = c gives v_{x₄} = 0. On timelike worldlines, v_{x₄} coincides with the standard time-dilation-related quantities of special relativity: v_{x₄} = c dτ/dt = c/γ for 0 ≤ v < c. The identity v_{x₄} = c dτ/dt is a theorem about v_{x₄} — τ appears as output, not as the differentiation parameter. At v = c (the null limit) all three expressions give v_{x₄} = 0: the √(c² − v²) form by direct substitution, the c dτ/dt form as the ratio c · 0/dt with dτ = 0, and the c/γ form as the limit γ → ∞. The framework uses v_{x₄} as the primary symbol because it is defined by direct substitution on the null boundary. Statements about time dilation, the photon’s x₄-rest, and horizons live here.

*(R3) The Lorentzian temporal component* u⁴ = dx₄/dτ = icγ; parameter: proper τ; unbounded, undefined on null worldlines; a chain-rule consequence. Derived from (R1) by the chain rule (Theorem 5.1.2.8): dx₄/dτ = (dx₄/dt)(dt/dτ) = icγ. Its magnitude cγ is unbounded as v → c, and on a null worldline (dτ = 0) the quantity is undefined — there the affinely-parametrised null tangent dx^μ/dλ replaces it (Lemma 7.2.2.1a).

*(R4) The accumulated x₄-advance along γ*, X₄[γ] ≡ ∫_γ ic dτ = ic τ[γ]; a worldline functional, path-dependent. Distinct from the coordinate x₄. Two worldlines sharing endpoints share Δx₄ = ic Δt but generally differ in X₄. Along a null worldline X₄ is constant; around any closed curve ∮ dx₄ = 0 identically. Statements about differential aging (the twins) live in (R4). The bridge to (R2): dX₄ = ic dτ = i v_{x₄} dt.

**A.2 Which One Is the Principle**

The Principle is dx₄/dt = ic: the coordinate-time reading (R1). Because x₄ is a coordinate function of t alone, the rate dx₄/dt takes the same value ic at every event of 𝓜_G — an expanding photon-event, a horizon event, an event on any accelerated worldline. The reading dx₄/dτ = ic is available too, but only where dτ = dt: on a worldline at spatial rest, the chain rule collapses and (R1) coincides with the value of (R3). This is Lemma 4.7 (the rest-frame reverse-reading equivalence), and the qualification dτ = dt is what makes it valid. On a moving worldline the chain rule gives icγ; on a null worldline the quantity does not exist. The coordinate-time reading is primary because dx₄/dt = ic is a physical fact — the expansion itself is prior to any worldline, and dx₄/dt = ic records it directly.

**A.3 Reading the Photon, the Horizon, and the Time-Dilation Formula**

**A.3.1 What is the photon’s x₄-rate?** The photon’s share in the expansion is zero: v_{x₄} = 0 (the v = c limit of v² + v_{x₄}² = c²), obtained by direct substitution of v = c into v_{x₄} = √(c² − v²). The reader may be tempted to write “dx₄/dt = 0 for the photon” or “dx₄/dτ = 0.” Neither is available. The first is the coordinate rate (R1), which is ic at every event of 𝓜_G including the photon’s — what vanishes is the photon’s share, not the expansion. The second is 0/0: proper time is not defined for a photon (dτ = 0 pointwise). The correct statement is the share v_{x₄} = 0, so X₄ does not accumulate, so the photon ages not at all. The Principle continues to hold at every event the photon crosses.

**A.3.2 What does “the x₄-rate at the horizon” mean?** The rate is gravity-rigid: ∂(dx₄/dt)/∂ g_{μν} = 0 (GR T2). The rate dx₄/dt = ic takes the same value near the horizon as at infinity. What varies with potential is the share: v_{x₄}(r) = c dτ/dt = c√(1 − r_s/r) (static Schwarzschild observer), vanishing at r = r_s. On the infaller’s own clock dτ = dt locally and Lemma 4.7 applies: the local rate is the full ic throughout. Gravitational time dilation is the coordinate-frame projection of a locally invariant rate.

**A.3.3 Which identity is “c² through spacetime”?** *Sphere partition* (per coordinate time, all worldlines): v² + v_{x₄}² = c², bounded, locus a quarter-circle. *Lorentzian four-velocity norm* (per proper time, massive worldlines): u^μ u_μ = |dx₄/dτ|² − |d𝐱/dτ|² = −c², unbounded in each term, signature difference fixing the invariant. The Euclidean per-proper-time sum |dx₄/dτ|² + |d𝐱/dτ|² equals c² only in the instantaneous rest frame; elsewhere c²γ². Plus-sign identities belong to (R2); minus-sign identities to (R3).

**A.3.4 How does the twin paradox read?** Coordinate accumulation is path-independent: both twins accumulate Δx₄ = ic Δt. Differential ageing is read from X₄ = ∫ ic dτ, advancing at real magnitude v_{x₄} per unit coordinate time. On the traveling leg v_{x₄} < c, so the traveler accumulates less X₄, so less proper time. The stay-at-home twin retains v_{x₄} = c and accumulates the maximal X₄. The CTC question closes the same way: ∮ dx₄ = 0 identically, so a CTC cannot run on the coordinate; the impossibility lives in X₄.

**A.3.5 γ-projection and rapidity.** A boost is hyperbolic: rapidity φ with cosh φ = γ. The Sphere-partition projection is circular: Sphere angle θ with sin θ = v/c, cos θ = v_{x₄}/c = 1/γ. Related by the Gudermannian, θ = gd(φ), so cos θ = sech φ. The 1/γ projection is about θ; unbounded component growth is about φ.

**A.4 Why Nothing of Relativity Is Lost**

The fourth dimension advances at the same rate at every event of 𝓜_G; worldlines differ only in how much of that advance they share in. Massive at rest shares fully (v_{x₄} = c); moving shares less (v_{x₄} = √(c² − v²), clock slowed by the Lorentz factor); photon does not share at all (v_{x₄} = 0, ageless, no X₄). Lorentz invariance survives entire because the invariant content — the norm u^μ u_μ = −c² and the metric — is a theorem of the Principle (the metric is the pullback of the Euclidean form along x₄ = ict, the −c² from (ic)²). Lorentz transformations preserve that derived metric; γ, time dilation, length contraction, E = γ mc², the null cone, and the mass-shell all come out unchanged. The foliation quantities v_{x₄} and X₄ are additional bookkeeping frame-anchored to the cosmic-comoving foliation (the CMB rest frame); the Cloaking Theorem (Theorem 39) explains why local metrology cannot detect them. GR survives by the invariant/deformable split: the rate is gravity-rigid; gravity deforms the spatial sector. Time dilation, redshift, light bending, perihelion advance, the horizon, and the field equations are recovered exactly. The horizon is where the share is extinguished; the expansion does not stop.

**A.5 Worked Examples**

**A.5.1 The twin paradox.** Traveler at speed v for coordinate time Δt. Coordinate accumulation: Δx₄ = ic Δt for both. Traveler’s share: v_{x₄} = c√(1 − v²/c²). Accumulated: ΔX₄^{trav} = ic√(1 − v²/c²) Δt vs ΔX₄^{stay} = ic Δt. Differential aging: Δτ^{trav} − Δτ^{stay} = Δt (√(1 − v²/c²) − 1) < 0: the traveler is younger by exactly the Lorentz factor.

**A.5.2 GPS clock rate.** Satellite at radius r, speed v, mass M. Principle: dx₄/dt = ic. Gravitational share: v_{x₄}^{grav}/c = √(1 − 2GM/rc²); kinematic factor √(1 − v²/c²). Total: v_{x₄}^{sat}/c ≈ 1 − GM/(rc²) − v²/(2c²). Ground: ≈ 1 − GM/(R_⊕ c²). Fractional difference ≈ GM(1/R_⊕ − 1/r) − v²/(2c²), matching the +4.5 × 10⁻¹⁰ pre-launch offset at r = 26,600 km, v = 3.87 km/s.

**A.5.3 Schwarzschild gravitational redshift.** Photon from r₁ to r₂ > r₁. Principle: dx₄/dt = ic. Local clock rate: v_{x₄}(r)/c = √(1 − r_s/r). Frequency ratio ν₂/ν₁ = √((1 − r_s/r₁)/(1 − r_s/r₂)); weak-field Δν/ν ≈ −GM(1/r₂ − 1/r₁)/c², matching Pound–Rebka 1959. Photon: v_{x₄} = 0 throughout, no X₄. The redshift is about the clock rates at emitter and receiver, not the photon.

**A.5.4 Hawking temperature.** At r = r_s, local share v_{x₄}(r) → 0. Surface gravity κ = c/(2r_s); compactification circumference L_H = 4π r_s = cℏβ_H; temperature T_H = ℏ c³/(8π G M k_B). The expansion does not stop at the horizon; the share is extinguished on the static foliation, and compactification of the Euclidean-section x₄-axis at L_H produces the thermal spectrum.

**A.5.5 Bell’s spaceship.** Rockets A, B at proper distance L₀, accelerating identically. Coordinate rate: dx₄/dt = ic. Share: v_{x₄}(t) = c√(1 − v(t)²/c²), decreasing. Accumulated: X₄^A = X₄^B, so both age identically. Launch-frame separation constant L₀; co-moving separation L(t) = L₀ c/v_{x₄}(t), growing without bound. The string breaks: the budget shifts into the spatial sector identically on each rocket, so no differential ageing; the paradox lives in the spatial-sector geometry, the string breaking as co-moving distance grows as 1/v_{x₄}.

**A.6 Anchor Table**

The definitions and results this appendix refers to are anchored in the body as follows: the four-quantity notation (R1)–(R4) at Definition 5.1.3.4-ter (Chapter 5); the Sphere-tangent share partition v² + v_{x₄}² = c² at Lemma 5.1.3.2 (Chapter 5); the reverse-reading equivalence (dx₄/dτ = ic on rest worldlines) at Lemma 4.7 (Introduction, after Theorem 4); the McGucken–Wick coordinate identity τ_W = x₄/c at Theorem 4; the null-worldline replacement for u^μ at Lemma 7.2.2.1a (Chapter 7); the photon at v_{x₄} = 0 at Lemma 7.2.3.1 (Chapter 7); the gravity-rigidity of the rate ∂(dx₄/dt)/∂ g_{μν} = 0 at GR T2 (Chapter 5); the chain-rule identity u⁴ = icγ at Theorem 5.1.2.8 (Chapter 5); the Cloaking Theorem at Theorem 39 (Chapter 20); the extended photon treatment at §20.9quater (Chapter 20); and KMS periodicity as x₄-compactification at Chapter 15bis.

### E.2 The Four x₄-Rate Quantities in the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian Routes of the Present Paper

**McGucken Principle anchor.** dx₄/dt = ic has the same value ic at every event, and the proper time of a worldline records how much of that expansion the worldline shares. The Hamiltonian route of §II uses the rate itself, which is the same for every state. The Lagrangian route of §III uses the accumulated x₄-advance, which differs from path to path, and that difference is the phase by which paths interfere. The two propositions below prove this with the four quantities of §A.1 of the reproduced appendix: the coordinate rate dx₄/dt = ic, the share v_{x₄} = √(c² − v²), the proper-time component dx₄/dτ = icγ, and the accumulated x₄-advance X₄[path] = ic·τ[path], proper time up to the factor ic.

Notation: v = |dx/dt| is the speed along a path, γ = (1 − v²/c²)^(−1/2), μ = mc/ℏ is the Compton wavenumber and ω_C = mc²/ℏ = μc the Compton angular frequency, as in §III.2 and Proposition L.3.

**Proposition E.1** *(The Hamiltonian route uses the coordinate rate dx₄/dt = ic).*

*(i) For every differentiable function F of (x₁, x₂, x₃, x₄), evaluated on x₄ = ict, ∂F/∂t = ic ∂F/∂x₄.*

*(ii) With p̂₄ = −iℏ∂/∂x₄ as in Lemma H.3.1, the generator of time translations is Ĥ = iℏ∂/∂t = −ic p̂₄.*

*(iii) On the analytic vectors of Ĥ, the evolution operator exp(−iĤt/ℏ) = exp(ict·∂/∂x₄) of Lemma V.7.3.A translates x₄ by Δx₄ = ict. This displacement is the same for every state.*

*(iv) On the plane wave ψ = exp(i(k·x − ωt)) with E = ℏω, p̂₄ψ = (iE/c)ψ. For matter of mass m and speed v, E = γmc², and the eigenvalue is iE/c = m·icγ = m·dx₄/dτ.*

**Proof.** Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄ at the rate c, dx₄/dt = ic. (i) is the chain rule with dx₄/dt = ic. (ii) By (i), iℏ∂/∂t = iℏ·ic·∂/∂x₄ = −ℏc ∂/∂x₄, and −ic p̂₄ = −ic·(−iℏ)∂/∂x₄ = −ℏc ∂/∂x₄. (iii) −iĤt/ℏ = t ∂/∂t = ict·∂/∂x₄ by (i), and on an analytic vector the exponential series of a·∂/∂x₄ is the Taylor series of the shift x₄ → x₄ + a, here with a = ict. The value ict does not depend on the vector. (iv) By (i), p̂₄ψ = −iℏ·(1/(ic))·∂ψ/∂t = −(ℏ/c)(−iω)ψ = (iℏω/c)ψ. The chain rule gives dx₄/dτ = (dx₄/dt)(dt/dτ) = icγ, so m·dx₄/dτ = iγmc = iE/c. ∎

**The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic reading.** Proposition E.1 places the coordinate rate and the proper-time component in the dx₄/dt = ic Algebraic Channel. The operators p̂₄ and Ĥ and the evolution operator are built from the coordinate rate dx₄/dt = ic alone, parts (i) to (iii). The proper-time component dx₄/dτ = icγ appears only as an eigenvalue, part (iv), and it is obtained from the coordinate rate by the chain rule, as §A.2 of the reproduced appendix states. The sum of squares of the eigenvalues is |p|² + p₄² = |p|² − E²/c² = −m²c², which is m² times the four-velocity norm u^μ u_μ = −c² and is the relation ω²/c² − |k|² = μ² of the matter wave equation of §III.2. The norm is therefore a consequence of the Principle in the present paper as well. The homogeneity used in Proposition H.2, that the rate is c at x and at x + a, is the statement of §A.1 that the coordinate rate holds at every event.

**Proposition E.2** *(The Lagrangian route uses the accumulated x₄-advance X₄[path]).*

Let a path x(t), t_i ≤ t ≤ t_f, have speed v(t) < c at every t, and let T = t_f − t_i. Then:

*(i) X₄[path] = ic·τ[path] = i ∫ v_{x₄} dt, and the coordinate accumulation Δx₄ = icT is the same for every path between the same two events.*

*(ii) The weight of Proposition L.3 is exp(−μ X₄[path]) = exp(i S_rel[path]/ℏ) with S_rel[path] = −mc ∫ v_{x₄} dt. The relativistic Lagrangian of free matter is L = −mc·v_{x₄}.*

*(iii) v_{x₄} = c − v²/(2c) − r(v) with v⁴/(8c³) ≤ r(v) ≤ v⁴/(2c³) for 0 ≤ v ≤ c. Hence*

*exp(−μ X₄[path]) = exp(−μ Δx₄) · exp((i/ℏ) ∫ ½mv² dt) · exp((i/ℏ) ∫ mc·r(v) dt),*

*where the first factor exp(−μ Δx₄) = exp(−iω_C T) is the same for every path and the exponent of the last factor is bounded in modulus by (m/(2ℏc²)) ∫ v⁴ dt.*

*(iv) For two paths, path₁ and path₂, between the same two events, the ratio of their weights is exp(−μ(X₄[path₁] − X₄[path₂])) = exp(−iω_C(τ[path₁] − τ[path₂])).*

*(v) Along a null path v_{x₄} = 0 and X₄ is constant, so no phase accumulates along the cone of the McGucken Sphere.*

**Proof.** Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄ at the rate c, dx₄/dt = ic. (i) dτ = (1 − v²/c²)^(1/2) dt = (v_{x₄}/c) dt, so ic·τ[path] = i ∫ v_{x₄} dt. The coordinate x₄ = ict is a function of t alone, so Δx₄ = ic(t_f − t_i). (ii) −μ X₄[path] = −(mc/ℏ)·i ∫ v_{x₄} dt = (i/ℏ)(−mc ∫ v_{x₄} dt), and −mc ∫ v_{x₄} dt = −mc² τ[path] = S_rel[path]. (iii) Put u = v²/c² and ρ(u) = 1 − u/2 − (1 − u)^(1/2). Since (1 − u/2)² − (1 − u) = u²/4, ρ(u) = (u²/4)/(1 − u/2 + (1 − u)^(1/2)). For 0 ≤ u ≤ 1 the denominator lies between ½ and 2, so u²/8 ≤ ρ(u) ≤ u²/2. With r(v) = c·ρ(u) this is the stated expansion and bound. Substituting in (ii), −mc ∫ v_{x₄} dt = −mc²T + ∫ ½mv² dt + ∫ mc·r(v) dt, and −mc²T/ℏ = −ω_C T, while −μ Δx₄ = −(mc/ℏ)·icT = −iω_C T. (iv) follows from (ii) and X₄ = ic·τ. (v) v = c gives v_{x₄} = 0 in (i). ∎

**The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic reading.** Proposition E.2 places the accumulated x₄-advance in the dx₄/dt = ic Geometric Channel. Part (iii) separates the Feynman weight into the two quantities that §A.3.4 of the reproduced appendix separates for the twins. The coordinate accumulation Δx₄ = icT is shared by all paths and gives the common factor exp(−iω_C T), which cancels in every interference pattern. The deficit X₄[path] − Δx₄ = i ∫ (v_{x₄} − c) dt differs from path to path and gives exp(iS/ℏ) with S = ∫ ½mv² dt, up to the bounded remainder. This is the weight used in Lemma L.4.1. The remainder of Lemma L.4.1 is the same expansion of the one square root, written in the momentum where part (iii) writes it in the velocity: the energy c(ℏ²κ² + m²c²)^(1/2) differs from mc² + ℏ²κ²/(2m) by at most ℏ⁴κ⁴/(8m³c²), which over a time step ε gives the phase bound εℏ³κ⁴/(8m³c²) stated there. Part (iv) states that the relative phase of two paths is the Compton angular frequency multiplied by the difference of their proper times: the interference of matter is the differential ageing of §A.3.4 and §A.5.1, counted in Compton periods. Part (v) is the statement “the photon is at rest in x₄” of §A.3.1, and it is why, in the Sphere-chain expansion of Lemma L.2.1, the n = 0 term G₀ on the cone carries no factor of μ and every factor −μ² sits at an intermediate event where one Sphere expansion ends and the next begins. It is also the fact used in §V.8: two photons from a common emission event have ΔX₄ = 0 at all later times.

The four quantities and the results of the present paper that use them:

| Quantity of the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic | Definition | Result of the present paper that uses it |
|---|---|---|
| Coordinate rate, the Principle | dx₄/dt = ic at every event | Proposition H.1 (x₄ = ict and the metric); Proposition H.2 (homogeneity); Lemma H.3.1 (p̂₄, Ĥ = −ic p̂₄); Lemma V.7.3.A (evolution as translation along x₄); Proposition E.1 |
| Share of the expansion, the time-dilation factor c·dτ/dt | v_{x₄} = √(c² − v²) | Proposition L.3 and Proposition E.2(ii) (L = −mc·v_{x₄}); Lemma L.4.1 (expansion in v/c); Lemma L.2.1 and §V.8 (v_{x₄} = 0 on the cone) |
| Proper-time component of the four-velocity | dx₄/dτ = icγ | Proposition E.1(iv) (eigenvalue of p̂₄); the relation ω²/c² − |k|² = μ² of §III.2 |
| Accumulated x₄-advance, proper time up to the factor ic | X₄[path] = ic·τ[path] | Proposition L.3 (weight exp(−μX₄)); Proposition L.4 (sum over paths); Proposition E.2 |

### E.3 Worked Examples for the Present Paper

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The two examples compute, from dx₄/dt = ic and the quantities of §E.2, the phase of a matter wave on its own worldline and the fringe phase of a two-path experiment.

**E.3.1 The matter wave on its own worldline.** Matter of mass m moves at constant velocity v. Its wave is exp(i(k·x − ωt)) with ℏk = γmv and ℏω = γmc² (§III.2). On the worldline x = vt the phase is (γmv²/ℏ − γmc²/ℏ)t = −γω_C(1 − v²/c²)t = −ω_C t/γ = −ω_C τ. Hence on the worldline exp(i(k·x − ωt)) = exp(−μX₄), by Proposition E.2(ii). The wave of §III.2, which the Hamiltonian route treats as an eigenfunction of p̂ and Ĥ, takes on the worldline the value that the Lagrangian route assigns to that worldline as a path. Its wavelength in space is 2π/|k| = h/(γmv), the de Broglie wavelength [109].

**E.3.2 Two paths between the same two events.** Matter of mass m leaves a source at t_i and reaches a detector at t_f = t_i + T by one of two straight paths of lengths ℓ₁ and ℓ₂, each at constant speed v_j = ℓ_j/T < c. By Proposition E.2(iv) the relative phase is

*Δφ = −ω_C T ((1 − ℓ₁²/(c²T²))^(1/2) − (1 − ℓ₂²/(c²T²))^(1/2)).*

This is ω_C multiplied by the difference of proper times of §A.5.1 of the reproduced appendix, with both twins moving. By Proposition E.2(iii),

*Δφ = m(ℓ₁² − ℓ₂²)/(2ℏT) + R, |R| ≤ m(ℓ₁⁴ + ℓ₂⁴)/(2ℏc²T³).*

With p = m(ℓ₁ + ℓ₂)/(2T), the mean momentum of the two paths, the leading term is p(ℓ₁ − ℓ₂)/ℏ = 2π(ℓ₁ − ℓ₂)/λ with λ = h/p. The fringe spacing of the two-path experiment is therefore fixed by the difference of the accumulated x₄-advance of the two paths, and the common coordinate accumulation Δx₄ = icT does not enter.

## Appendix F. The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic and the Hilbert Space: Theorem 6.1.5 and Lemma 6.1.6 of [167], Reproduced Verbatim and in Full, with Their Correspondence to Theorem H.0

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension x₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: dx₄/dt = ic, with x₄ = ict, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action h and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

**McGucken Principle anchor.** dx₄/dt = ic expands a McGucken Sphere from every event, and the wavefronts of those Spheres add. Theorem H.0 of §II.1A builds the Hilbert space from those wavefronts within the present paper and quotes the statement of the corpus theorem. The corpus theorem, its proof, and the lemma on which the proof depends are reproduced here verbatim and in full from [167, §6.4.5]. Section, theorem and lemma numbers inside the reproduced text are those of [167].

**Theorem 6.1.5 (Hilbert space from McGucken-Sphere wavefront overlap, dx₄/dt = ic Geometric Channel).** *The Hilbert space 𝓗 of nonrelativistic single-particle quantum mechanics is the Cauchy completion of the complex vector space of Huygens–Fresnel–Kirchhoff superpositions of McGucken-Sphere wavefronts, over source-event distributions of rapid decay, on the spatial slice* ℝ³ *at parameter time t, equipped with the geometric overlap inner product*

*⟨ φ, ψ ⟩_t := ∫_{ℝ³} φ^∗(x, t) ψ(x, t) d³x,*

*defined as the integral over the spatial slice of the pointwise product of two wavefronts’ complex values at each spatial-slice point — the geometry of “the simultaneous presence of two McGucken-Sphere wavefronts at the same spatial point” integrated over the slice. The inner product’s three pre-Hilbert axioms — sesquilinearity, conjugate symmetry, positive-definiteness — are inherited from McGucken structures already established in the dx₄/dt = ic framework: rank-2 sesquilinearity from Lemma 7.1 (rank-2 metric pairing), conjugate symmetry from Theorem 7.11 (PT-symmetry through the source event), positive-definiteness from Theorem 7.4 (Born-density non-negativity). The Cauchy completion in the norm topology gives*

𝓗 ≅ L²(ℝ³, d³x),

*the same Hilbert space supplied by the Algebraic Channel’s Stone–von Neumann derivation. The convergence of Geometric and Algebraic Channels on the same* 𝓗 *is the consistency of the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework: the algebraic representation theory (Algebraic Channel) and the wavefront overlap geometry (dx₄/dt = ic Geometric Channel) describe the same Hilbert space from two derivational directions, both descending from dx₄/dt = ic.*

**Proof (Geometric Channel: wavefront overlap geometry).**

By Principle 2.1 and clause 3 of §2.4, every event E ∈ Minkowski Space 𝕄_{1,3} emits a McGucken-expansion wavefront — a spherically symmetric wavefront expanding at velocity c from E, with the McGucken Sphere 𝓜_E(t) at parameter time t being the spatial-slice locus of points reached by the wavefront. By Definition 2.6, the wavefront’s complex value at a spatial-slice point P at parameter time t is the path-integral kernel

ψ_E(P, t) = ∑_{γ : E → (P, t)} exp(i S[γ]/ℏ),

with the action S[γ] = ∫ L dt along the path γ. The wavefront ψ_E(·, t) : ℝ³ → ℂ is a complex-valued function on the spatial slice, supplied by McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework Theorem 3.1 (the ℂ-valued character of McGucken amplitudes from x₄-perpendicularity) and the Frobenius selection of §6.4 Step 3 (one perpendicular axis → one imaginary unit → ℂ uniquely selected by Frobenius 1877 [52 in the source paper’s numbering]). These McGucken-Sphere wavefronts are the **primitive vectors** of the Hilbert space construction — physical wavefronts arising from definite source events, from which every vector of the Hilbert space is built by superposition.

Linear superposition of McGucken-Sphere wavefronts descends from the **universality clause** of the McGucken Principle: by Theorem 2.4 clause (iv), every spacetime event p ∈ 𝕄_{1,3} has its own McGucken-expansion wavefront Σ^+(p) at +ic, with the wavefront assignment Σ_M : p ↦ Σ^+(p) translation-invariant by the universality of dx₄/dt = ic. Multiple source events at distinct spacetime locations therefore each contribute their own Sphere wavefront *independently* of the others — the existence of one Sphere wavefront from E₁ does not modify the Sphere wavefront from E₂, because each is generated by the same universal principle acting locally at its own source event. By the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework Huygens–Fresnel–Kirchhoff principle (Remark 7.16), for a collection {E_i} of source events and complex weights c_i ∈ ℂ, the total wavefront at parameter time t is the linear sum

*ψ(P, t) = Σ_i c_i · ψ_{E_i}(P, t), P ∈ ℝ³.*

Linearity is therefore the wavefront-level expression of dx₄/dt = ic’s universality, not a postulated linear-superposition axiom: the source-event-additivity of the McGucken-expansion is forced by the universality clause that every event independently emits its own Sphere at +ic. The wave equation (□ − m² c²/ℏ²)ψ = 0 of §9.1.5 is the self-consistency condition that the superposition reproduces a coherent wavefront satisfying the same equation. The complex coefficients c_i encode the relative amplitudes and phases of the source-event contributions, with their ℂ-valued character supplied by the Frobenius selection of Step 3 of §6.4.

The same universality gives the superposition over a continuous distribution of source events on the spatial slice. For a complex weight function g on ℝ³, with ψ_{x_E}(·, t) the McGucken-Sphere wavefront at parameter time t > 0 from a source event at spatial position x_E, the Huygens–Fresnel–Kirchhoff superposition is

*ψ_g(x, t) := ∫_{ℝ³} g(x_E) ψ_{x_E}(x, t) d³ x_E.*

The weight functions are taken in the Schwartz class 𝒮(ℝ³) of smooth functions of rapid decay, for the following reason. In the nonrelativistic regime the wavefront of a single source event is the kernel of the free evolution of Theorem 9.0.5,

*ψ_{x_E}(x, t) = (m/(2π i ℏ t))^{3/2} exp((i m |x − x_E|²)/(2ℏ t)), |ψ_{x_E}(x, t)| = (m/(2π ℏ t))^{3/2} for every x ∈ ℝ³,*

the same modulus at every point of the slice. The integral of |ψ_{x_E}|² over the slice therefore diverges, and the overlap integral defined below does not converge on single-event wavefronts or on their finite sums. A single-event wavefront is a tempered distribution, an element of 𝒮'(ℝ³), in the way the position eigenstate |x⟩ is; the vectors of the construction are the superpositions ψ_g with g of rapid decay, for which every integral below converges (Lemma 6.1.6 (i)). Finite superpositions approximate every ψ_g in the sense of Lemma 6.1.6 (iii).

The space of McGucken-Sphere superpositions under this linear structure is

𝓥 := { ψ_g(·, t) : g ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³) } = { ψ : ℝ³ → ℂ | ψ is a Huygens–Fresnel–Kirchhoff superposition of McGucken-Sphere wavefronts at parameter time t over a source-event distribution of rapid decay }.

𝓥 is a complex vector space under pointwise addition and ℂ-scalar multiplication. The complex structure is supplied by Frobenius selection (Step 3 of §6.4): with exactly one perpendicular axis x₄ to ordinary 3-space, Frobenius 1877 forces ℂ as the unique division algebra over which the wavefront amplitudes are defined.

Define the inner product on 𝓥 as the **geometric overlap** of two wavefronts at the spatial slice:

*⟨ φ, ψ ⟩_t := ∫_{ℝ³} φ^∗(x, t) ψ(x, t) d³x.*

The integrand φ∗(x, t) ψ(x, t) is the pointwise product of two wavefronts’ complex values at the spatial-slice point x — the geometry of *simultaneous presence of two McGucken-Sphere wavefronts at the same spatial point*, with the conjugate factor φ∗ corresponding to the orientation-reversed wavefront (Definition 7.3) and ψ corresponding to the forward wavefront. The integration over ℝ³ sums these pointwise geometric overlaps across all spatial-slice points where either wavefront has support.

The inner product is therefore **constructed from the geometry of the McGucken-Sphere wavefront**: at each spatial-slice point x, the simultaneous presence of two wavefronts is captured by the pointwise product φ∗(x, t) ψ(x, t); the integration over the spatial slice gives the total geometric overlap. By Lemma 7.1 (bilinearity from the rank-2 Minkowski metric), the integrand is the rank-2 sesquilinear pairing of the metric tensor contracted with (φ, ψ) at each spatial point — a natural metric pairing supplied by the rank-2 character of η_μν (Lemma 2.5) descending from (ict)² = −c²t².

**Step 4: Inner-product axioms inherited from established McGucken structures.**

*Sesquilinearity from Lemma 7.1.* For α, β ∈ ℂ and φ, ψ₁, ψ₂ ∈ 𝓥, direct computation gives

⟨φ, αψ₁ + βψ₂⟩_t = ∫ φ∗(αψ₁ + βψ₂) d³x = α ∫ φ∗ψ₁ d³x + β ∫ φ∗ψ₂ d³x = α ⟨φ, ψ₁⟩_t + β ⟨φ, ψ₂⟩_t

— linearity in the second argument; conjugate-linearity in the first follows by direct conjugation under the integral sign. The sesquilinearity is the wavefront-level expression of **Lemma 7.1’s rank-2 metric pairing**: the rank-2 character of η_μν (Lemma 2.5) supplies a unique-up-to-scalar natural bilinear pairing on amplitudes, and the geometric overlap integral is that pairing integrated over the spatial slice. Sesquilinearity is therefore inherited from the rank-2 Minkowski metric.

*Conjugate symmetry from Theorem 7.11.* By direct computation,

⟨ψ, φ⟩_t = ∫ ψ∗φ d³x = (∫ φ∗ψ d³x)∗ = (⟨φ, ψ⟩_t)∗.

The conjugate symmetry is the wavefront-level expression of **Theorem 7.11’s PT-symmetry through the source event**: complex conjugation of the McGucken-Sphere wavefront ψ ↦ ψ∗ corresponds geometrically to the 4D PT-reflection through the source event E (Theorem 7.11 (ii): ψ(PT_E · event) = ψ∗(event) exactly). The inner product’s conjugate symmetry ⟨ψ, φ⟩ = ⟨φ, ψ⟩∗ is then the wavefront-level statement of this PT-symmetry: swapping (φ, ψ) ↦ (ψ, φ) in the geometric overlap integral is equivalent to PT-reflecting each wavefront through its source event, which is the complex conjugation operation under the integral. Conjugate symmetry is therefore inherited from PT-symmetry through the source event.

*Positive-definiteness from Theorem 7.4.* For any ψ ∈ 𝓥,

⟨ψ, ψ⟩_t = ∫ |ψ(x, t)|² d³x ≥ 0,

with equality iff ψ = 0 almost everywhere on ℝ³. The non-negativity is the wavefront-level expression of **Theorem 7.4’s Born-density geometric overlap**: |ψ(x, t)|² = ψ∗(x, t) ψ(x, t) is the geometric overlap of ψ with its own conjugate (orientation-reversed) wavefront at the spatial point x, which is non-negative by construction (geometric overlap of a wavefront with itself is always non-negative, and zero only if the wavefront vanishes at that point). The strict positive-definiteness (⟨ψ, ψ⟩_t = 0 ⟹ ψ = 0 a.e.) is the statement that a non-zero McGucken-Sphere wavefront has non-zero geometric overlap with itself at some spatial-slice point. Positive-definiteness is therefore inherited from the Born-density non-negativity of Theorem 7.4.

The three pre-Hilbert axioms — sesquilinearity, conjugate symmetry, positive-definiteness — are **all inherited from established McGucken structures** (Lemma 7.1, Theorem 7.11, Theorem 7.4 respectively), with no axiom postulated by hand. The inner product is the natural metric pairing on McGucken-Sphere wavefronts at the spatial slice, with all three axioms forced by the wavefront geometry.

The pre-Hilbert space (𝓥, ⟨·, ·⟩_t) is not yet complete: limits of Cauchy sequences in 𝓥 may correspond to wavefronts generated by square-integrable distributions of source events that are not of rapid decay, which are not themselves in 𝓥. The completion in the norm topology ‖ψ‖_t = √⟨ψ, ψ⟩_t adds these limits.

Physically, the completion corresponds to the limit of finer and finer Huygens–Fresnel–Kirchhoff superpositions of McGucken Spheres: the pre-Hilbert space 𝓥 contains the superpositions of McGucken-Sphere wavefronts over source-event distributions of rapid decay; the completion adds the superpositions over every square-integrable source distribution, continuous-spectrum wavepackets on the mass shell (§9.1.5 Step 5), nonrelativistic Schrödinger wavepackets (§9.2.5 Step 7), and any L² limit of vectors of 𝓥.

The density of 𝓥 in L²(ℝ³, d³x) is established by the following lemma, which shows that 𝓥 is the Schwartz class, exhibits the Hermite functions as vectors of 𝓥 with Gaussian source-event distributions and polynomial weighting, and states the sense in which finite McGucken-Sphere superpositions approximate every vector of 𝓥.

**Lemma 6.1.6 (The wavefront space is the Schwartz class; Hermite functions from Gaussian source-event distributions on McGucken Spheres; density in L²(ℝ³, d³x)).** *Let 𝓥 = {ψ_g(·, t) : g ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³)} denote the pre-Hilbert space of Huygens–Fresnel–Kirchhoff superpositions of McGucken-Sphere wavefronts at parameter time t > 0 over source-event distributions of rapid decay (Step 2 of §6.4.5), equipped with the geometric overlap inner product ⟨ φ, ψ ⟩_t = ∫ φ^∗ψ d³x (Step 3). Then:*

*(i) 𝓥 = 𝒮(ℝ³) as a set of functions on the spatial slice. The overlap integral converges on 𝓥, and the Cauchy completion of (𝓥, ⟨·,·⟩_t) in the norm topology ‖ψ‖_t = √(⟨ψ,ψ⟩_t) equals L²(ℝ³, d³x).*

*(ii) The Hermite functions h_n(x) = c_n H_n(x) exp(−x²/2), where H_n are the (physicists’) Hermite polynomials and c_n = (2^n n! √(π))^{−1/2}, taken in products over the three coordinates, form an explicit complete orthonormal basis of the Cauchy completion. Each basis function h is a vector of 𝓥: h = ψ_g(·, t) with source-event distribution g = U_{free}(−t) h, a polynomial multiplied by the Gaussian exp(−|x_E|²/(2(1 − iℏ t/m))).*

*(iii) For every g ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³) the finite McGucken-Sphere superpositions ψ_n = Σ_k g(x_{E_{n,k}}) δ_n³ ψ_{x_{E_{n,k}}} over the points of cubic grids of mesh δ_n → 0 lying in the ball of radius 1/δ_n converge to ψ_g uniformly on compact subsets of ℝ³ and in 𝒮'(ℝ³). Every vector of 𝓥, and in particular every Hermite basis function, is in this sense a limit of finite superpositions of McGucken-Sphere wavefronts.*

**Proof.**

*Step A (Superpositions over source-event distributions of rapid decay are the free evolution of the distribution; 𝓥 = 𝒮).* By Theorem 9.0.5 of §9.2.5 (itself a theorem of dx₄/dt = ic — the nonrelativistic Sphere wavefront’s envelope dynamics is shown there to be the free Schrödinger evolution via the McGucken-Sphere mass-shell constraint and Compton-frequency oscillation, see §9.2.5 Steps 1–7), the continuous Sphere superposition with source-event distribution g equals the free Schrödinger evolution of that distribution:

*ψ_g(x, t) = (U_{free}(t) g)(x),*

where U_{free}(t) = exp(−it H_{free}/ℏ) is the free-particle evolution operator, and ψ_{x_E}(x, t) is its integral kernel. In the Fourier variable k, U_{free}(t) is multiplication by exp(−iℏ t |k|²/(2m)). This function is smooth, has modulus one, and each of its derivatives is bounded by a polynomial in |k|, so multiplication by it maps 𝒮(ℝ³) into 𝒮(ℝ³). The Fourier transform is a bijection of 𝒮(ℝ³). Hence U_{free}(t) maps 𝒮(ℝ³) into itself, and since its inverse U_{free}(−t) does the same, U_{free}(t) 𝒮(ℝ³) = 𝒮(ℝ³). Therefore

*𝓥 = {U_{free}(t) g : g ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³)} = 𝒮(ℝ³).*

For φ, ψ ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³) the product φ^∗ψ is of rapid decay, so the overlap integral converges. By Plancherel’s theorem and the unit modulus of the multiplier, ⟨ U_{free}(t) f, U_{free}(t) g⟩_t = ∫ f^∗ g d³x for f, g ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³) (the conservation that Theorem 9.2 derives from x₄-flux conservation).

*Step B (Finite superpositions; clause (iii)).* Fix g ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³). For each n ∈ ℕ, let {x_{E_{n,k}}}_k be the points of the cubic grid of mesh size δ_n → 0, and set c_{n,k} := g(x_{E_{n,k}}) δ_n³. The finite Sphere superpositions over the grid points in the ball of radius 1/δ_n are

*ψ_n(x, t) := Σ_{k} c_{n,k} ψ_{x_{E_{n,k}}}(x, t).*

By translation invariance of the McGucken-Sphere construction — the universality clause of dx₄/dt = ic formalized as Theorem 2.4 clause (iv) — the wavefront from a translated source is the spatial translate of the wavefront from the origin, ψ_{x_E}(x, t) = ψ₀(x − x_E, t). The integrand x_E ↦ g(x_E) ψ₀(x − x_E, t) is smooth; |ψ₀| is constant, and the gradient of ψ₀(x − x_E, t) in x_E has modulus (m/(2πℏ t))^{3/2} m|x − x_E|/(ℏ t). For x in a compact set K the integrand and its gradient are therefore bounded by C_K (1 + |x_E|)⁻⁵, with C_K depending on g, t and K only, by the rapid decay of g and of its gradient. The difference between the integral over a grid cube and the value at the grid point times δ_n³ is at most √3 δ_n⁴ times the supremum of the gradient on the cube; summing over the cubes, and adding the integral over the complement of the ball, gives sup_{x∈K} |ψ_n(x, t) − ψ_g(x, t)| ≤ C′_K δ_n → 0. For convergence in 𝒮'(ℝ³), pair with a test function χ ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³): ∫ ψ_n χ d³x = Σ_k c_{n,k} F(x_{E_{n,k}}) with F(x_E) = ∫ ψ₀(x − x_E, t) χ(x) d³x. Since ψ₀(·, t) is even, F = U_{free}(t)χ ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³) by Step A, so gF ∈ 𝒮(ℝ³) and its Riemann sums converge to ∫ g F d³x_E = ∫ ψ_g χ d³x. No ψ_n lies in L²(ℝ³), since each is a finite sum of functions of constant modulus; the convergence is uniform on compact sets and distributional, and the limit ψ_g is the vector of 𝓥.

*Step C (Hermite functions as vectors of 𝓥; density).* The three-dimensional Hermite functions {h_{n₁}(x₁) h_{n₂}(x₂) h_{n₃}(x₃) : (n₁, n₂, n₃) ∈ ℕ³} form a complete orthonormal basis of L²(ℝ³, d³x) (Reed–Simon I, *Methods of Modern Mathematical Physics: Functional Analysis*, Theorem V.7, applied componentwise on the tensor product L²(ℝ) ⊗ L²(ℝ) ⊗ L²(ℝ) ≅ L²(ℝ³)). Each is a polynomial multiplied by exp(−|x|²/2), hence lies in 𝒮(ℝ³) = 𝓥 by Step A: it is the superposition ψ_g(·, t) with g = U_{free}(−t) h. To exhibit g: for s ∈ ℝ, U_{free}(s) exp(−|x|²/2) = (1 + iℏ s/m)^{−3/2} exp(−|x|²/(2(1 + iℏ s/m))), as one checks by substitution in the free Schrödinger equation, and x_j U_{free}(s) = U_{free}(s) (x_j + (s/m)p̂_j) with p̂_j = −iℏ ∂/∂ x_j, so U_{free}(s) maps a polynomial multiplied by exp(−|x|²/2) to a polynomial of the same degree multiplied by exp(−|x|²/(2(1 + iℏ s/m))). With s = −t this is the source-event distribution of clause (ii): a Gaussian of complex width 1 − iℏ t/m with polynomial weighting. The Hermite functions are thereby generated by the creation operator a† = (x − d/dx)/√2 in each coordinate, h_{n+1} = a† h_n/√(n+1), acting within 𝓥, since multiplication by x and differentiation both preserve 𝒮(ℝ³).

Since 𝓥 contains a complete orthonormal basis of L²(ℝ³, d³x), it is dense in L²(ℝ³, d³x), and the completion of a dense subspace of the complete space L²(ℝ³, d³x) in its norm is L²(ℝ³, d³ x). This establishes clause (i). Clause (ii) is established by Step C, and clause (iii) by Step B. ∎

The completion is therefore exhausted by limits of countable Cauchy sequences of vectors of 𝓥, giving

𝓗_t := completion of (𝓥, ⟨·, ·⟩_t) in ‖·‖_t = L²(ℝ³, d³x),

the standard one-particle nonrelativistic Hilbert space at parameter time t.

The construction is performed at each parameter time t ∈ ℝ. The Hilbert space ℋ_t at parameter time t is unitarily equivalent to ℋ_{t'} at any other parameter time t' via the McGucken-derived unitary evolution of Theorem 9.2. The unitarity descends explicitly from dx₄/dt = ic through the following chain:

*(i) Constant-rate x₄-advance.* The McGucken Principle (Principle 2.1) states that x₄ advances at constant rate c at every spacetime event — a homogeneous, conservative motion in the x₄-direction with no source or sink. This is the physical statement that the Sphere wavefront’s x₄-momentum is invariant along the wavefront’s worldline.

*(ii) x₄-flux conservation on the spatial slice.* The constant-rate x₄-advance implies an associated continuity equation for the McGucken-Sphere wavefront: at every spacetime point, the spatial divergence of the wavefront’s probability current is balanced by the temporal change in the probability density, ∂_t|ψ|² + ∇ · j = 0, where j = (ℏ/2mi)(ψ^∗ ∇ ψ − ψ ∇ ψ^∗) is the McGucken-derived probability current (cf. §9.2.5 for the derivation of the current from Sphere-wavefront kinematics). Integrating this continuity equation over the spatial slice ℝ³, with the wavefunction’s normalizable decay at spatial infinity making the boundary term vanish, gives (d/dt) ∫_{ℝ³} |ψ|² d³x = 0 — the L²-norm ‖ψ‖²_{L²(ℝ³)} is conserved under McGucken-expansion evolution (Theorem 9.2 establishes this conservation law from x₄-flux conservation explicitly).

*(iii) Unitary evolution from norm conservation.* The McGucken-expansion evolution preserves the geometric-overlap norm on ℋ_t ≅ L²(ℝ³, d³ x) by (ii). By Stone’s theorem (1930), an L²-norm-preserving one-parameter evolution on a separable Hilbert space is, equivalently, a strongly-continuous one-parameter unitary group; the McGucken-derived Schrödinger generator iℏ ∂_t ψ = Ĥ ψ supplied by §9.2.5 from the McGucken-Sphere wavefront’s nonrelativistic envelope dynamics is the self-adjoint generator of this unitary group.

*(iv) Parameter-time-independent identification of Hilbert space ℋ.* The unitary map U(t', t) : ℋ_t → ℋ_{t'} generated by Ĥ establishes the unitary equivalence of ℋ_t and ℋ_{t'} for any pair of parameter times t, t' ∈ ℝ. The Hilbert space is therefore well-defined as

*ℋ ≅ L²(ℝ³, d³ x),*

independent of the parameter time at which the construction is performed. The parameter-time independence is itself a dx₄/dt = ic consequence: the constant-rate x₄-advance (i) forces x₄-flux conservation (ii), which forces L²-norm conservation, which forces unitary evolution (iii), which forces parameter-time-independent identification of the Hilbert space (iv). The dx₄/dt = ic Geometric Channel derivation establishes Theorem 6.1.5 in the form stated above. ∎

**The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic reading.** Theorem H.0 of the present paper and Theorem 6.1.5 above build the same space L²(ℝ³, d³x) from the same physical objects, the wavefronts of McGucken Spheres, with the same inner product, the overlap of two wavefronts on a spatial slice. The correspondence is item by item. The space 𝓥 of superpositions ψ_g over source-event distributions g of rapid decay is the space V_t of wavefronts W(t)f of Theorem H.0, with the profile f in the Schwartz space 𝒮 in the place of g and the exact propagation W(t) = e^(−itΩ) of Lemma L.2.2 in the place of the free non-relativistic evolution. Lemma 6.1.6 (i), 𝓥 = 𝒮(ℝ³), is Theorem H.0(i), V_t = 𝒮. The conservation of the overlap, obtained above from Plancherel’s theorem and from the continuity equation, is Theorem H.0(iii). The identification of the spaces at different times, items (i) to (iv) at the end of the proof above, is Theorem H.0(vii). The completion ℋ ≅ L²(ℝ³, d³x) is Theorem H.0(vi). The reason both constructions take source-event distributions of rapid decay is the one stated in the proof above: the wavefront of a single source event has the same modulus at every point of the slice, so its overlap integral does not converge, and it enters the Hilbert space through superposition. Theorem H.0(iv) and (v) add two statements that Theorem 6.1.5 takes from Lemma 7.1 and Theorem 7.4 of [167]: the weight of the overlap is constant, and |ψ|² is the only function of the modulus whose total the expansion conserves.

## References

**Primary sources (prior art)**

[1] V. I. Arnold, *Mathematical Methods of Classical Mechanics*. Graduate Texts in Mathematics 60, Springer-Verlag, New York (1978). Second edition 1989. The standard reference for the symplectic-geometric formulation of classical Hamiltonian mechanics, with the dual-channel content at the classical level (T∗Q for Hamiltonian, TQ for Lagrangian) that the McGucken Quantum Formalism extends to the quantum level.

[2] A. Ashtekar, “New variables for classical and quantum gravity,” *Phys. Rev. Lett.* **57**, 2244–2247 (1986). The canonical reformulation of general relativity in terms of connection variables, which serves as starting point for loop quantum gravity.

[3] S. L. Adler, *Quantum Theory as an Emergent Phenomenon*. Cambridge University Press (2004). The trace-dynamics program deriving quantum mechanics as statistical thermodynamic average of classical matrix dynamics.

[4] D. Bohm, “A suggested interpretation of the quantum theory in terms of ‘hidden’ variables. I, II,” *Phys. Rev.* **85**, 166–179, 180–193 (1952). The original hidden-variable formulation with guiding equation.

[5] P. A. M. Dirac, *The Principles of Quantum Mechanics*. Oxford University Press (1930; 4th edition 1958). The textbook establishing the canonical commutation relation as a postulate of quantum mechanics.

[6] P. A. M. Dirac, “The Lagrangian in Quantum Mechanics,” *Physikalische Zeitschrift der Sowjetunion* **3**, 64–72 (1933). The observation that seeded Feynman’s path integral.

[7] R. P. Feynman, “Space-Time Approach to Non-Relativistic Quantum Mechanics,” *Rev. Mod. Phys.* **20**, 367–387 (1948). The path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics.

[8] B. C. Hall, *Quantum Theory for Mathematicians*. Graduate Texts in Mathematics 267, Springer (2013). Contains modern proofs of Stone’s theorem and the Stone-von Neumann theorem used in Propositions H.2 and H.5.

[9] D. Hestenes, *Space-Time Algebra*. Gordon and Breach, New York (1966). The foundational monograph of the geometric-algebra program.

[10] D. Hestenes, “Real spinor fields,” *J. Math. Phys.* **8**, 798–808 (1967).

[11] D. Hestenes, “The zitterbewegung interpretation of quantum mechanics,” *Found. Phys.* **20**, 1213–1232 (1990).

[12] G. ‘t Hooft, *The Cellular Automaton Interpretation of Quantum Mechanics*. Fundamental Theories of Physics 185, Springer (2016; arXiv:1405.1548, 2014).

[13] B. Kostant, “Quantization and Unitary Representations,” *Lecture Notes in Mathematics* **170**, 87–208 (1970). The foundational paper of geometric quantization.

[14] J. Lindgren and J. Liukkonen, “Quantum Mechanics Can Be Understood Through Stochastic Optimization on Spacetimes,” *Scientific Reports* **9**, 19984 (2019). The derivation of the Schrödinger equation from Lorentz-invariant stochastic optimal control.

[15] E. Nelson, “Derivation of the Schrödinger Equation from Newtonian Mechanics,” *Phys. Rev.* **150**, 1079–1085 (1966). The stochastic-mechanics derivation of quantum mechanics.

[16] E. Nelson, *Quantum Fluctuations*. Princeton University Press (1985). Extended treatment of stochastic mechanics.

[17] C. Rovelli, *Quantum Gravity*. Cambridge University Press (2004). The canonical reference for loop quantum gravity.

[18] F. P. Schuller et al., “Constructive Gravity: Foundations and Some Applications,” arXiv:2003.09726 (2020). The constructive-gravity programme deriving Einstein-Hilbert from matter principal polynomials.

[19] J. M. Souriau, *Structure des systèmes dynamiques*. Dunod, Paris (1970). The companion foundational paper for geometric quantization.

[20] M. H. Stone, “On one-parameter unitary groups in Hilbert space,” *Ann. Math.* **33**, 643–648 (1932). The foundational theorem used in Proposition H.2.

[21] J. von Neumann, “Die Eindeutigkeit der Schrödingerschen Operatoren,” *Math. Ann.* **104**, 570–578 (1931). The uniqueness theorem for the Schrödinger representation used in Proposition H.5.

[22] S. Weinberg, *The Quantum Theory of Fields*, Volumes I–III. Cambridge University Press (1995–2000).

[23] E. Witten, “Perturbative Gauge Theory as a String Theory in Twistor Space,” *Commun. Math. Phys.* **252**, 189–258 (2004); arXiv:hep-th/0312171 (2003).

[24] P. Woit, “Euclidean Twistor Unification,” arXiv:2104.05099 (2021). The Euclidean twistor unification programme.

[25] P. Woit, “Notes on Spinors, Twistors, and Unification,” arXiv:2206.00323 (2022).
